Accurate scene geometry retrieval and motion estimation
using deep learning techniques for holographic video
compression.

R ÉSUMÉ EN F RANÇAIS
Introduction
L’holographie [1], introduite par Dennis Gabor en 1948, est une technologie d’imagerie
cohérente en deux étapes permettant d’enregistrer et de reproduire l’onde lumineuse diffusée par un objet éclairé au moyen des interférences et diffraction de la lumière cohérente [2]. Bien qu’initialement limitée aux modèles fixes monochromatiques capturés
dans les laboratoires d’optique, son évolution moderne vers l’holographie numérique a
récemment permis le traitement d’un contenu totalement général, tel que des séquences
d’hologrammes animés en couleur.
Elle se distingue des autres technologies émergentes par sa capacité à reproduire une
expérience 3D réaliste, authentique et confortable pour l’utilisateur sans nécessiter le port
d’un casque spécial, ce qui pourrait entraîner, après une longue utilisation, une fatigue
visuelle et des maux de tête [3].
L’onde lumineuse reconstruit peut etre visuelle à l’œil nu à partir d’un ensemble continu de points de vue et de profondeurs comme si la scène était physiquement présente
devant l’utulisateur. Cependant, étant donné qu’un hologramme crée l’illusion de la profondeur en diffractant le faisceau de lumière incident, sa zone de visualisation dépend de
la taille de ses pixels qui idéalement devraient être comparables à la longueur d’onde de la
lumière visible, soit quelques centaines de nanomètres. Pour une taille d’hologramme donnée, plus l’angle de visualisation souhaité augmente, plus le pas de pixel requis diminue,
ce qui entraîne la nécessité de stocker un plus grand nombre de pixels.
Cette contrainte de stockage constitue un frein important pour la mise en pratique de
l’holographie, notamment pour des applications de streaming en temps réel de contenu
vidéo. En raison des différences significatives entre un signal holographique et une image
naturelle [4], les codecs vidéo existants ne peuvent pas être utilisés efficacement pour
compresser les hologrammes avant la transmission. Cela nécessite donc l’introduction de
nouveaux codecs qui prennent en compte les particularités du signal holographique afin
de produire un débit de compression raisonnable pouvant être transmis par les canaux de
communication filaires ou sans fil actuelle.
3
Un codec vidéo standard [5] est généralement composé d’un encodeur et d’un décodeur.
L’encodeur est responsable de la compression de la vidéo côté serveur et est principalement composé de trois grandes parties. Tout d’abord, il y a la partie de l’estimation
du mouvement entre les différentes trames de la vidéo. Ensuite, il y a une partie de
compensation de mouvement estimée pour créer des trames compensées qui sont ensuite
soustraites des trames réelles afin de ne compresser que le résidu. Enfin, il y a une partie
qui compresse efficacement le résidu et les vecteurs de mouvement ainsi que la première
trame, puis les transmet au décodeur qui se trouve côté client. Dans cette thèse, nous
allons étudier la conception de la première partie de l’encodeur, qui est responsable de
l’estimation du mouvement, et nous allons discuter de la façon de l’adapter à des séquences
vidéo holographiques.
Une question que peut se poser le lecteur est pourquoi ne pas compresser directement la
géométrie de la scène puis générer l’hologramme côté client. En effet, l’état de l’art actuel
contient une variété de méthodes pour la compression efficace de la géométrie 3D sous
différents types de représentations [6]. La réponse à cette question réside en deux points.
Tout d’abord, la géométrie de la scène n’est pas connue à l’avance, comme cela peut être le
cas lors d’une acquisition optique de l’hologramme. Ensuite, la génération d’hologramme
côté client demande une puissance de calcul importante qui n’est pas toujours disponible
côté client. Ainsi, il est préférable de compresser les hologrammes avant la transmission
et la visualisation par l’utilisateur.
Objectifs
Les algorithmes d’estimation des vecteurs de mouvement [7] entre deux trames consécutives, également appelés algorithmes de flux optique, sont principalement composés
de trois étapes. Tout d’abord, l’extraction des caractéristiques de texture locales est effectuée, soit en utilisant des opérateurs mathématiques, soit en utilisant des réseaux à
convolution. Ensuite, les caractéristiques extraites sont corrélées, soit en définissant une
fenetre de correlation (ou de recherche) et un opérateur de corrélation, soit, dans les méthodes les plus modernes, à l’aide d’un mécanisme d’attention [8] permettant une corrélation
globale. Enfin, les valeurs de corrélation extraites sont utilisées pour prédire les vecteurs
de mouvement, soit en une seule étape, soit itérativement en minimisant progressivement
l’erreur entre la trame avec la compensation de mouvement et la trame réelle.
Les principaux inconvénients de la mise en pratique de ces algorithmes sur des vidéos
holographiques sont les suivants :
4
— Tout d’abord, on peut constater le manque de localisation spatiale des objets de la
scène sur le plan de la scène, qui est dû au fait que chaque pixel de l’hologramme
contribue à l’acquisition de la scène. Cela entraîne non seulement un manque de
blocs de pixels similaires intra-trame, mais également inter-trame. Ce point est
particulièrement accentué par la rapide variation des motifs d’interférence entre
deux trames consécutives, même pour de très petits mouvements de la scène.
— Ensuite, l’estimation des vecteurs sur le plan de l’hologramme ne permet d’extraire
que l’information de mouvement sur les axes X et Y, sans prendre en compte le
mouvement de la scène sur l’axe Z. Cela pose problème car le mouvement de la
scène entre deux trames se produit le long des trois axes, et compenser uniquement
une partie de ce mouvement entraînerait un résidu important et donc une mauvaise
qualité de compression.
Afin de pallier à ces limitations, nous proposons dans cette thèse une nouvelle procédure d’estimation du mouvement basée sur deux étapes. La première étape consiste en
l’estimation de la géométrie de la scène à partir de trames holographiques sous un format RGB-D. Cette représentation permettra non seulement de connaitre la localisation
spatiale des objets de la scène mais aussi leur profondeur. Ensuite, dans la deuxième
étape, les représentations RGB-D extraites seront utilisées pour estimer précisément le
mouvement de la scène dans la vidéo holographique.
Une attention particulière sera portée à l’étude de l’impact de la qualité de la géométrie
estimée sur la précision des vecteurs de mouvement, ainsi qu’aux solutions qui peuvent
être apportées pour assurer la robustesse de l’approche proposée.
État de l’art des méthodes d’estimation de profondeur
à partir de données holographiques
La tache estimation de la profondeur d’une scène à partir d’un hologramme est particulièrement bien documentée dans le cadre de la microscopie holographique [9, 10].
Celle-ci est le plus souvent appliquée pour l’estimation de la structure de cellules semitransparents [11], le suivi de particules [12] en milieux complexes, ou encore la surveillance
cellule vivante [13].
De manière générale, l’estimation de la géométrie de la scène n’est pas effectuée directement, mais elle est réalisée dans une deuxième étape après avoir estimé la distance du
plan de focalisation optimale qui correspond au plan de reconstruction, où le niveau de fo5
calisation est le plus élevé. Ensuite, la géométrie de la scène est extraite de l’argument ou
bien de la phase de la reconstruction numérique à la distance estimée. En effet, la microscopie holographique est principalement dédiée aux cellules transparentes qui vont créent
un délai de phase [14] de la lumière de référence lors de l’acquisition de l’hologramme,
qui est contenu dans la phase estimée à partir de la reconstruction numerique. Ce délai
de phase va etre corrélée non seulement à la structure de la cellule mais également aux
indices de réfraction de chaque région qui la compose. Il est possible d’utiliser des méthodes [15] dites de "decoupling" pour separe ces deux termes de la phase obtenu, mais
le plus souvent, la phase extraite est directement visualisée et utilisée pour analyser la
structure de la scène.
Les méthodes pour estimer le niveau de focalisation optimale peuvent être regroupées
en quatre groupes [16]. Les trois premiers groupes contiennent des méthodes sans apprentissage et sont basés sur des opérateurs mathématiques manuellement définis. L’ensemble
de ces méthodes ont pour but de créer un opérateur permettant d’évaluer le niveau
de focalisation le plus précisément possible pour une reconstruction numérique donnée.
Ensuite, cet opérateur est utilisé pour sélectionner parmi une série de reconstructions
numériques celle où le niveau de focalisation est optimal. Les principales distinctions de
ces trois groupes peuvent être énumérées comme suit :
— Opérateurs de parcimonie : dans le premier groupe, on trouve les méthodes
qui évaluent le niveau de parcimonie de la reconstruction numérique [17]. Plus
celui-ci sera élevé, moins le niveau de focalisation sera important.
— Opérateurs basés sur les gradients : dans le deuxième groupe, on trouve
les méthodes qui évaluent les contours dans la reconstruction numérique [18]. Plus
ceux-ci seront importants, plus le focus sera élevé.
— Opérateurs basés sur des transformées : dans le troisième groupe, on trouve
les méthodes qui utilisent des transformées comme celle en ondelettes [19] pour
évaluer non seulement la concentration de l’énergie mais également le niveau de
contours de la reconstruction numerique.
Dans le quatrième groupe, on retrouve des méthodes plus modernes basées sur l’apprentissage
profond. Celles-ci [20] peuvent être regroupées en deux sous-ensembles. Le premier contient des méthodes qui fonctionnent de la même manière que les opérateurs des trois
groupes précédents, mais qui sont basées sur des architectures neuronales. Le deuxième contient des méthodes qui prennent directement en entrée l’hologramme et prédisent
l’amplitude ou la phase au plan de focalisation optimale.
6
Limitations et verrous technologiques
L’ensemble des méthodes décrites plus haut sont conçue pour estimer une seule profondeur par hologramme, puis à retrouver la géométrie de la scène à partir de la phase de
reconstruction numerique calculée à la profondeur obtenue. Cependant, cette approche
ne peut être effectuée dans le cadre de cette thèse, car la phase est aléatoirement générée
lors de l’acquisition de l’hologramme et ne contient pas de retard de phase qui pourrait
être utilisé pour estimer la géométrie de la scène.
Il est donc nécessaire de proposer de nouvelles méthodes qui peuvent directement estimer la géométrie de la scène à partir de l’hologramme. Les méthodes proposées doivent
être assez précises pour pouvoir utiliser les résultats produits dans des applications nécessitant une haute précision, telles que l’estimation du flux optique de la scène.
Contributions
Les contributions de cette thèse sont articulées en trois grandes parties.
Méthodes basées sur la reconstruction numérique
La première partie de la thèse vise à élaborer des méthodes efficaces pour estimer la
scène à partir des reconstructions numériques obtenues à partir de l’hologramme. Toutes
les méthodes proposées reposent sur l’idée que plus la distance de reconstruction se rapproche de la géométrie de la scène, plus le niveau de netteté est élevé. Ainsi, localiser
précisément la position de la scène revient à déterminer à quelle distance de reconstruction
le niveau de netteté de la reconstruction numérique est optimal.
L’ensemble de ces méthodes sont construit en trois étapes. Tout d’abord, une série
de reconstructions numériques est calculée à différentes distances préalablement échantillonnées. Ensuite, le niveau de focalisation est estimé à l’aide d’un opérateur de mise
au point. Enfin, le plan de focalisation optimal est sélectionné en fonction d’un critère
préalablement défini. Elle se distingue néanmoins par la formulation de l’opérateur de
calcul du niveau de focalisation, ainsi que par la manière dont il est appliqué. On peut
énumérer leurs principales distinctions de la manière suivante :
— L’approche Depth-from-focus modifié: dans cette contribution, nous avons
utilisé des opérateurs de focalisation définis mathématiquement à l’aide d’opérations
simples sur l’image telles que le gradient et le laplacien. Ensuite, les opérateurs
7
choisis sont appliqués localement autour de chaque pixel du domaine spatial à
l’aide d’un patch centré autour du pixel. Enfin, le plan de focalisation optimal
est choisi de manière automatique afin de prendre en compte si la texture locale
est fortement ou uniformément texturée. La géométrie initiale obtenue est ensuite
affinée à l’aide d’une méthode proposée basée sur le consensus local.
— Une mesure de mise au point apprise : Dans cette seconde contribution,
nous avons remplacé les mesures de focalisation traditionnelles par une mesure
apprise. Pour ce faire, nous avons choisi un réseau de convolution sous une forme
en U et l’avons entraîné à l’aide d’un jeu de données en libre accès. Une fois le
réseau pré-entraîné, il peut être utilisé pour évaluer le niveau de focalisation locale
sur chaque reconstruction numérique sans nécessiter un découpage préalable du
domaine spatial en patch.
— L’estimation du niveau de focalisation de manière horizontale : dans
cette troisième contribution, nous avons évalué quel était le gain de performance
et de temps de calcul lorsque l’estimation du niveau de focalisation était réalisée
horizontalement plutôt que verticalement. Pour ce faire, nous avons créé un jeu de
données composé de coupes horizontales issues des volumes de reconstruction 3D
calculés à partir des hologrammes d’entraînement. Ensuite, nous avons entraîné un
réseau de neurones avec une structure en U, en adaptant la fonction de perte pour
compenser la distribution déséquilibrée des points en focalisation sur les coupes
horizontales. Le réseau entraîné est ensuite utilisé de la même manière qu’un
opérateur de focalisation classique, et la distance où la focalisation est la plus
grande est sélectionnée comme la distance la plus probable de la position de la
scène.
Les expérimentations réalisées pour cette première partie ont montré que les opérateurs de
focalisation supervisée donnent de meilleurs résultats avec un temps de calcul réduit par
rapport à ceux définis manuellement. De plus, on peut noter que l’estimation horizontale
produit moins d’artefacts que l’estimation verticale, avec une vitesse d’inférence plus
rapide.
Nous avons finalement proposé une dernière contribution pour étudier la possibilité de
combiner plusieurs opérateurs non supervisés afin d’approcher les performances des opérateurs supervisés. Cette dernière contribution comprend deux étapes. Dans la première,
une série d’estimations dites ’cormpus’ sont extraites à l’aide de plusieurs opérateurs de
focalisation et de différentes tailles de patch. Ensuite, dans la deuxième étape, un réseau
8
de neurones est auto-supervisé pour combiner toutes les solutions et trouver celle qui est
la plus proche de la vérité de terrain.
Méthodes basées sur l’espace des phases
La deuxième partie de la thèse vise à élaborer des méthodes pour estimer la scène
à partir de la représentation dans l’espace des phases de l’hologramme. L’ensemble des
méthodes proposées est basé sur l’utilisation de la forme 4D du support de la représentation dans l’espace des phases de l’hologramme pour estimer la géométrie de la scène. Les
principales distinctions des méthodes proposées peuvent être énumérées comme suit:
— L’estimation itérative de la géométrie : Dans cette cinquième contribution,
nous avons proposé une méthode itérative pour estimer la géométrie de la scène à
partir de la représentation de l’hologramme dans l’espace des phases. La méthode
proposée est constituée de trois étapes. Tout d’abord, nous extrayons le support
de la représentation dans l’espace des phases à l’aide d’un algorithme simple de
binarisation. Ensuite, des points candidats ayant une forte probabilité d’appartenir
à la scène sont extraits à partir du support obtenu et de contraintes géométriques.
Enfin, l’ensemble des points candidats extraits est progressivement réduit pour
trouver la représentation minimale, ou une estimation de la géométrie de la scène
qui a la même forme dans l’espace de phase que le support calculé.
— L’estimation bout-en-bout de la géométrie : Dans cette sixième contribution,
nous avons proposé une méthode bout-en-bout qui améliore la méthode itérative
non seulement en termes de temps de calcul, mais également en termes de précision.
Cette méthode est composée de quatre étapes. Tout d’abord, le support de la
représentation dans l’espace des phases est extrait à l’aide d’un réseau de neurones.
Ensuite, dans la deuxième étape, un volume 3D associant à chaque point de l’espace
un score d’appartenance à la scène et une information de couleur est créé. Dans
la troisième étape, le volume 3D est découpé en coupes horizontales et chaque
coupe est donnée comme entrée à un réseau de neurones pour la prédiction de la
géométrie de la scène. Enfin, la géométrie prédite est affinée à l’aide d’un troisième
réseau de neurones pour des applications demandant une forte précision.
Les expérimentations menées dans cette deuxième partie ont montré que les résultats
obtenus par la méthode bout-en-bout étaient plus précis que les méthodes basées sur les
reconstructions numériques, avec un temps de calcul plus faible. Cependant, cette méthode est limitée aux hologrammes à très faible pas de pixel qui produisent une représen9
tation temps-fréquence très localisée, et elle peut ne pas adaptée à un pas de pixel très
élevé.
Méthodes pour l’estimation du flux optique
Dans la troisième partie de la thèse, nous avons proposé une méthode en deux étapes
pour l’estimation du mouvement de la scène au sein d’une vidéo holographique. Dans la
première étape, les méthodes d’estimation de la scène précédemment décrites sont utilisées
pour extraire une représentation RGB-D de la géométrie. Ensuite, dans la deuxième
étape, les séquences d’images RGB sont utilisées pour estimer les mouvements spatiaux
de la scène à l’aide des méthodes de flux optique proposées dans l’état de l’art actuel.
Les vecteurs de mouvement estimés sont ensuite utilisés pour compenser les cartes de
profondeur et les résultats sont soustraits des cartes de profondeur réelles pour estimer
le mouvement axial de la scène. Ainsi, le mouvement de la scène le long des trois axes
principaux est obtenu.
Les expérimentations menées dans cette dernière partie ont montré que la qualité de la
géométrie de la scène estimée avait un impact important sur la précision des vecteurs de
mouvement, et qu’elle devrait être raffinée préalablement afin d’améliorer considérablement l’estimation du mouvement de la scène au sein de la vidéoholographique."
Conclusion
Dans cette thèse, nous avons d’abord introduit différentes méthodes pour l’estimation
de la géométrie de la scène à partir d’un hologramme. Ensuite, nous avons discuté comment les méthodes proposées peuvent être utilisées pour estimer le 3D mouvement de la
scène à l’intérieur d’une séquence vidéo holographique.
Les expérimentations qui ont été conduites à travers cette thèse montrent que le problème inverse d’estimation de la géométrie de la scène à partir de l’hologramme était en
effet soluble, avec des performances qui dépendent de la formulation du problem utilisée
ainsi que des algorithmes mis en pratique pour sa résolution.
D’une manière générale, on peut constater que les algorithmes basés sur la reconstruction numérique sont plus précis que ceux basés sur la représentation en espace des phases
de l’hologramme. Cependant, ces méthodes nécessitent un temps de calcul et de stockage
assez important en raison de leur utilisation intensive de la reconstruction numérique.
10
De plus, les expérimentations ont montré que ce type d’approches, indépendamment de
l’architecture de réseaux de neurones utilisée ou de la façon dont la focalisation est estimée, ont des performances limitées en raison de leur incapacité à prédire correctement
les fortes variations de la géométrie de la scène.
Les régions où ces méthodes échouent à prédire correctement la géométrie de la scène
sont visibles par la présence d’un bruit speckle sur la carte de focus étendue associer a
la carte de profondeur estimée. L’intensité du bruit va dépendre de la magnitude de la
dégradation, et sa distribution va dépendre de la géométrie et de l’orientation de la scène.
Le plus souvent, on peut observer que le bruit est concentré sur les parties avec de fortes
variations de la geometrie, et il est moins présent sur des régions relativement plates ou
peu variables. En plus d’aider à diagnostiquer visuellement la qualité de l’estimation de
la géométrie de la scène, la carte de focus étendue, même si elle est fortement dégradée,
permet de connaître le contenu de la scène et la forme des différents objets qui la composent. Ainsi, on peut suggérer que la géométrie initiale de la scène pourrait être affinée
en utilisant des a priori de forme qui peuvent être extraits de la carte de focus étendue,
combinée à des caractéristiques locales extraites par un réseau de neurones.
Cette nouvelle géométrie affinée contiendrait moins d’artefacts et de discontinuités,
et produirait une carte de focus étendue sans bruit additif qui pourrait directement être
utilisée dans divers applications intermédiaires. Une des applications qui a été étudiée
lors de cette thèse est l’estimation du mouvement de la scène à partir des géométries
estimées. Nous avons pu constater que le manque d’une étape de raffinement cause un
manque important de précision des vecteurs de mouvement predits, dû à la dégradation
aléatoire de la géométrie de la scène sur des trames holographiques successives.
Il est donc important d’introduire une étape de raffinement afin d’atténuer les dégradations commises et d’assurer des performances stables sur les différentes trames consécutives, et ainsi améliorer considérablement l’estimation des vecteurs de mouvement.
Les méthodes basées sur l’espace des phases peuvent pallier à certaines limitations
des méthodes basées sur la reconstruction numérique, notamment le temps de calcul et
l’espace de stockage. Cependant, à l’heure actuelle, certaines limitations de ces méthodes
doivent être résolues afin d’améliorer considérablement leur robustesse et leur précision.
La principale modification à apporter est de remplacer l’extraction des points candidats
par l’extraction de points de correspondance entre les imagettes de la représentation en
espace des phases. Cela pourrait non seulement considérablement réduire le nombre
d’imagettes à traiter, mais également assurer de bonnes performances en présence d’une
11
densité élevée d’objets dans la scène.
En effet, les méthodes proposées sont basées sur un score qui évalue la probabilité
d’appartenance à la scène, en estimant l’aire d’intersection entre la forme produite par
un point dans l’espace des phases et celle de la scène. Cependant, dans le cas où la scène
s’étend tout le long du domaine spatial, ce score sera égal à un peu importe l’emplacement
du point candidat. Ainsi, tous les points de l’espace 3D seront considérés comme des points
candidats, ce qui rendra inefficaces les étapes suivantes des méthodes proposées qui sont
entraînées à reconnaître et à inverser des formes de diffraction bien spécifiques.
Une autre limitation est l’utilisation du dataset actuel, lequel contient un ensemble
d’objets limité, placés uniquement à certains endroits de l’espace, ce qui restreint considérablement la généralisation des méthodes actuelles. Malheureusement, l’augmentation de
ce jeu de données n’est pas possible en raison du manque de méthodes d’augmentation qui
agissent directement sur l’hologramme et donc sur la scène tridimensionnelle. Les seules
augmentations possibles à l’heure actuelle se font sur les reconstructions numériques et
sont limitées au retournement horizontal et vertical.

4.1
The figure illustrates the process of focus-level map extraction using the
pre-trained the learned Focus Measure (FM) operator called Uθ . The operator takes a sequence of computed numerical reconstructions as input.
Then it predicts a focus level map for each one that indicates the local
level of focus for each pixel in the spatial domain. For a given pixel (x, y),
the predicted values across the input numerical reconstructions sum to 1. . 102

4.6
A scatter plot that illustrates the obtained MAE error on the y-axis with

the local SVI metric around the point. The reader can observe that the
higher the local surface variation (given by the SVI), the lower the in-focus
areas (given by the MIA) that the network can use to estimate the focus
level, resulting in a larger estimation error (given by the MAE). . . . . . . 113

4.10 Illustration of the focus curve produced by the Patch-Net and the GLVN.
The reader can observe that the PatchNet model produces a Dirac function with a global maximum at the optimal focus plane, contrary to the
GLVN operator, which produces a noisy bell-shaped curve with a standard
deviation approximately equal to the depth extent of the local object surface.117

I NTRODUCTION
0.1
Thesis context
Holography [21] is a promising 3D visualization technique that offers a remarkably
immersive experience without requiring specialized viewing equipment. By capturing and
reproducing light waves reflected from a given scene, holography creates a realistic and
natural depth illusion. The viewer can observe the reconstructed scene with naked eyes
from various viewpoints and depths as if the scene is right in front of him.
In conventional optical holography [2], the scene acquisition necessitates a meticulously
stabilized optical setup within a controlled environment to eliminate any small vibrations
that could degrade the produced interference patterns. To address this recording challenge, contemporary holographic techniques employ computers to precisely estimate the
light scattered by real-world or synthetic scenes toward the hologram plane. The recorded
Computer-gnerated Hologram (CGH) is a rich source of information about the acquired
3D scene and can be used in various domains such as art, architecture, medicine, education, etc.
Holography distinguishes itself from other immersive media technologies, which typically rely on stereoscopic technique [22] that provides a 3D depth illusion by replicating binocular disparity through special headsets. Although this technique may be relatively simple to implement, it comes with several limitations including a limited viewing
zone [23], a lack of monocular motion parallax, and vergence accommodation conflict that
can lead to visual fatigue and headaches for viewers [3].
All these limitations are solved using hologram but a cost of several realization challenges [4], including generation, display, and compression. This thesis addresses the signal
processing issues related to the compression of video holographic sequences.
A natural question that can arise is why not encode directly the 3D representation of
the scene in the source domain, and transmit the encoded data using the efficient transmission systems already established in standard video compression frameworks. Then,
perform the hologram computation from the decoded data on the client side.
In fact, the high spatial redundancies present in the source domain could be lever35
Introduction
aged to efficiently compress the scene representation with minimal detail loss during the
transmission and the decoding process. However, real-time hologram computation and
displays require a rapid processing speed which is not universally available on all client
devices.
In addition, the 3D scene representation is not always readily available, especially
in cases where the hologram is acquired using optical means. In such scenarios, direct
encoding of the holographic patterns using a proper compression framework becomes
necessary.
0.2
Motivations and objectives
In recent years, there has been a growing interest in holography due to its capability
to deliver a realistic, faithful, and immersive 3D experience. This particular interest
is particularly visible in the recent literature, which features an increasing number of
published papers discussing the generation [24], display [25], and quality assessment [26]
of digital holograms. However, few research efforts have been dedicated to the design
of efficient compression and transmission frameworks [27–31]. The current compression
strategies rely on standard video codecs [32], that do not take into account the special
characteristics of the holographic data and usually result in poor compression quality,
especially for high-resolution holograms.
The primary characteristics of holographic video sequences that hinder efficient compression using natural image compression frameworks can be summarized as follows:
— Storage requirement: For a given hologram size, the achievable viewing angle
during hologram visualization is constrained by the recording pixel pitch, which
ideally should be comparable to the wavelength of visible light. As the desired
viewing angle increases, the required pixel pitch decreases, resulting in the need
to store a larger number of hologram pixels. This can lead to a significant storage
requirement for high-resolution holograms, which is further compounded by factors
such as the number of hologram channels and frames within the video sequence.
This culminates in an overwhelming volume of data that becomes increasingly
challenging to manage.
— Signal properties: The hologram is a complex-valued signal that exhibits a uniform frequency distribution spanning from low to high frequencies. Contrarly to a
natural image which is predominantly distributed in the low frequencies, facilitat36
Introduction
ing its efficient compression.
— Intra-frame difference: During hologram recording, each point in the scene
contributes to every pixel of the hologram. This leads to a loss of spatial localization
of scene objects within the hologram. Consequently, finding matching blocks with
similar texture variations inside the spatial domain becomes challenging.
— Inter-frame difference: Even slight movements of the recorded scene between
timestamps lead to significant variations in the resulting holographic patterns.
This, coupled with the lack of spatial localization, significantly affects traditional
motion estimation techniques, which rely on finding local similarities within a given
search interval.
These combined characteristics pose limitations on traditional compression frameworks in
two key aspects. First, the computed motion vectors are often of poor quality, leading to
high residual error post-motion compensation. Typically, holographic video compression
is conducted without temporal constraints between frames, with each frame compressed
independently. This usually leads to poor compression quality due to the fundamental
differences in signal properties between holograms and natural images. Second, scene
motion between frames involves 3D motion. However, classical motion estimation and
compensation algorithms are designed only for spatial motion and do not consider axial
scene motion. Additionally, the space transform and quantization tools implemented in
the current compression framework are designed for real-valued data and are not suitable
for complex-valued holograms.
To overcome these challenges a novel compression framework should be introduced
with robust motion estimation and compensation techniques that are specifically designed
for holographic data. This thesis focuses solely on the motion estimation aspect and
introduces novel motion estimation techniques that can accurately estimate scene motion
between two consecutive holographic frames.
The lack of spatial localization of scene objects within the hologram plane does not
allow for direct estimation of the scene motion from the holographic data. Therefore, in
this thesis, we first devoted a significant amount of work to estimating the geometry of
the scene organized into an RGB-D format from the holographic data. This extracted
representation provided well-localized scene objects in the spatial domain and is more
adequate for scene motion estimation. Then, at the end of the manuscript we have
discussed how to effectively use the obtained representation to efficiently estimate the
scene motion in a holographic video sequence.
37
Introduction
0.3
Contributions
The contributions of this thesis are organized into three parts. The first part of this
work aims at designing efficient hologram scene geometry retrieval based on hologram
numerical propagation. To this end, we designed and proposed four algorithms:
— A modified depth-from-focus approach: This approach utilizes local focus
changes across different computed hologram numerical reconstructions to precisely
retrieve the scene geometry. The algorithm consists of four steps. First, the input
hologram is numerically propagated at sampled candidate axial distances. Second,
the local focus change around each pixel in the spatial domain is evaluated using
a mathematically defined focus measure. Third, a coarse scene geometry estimate
is obtained by selecting, for each pixel, the distance at which the focus level is
optimal using a proposed auto-switch method. Finally, the coarse estimate is
enhanced using a proposed refinement method based on local depth consensus.
Experimental results have demonstrated that the proposed method can precisely
retrieve the scene geometry without requiring complex focus measures.
— A learned focus measures: This approach replaces traditional mathematically
defined focus measures with a U-shaped Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) architecture. The approach comprises two steps. First, the selected neural network
is trained using randomly sampled patches extracted from computed numerical
reconstructions of training hologram samples. Second, given a test hologram, the
pre-trained network evaluates the local focus level around each pixel in the spatial
domain in a single network inference pass. Then, the scene geometry is retrieved
by selecting, for each pixel, the axial distance where the focus level is optimal. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves better precision
and lower inference time compared to traditional focus measures.
— Horizontal focus level estimation: In this approach, we shifted the paradigm
by evaluating focus levels horizontally rather than vertically. To achieve this,
we constructed a dataset comprising horizontal slices extracted from consecutive
numerical reconstructions of training hologram samples. This dataset served as
the foundation for supervising a U-shaped neural network tasked with accurately
estimating in-focus points on each slice. The trained network was subsequently
employed to infer scene geometry on the test set. Our experimental findings showcase that this methodology not only has faster inference times but also consumes
38
Introduction
fewer GPU resources while minimizing artifacts in the retrieved scene geometry.
— Self-supervised focus measures fusing: This approach aims to achieve the
performance obtained using the previously proposed supervised neural network
through the combination of a weak set of focus measures. The algorithm comprises two main steps. Initially, a series of weak estimates are generated using the
modified depth-from-focus approach, utilizing a diverse range of focus measures
with differing patch sizes. Subsequently, these weak estimates are combined using
a neural network trained in a self-supervised manner. Experimental results illustrate that even though individual estimates of the used weak estimator may be
degraded, their combination can achieve performance similar to a self-supervised
model.
The second part of this work aims at designing efficient hologram scene geometry
retrieval based on hologram PSR. To this end, we designed and proposed two algorithms:
— Iterative phase space depth estimation: This approach utilized the acquired
shape of the hologram PSR to estimate the scene geometry. The algorithm comprises two steps. Initially, the hologram PSR is computed and binarized to obtain
its support. Subsequently, the scene geometry is iteratively searched by identifying candidate geometries that yield the same PSR support as the estimated one.
The conducted experiment has demonstrated that the scene geometry can be directly retrieved from the hologram PSR without necessitating extensive utilization
of hologram numerical reconstruction.
— End-to-end phase space depth estimation: This approach extends the previously proposed iterative method with a neural network-based approach to significantly enhance algorithm precision and reduce inference time. The algorithm
encompasses three steps. Initially, a neural network is employed to compute and
binarize the hologram PSR to obtain its support. Second, the acquired support
is utilized to extract a set of candidate points with a high likelihood of belonging
to the scene. Third, the initial candidate set is refined to yield a coarse estimate
of scene geometry using a second neural network. Subsequently, for high-precision
applications, this coarse estimate undergoes further refinement employing a third
neural network. Experimental results demonstrate the clear superiority of the proposed method over the state-of-the-art approach based on numerical reconstruction. Besides its precision, the proposed method exhibits lower inference time as
it solely relies on computing the hologram PSR.
39
Introduction
The third part of this work aims to evaluate the impact of the scene geometry retrieval
of the scene 3D motion estimation. To this end, we have proposed one algorithm:
— Two stages optical flow approach: This approach builds upon the previously
proposed scene geometry method to accurately estimate scene motion within a
holographic video sequence. The algorithm comprises three main steps. First, the
scene geometry, organized in an RGB-D format, is extracted for each frame of the
video sequence. Second, traditional optical flow algorithms are employed on the
RGB images to compute scene spatial motion. Third, the computed spatial motions
are utilized to compensate for depth maps, and axial motion is determined by the
difference between the actual and motion-compensated depth maps. Experimental
results demonstrate that the quality of the retrieved scene geometry significantly
influences the accuracy of the estimated motion vectors.
0.4
Thesis structure
The dissertation is composed of nine chapters organized as follows:
— Chapter 1 includes technical backgrounds that are essential for understanding
the thesis.
— Chapter 2 provides a comprehensive review of current state-of-the-art for hologram scene depth estimation .
— Chapter 3 delineates our inaugural contribution, which centers on the modified
depth-from-focus approach. Initially, the chapter formulates the addressed problem and presents a geometric interpretation of the method. Then explain the need
for the proposed auto-switch method. Finally, a series of experiments are conducted to validate the proposed methodology and discuss the impact of various
hyperparameters on the obtained results.
— Chapter 4 is dedicated to our second contribution, namely the learned CNN
architecture for focus estimation. Initially, the chapter examines the geometric
limitations of traditional focus measures. It then reformulates the focus estimation problem to facilitate the efficient training of the neural network architecture.
Subsequently, a series of experiments is conducted to assess the proposed method
and compare its performance to traditional focus measures. Moreover, an extensive analysis is undertaken to address the weaknesses inherent in the proposed
approach.
40
Introduction
— Chapter 5 elaborates on our third contribution, the horizontal focus estimation
approach. Initially, the chapter delves into the benefits of reformulating focus
estimation horizontally as opposed to vertically. It then discusses the geometric
interpretation of horizontal focus detection. Subsequently, a series of experiments
are conducted to compare the performance differences between vertical and horizontal focus estimation. Additionally, the limitations of the proposed method are
carefully analyzed.
— Chapter 6 outlines our fourth contribution, the self-supervised focus measure
fusing approach. Initially, the chapter explains the intuition behind the proposed
method and delineates the relationship between the network learning path and the
anticipated refined solution. Subsequently, the proposed method is evaluated on a
set of test holograms, and the obtained results are discussed.
— Chapter 7 elaborates on our fifth contribution, dedicated to an iterative phase
space depth estimation approach. The chapter begins by introducing the theoretical background of the hologram PSR and its relation to the targeted scene
geometry. Subsequently, it meticulously describes the various steps of the proposed method. Finally, the proposed methods are evaluated on test holograms
with varying types of scene geometry, and the resultant outcomes are analyzed.
— Chapter 8 introduces our sixth contribution, dedicated to an end-to-end phase
space depth estimation approach. The chapter initiates by discussing the limitations of the iterative approach. It then delves into how these limitations can be
addressed and introduces the various steps of the proposed method. Finally, a
series of experiments is conducted to validate the proposed approach and compare
it with the numerical reconstruction-based methods.
— Chapter 9 introduces our final contribution, which is a two-stage optical flow
estimation method. The chapter initially outlines the proposed method. Subsequently, it evaluates this method through a series of experiments, aiming not only
to assess the quality of the retrieved scene geometry but also to examine how this
quality influences the precision of the estimated motion vectors. An extensive analysis is conducted to discuss the obtained results, highlighting the advantages and
weaknesses of the proposed approach.
— Chapter 10 provides a brief summary of the contributions made in the thesis. It
then delves into discussing the advantages and limitations of the proposed methods.
Finally, it addresses major open issues that remain unsolved for future works.

Chapter 1
BACKGROUND ON
C OMPUTER - GENERATED H OLOGRAM
This chapter provides the necessary theoretical background for understanding the
thesis. First, a brief overview of the problem addressed in the present thesis is given in
Section 1.1. Then, the scene data format and the hologram acquisition are described in
Section 1.2. Section 1.3 provides a theoretical background on scalar diffraction theory
for hologram backpropagation and its relation to focus change. Section 1.4 discusses the
computation of hologram 3D numerical reconstruction volume and how it can be used
in practical applications. Section 1.5 gives a brief background on hologram PSR and its
visual interpretation. Finally, Section 1.6 introduces some evaluation metrics to assess
the quality of the retrieved scene geometry.
1.1
Problem Formulation
Suppose we aim to efficiently compress a holographic video sequence {Ht }recorded
from a moving scene denoted as St . Additionally, we will assume that an exact holographic
motion compensation algorithm is provided. However, the scene motion between two
consecutive timestamps is unknown and needs to be estimated through the recorded
holographic data.
Throughout this thesis, we will progressively discuss how this task can be achieved in
two steps: first, the scene geometry retrieval, and then the 3D scene motion estimation.
The first step is particularly challenging due to the lack of spatial localization of the scene
objects within the hologram plane. To address this, we will discuss in this chapter two
alternative hologram representations namely: the hologram 3D reconstruction volume
and phase-space representation, that provide the necessary spatial localization necessary
for precise scene geometry retrieval.
Before that, we will discuss hologram acquisition to better understand the main lim43

1.2
Computer Generated Holograms
In the early stages of holography, optical methods were employed for hologram acquisition, utilizing a straightforward setup depicted in Figure 1.1(a). A laser source emitted
coherent light, split by a beam splitter into two waves. The first, known as the object wave
(O), illuminated the target object, underwent modulation, and then reflected to the photosensitive plate. Simultaneously, the second wave, referred to as the reference wave (R),
directly illuminated the plate, creating interference fringes with the object wave. These
interference patterns were subsequently recorded through the chemical development of
the photosensitive medium.
The resulting interference pattern, called a hologram, can be mathematically defined
as follows:
H = (O + R)(O + R)∗
= |O|2 + |R|2 + OR∗ + RO∗
(1.1)
where O and R represent the object wave and the reference wave, respectively.
The recorded hologram can be reconstructed by illuminating it with the reference wave
R used during the acquisition step, as shown in Figure 1.1(b). This results in a transmitted

where the +1 diffraction order is proportional to the object wave O, the 0 diffraction
order is proportional to the reference wave R, and the −1 diffraction order is proportional
to the complex conjugate of the object wave O∗ .
The 0 and -1 diffraction orders are unwanted artifacts and are typically removed
either directly from the recorded hologram [33] or by modifying the optical setup used for
recording [2].
In the present manuscript, we will mainly work with CGH that directly encodes the
whole object light wavefield O in the recorded hologram. In the current literature [28, 34],
numerous methodologies exist for CGH computation. However, since our main objective
is to extract scene geometry from recorded holograms, we will only discuss the most
relevant methods for our work: the point-based and the layer-based method illustrated in
Figure 1.2. Interested readers can refer to the cited articles for a more detailed review.
Before diving into the hologram computation methods, we will briefly discuss the scene
data format and the acquisition setup.
1.2.1
Scene Data Format
In the present manuscript, we will consider that the targeted scene is a 2D parametric
function S, that associates for each spatial position (x, y) ∈ X × Y four distinct values
45
Partie , Chapter 1 – Background on Computer-generated Hologram
which are: the point color in RGB format, the axial distance (or depth) w.r.t to the
hologram, and a binary value which 1 if the point belongs to scene and 0 otherwise. In
the case of a discretized spatial domain X × Y, this representation can be simplified into
a well-established data format called RGB-D format which consists of two images: an
RGB image and a depth image that provides the color information and the axial position
of each scene point respectively.
The scene parametric function S can be formulated using RGB-D format as follows:
S(x, y) =

(x, y), and A to the amplitude or intensity value of one of the color channels of RGB(x, y).
The value A we will refer by default to the intensity of the red channel, if not specified
otherwise.
1.2.2
Acquisition Setup
Given a scene in an RGB-D format of size L × L × 2 and a hologram plane of size
L × L, the acquisition step consists of decomposing the scene into geometric primitives,
and then numerically simulating the light diffraction patterns produced at the hologram
plane. Here for the sake of simplicity, we will assume that the scene and the hologram
points are defined using the same spatial domain discretization as illustrated in Figure 1.3.
This spatial discretization can be mathematically formulated as :

the reconstruction plane. For an intuitive mathematical formulation in the subsequent
sections, the points located on the 2D hologram plane (x, y, 0) are denoted as (ξ, η, 0),
where ξ and η are used to replace x and y, respectively.

1.2.3
Point-based Approach
The point-based approach [35] illustrated in Figure 1.2(a) is the simplest way to record
a hologram. The approach assumes that the scene points are individual light sources that
emit spherical light waves toward the hologram. Then, the scene hologram is computed
by summing up all the spherical waves scattered by each scene point at the hologram
plane, as follows:
H(ξ, η) =
X Ai zejϕ(r)
x,y
iλr2
q
−2π
, with ϕ(r) :=
r and r := (ξ − x)2 + (η − y)2 + z 2 . (1.6)
λ
where z and Ai are the axial positions and the color intensity of the point at position
(x, y), and λ is the recording wavelength. The hologram generated from a single point
source is called the Point Spread Function (PSF) [36] and has circular diffraction patterns.
The shape and size of the circular pattern in the PSF can be influenced by various factors,
including the hologram pixel pitches, the wavelength of light, and any aberrations present
in the optical system. In ideal conditions, the PSF may appear as a symmetric circular
spot, but in practical situations, aberrations and imperfections can lead to deviations
from this ideal shape.
1.2.4
Layer-based Method
The point-based approach is a relatively slow approach that will essentially be used in
the manuscript to simplify notation for the phase space methods. The method that will
47
Partie , Chapter 1 – Background on Computer-generated Hologram
be extensively used is the layer-based approach [37] illustrated in Figure 1.2(b), which in
essence discretizes the axial distance into a set of bins. Then, the light wave emitted from
the scene points located at each bin is summed to get the scene hologram.
More formally, the axial axis is first sliced into N distinct axial distances which are
located between a minimal zmin and maximal zmax axial distance. The obtained discrete
axial values called zi are numbered from 0 to N − 1, from the nearest to the farthest
to the hologram plane. The 3D plane (x, y, zi ) that cuts the scene at a distance zi from
the hologram plane is referred to as a Layer. Second, the complex wavefront of the light
scattered by the scene point is recursively computed using the following formula:
uzi (x, y) = ozi (x, y) + md (x, y)Pdz {uzi−1 }(x, y),
(1.7)
where md is an occlusion mask, and Pz is the numerical propagation of light between
two layers separated by a distance z, dz = |zi − zi−1 | is the distance between the layer zi
and the layer zi−1 , and ozi is the complex wave of the scene point at distance zi of the
hologram given as,
(1.8)
ozi (x, y) = Azi (x, y)exp [jϕzi (x, y)] .
Finally, the light scattered at the last layer is numerically propagated to the hologram
plane which is located at a distance zmin , as follows:
H(x, y) = Pzmin {uz0 }(x, y).
(1.9)
The obtained hologram H(x, y) is a complex-valued matrix of size L × L. In the case of
a colored scene, a hologram is recorded for each color channel.
1.3
Hologram Numerical Reconstruction
The recorded hologram can be numerically reconstructed (or rendered) at any reconstruction plane (x, y, z), located at a distance z from the hologram plane. Different
methods can be used for this purpose; however, due to limited space, we will only introduce one of them that has been used throughout this manuscript, namely the Angular
Spectrum Method (ASM). Interested readers can refer to [38, 39] for a comprehensive
study of other numerical propagation methods.
48
1.3. Hologram Numerical Reconstruction
1.3.1
Angular Spectrum Method
The recorded hologram H can be viewed using a dedicated device [40], or it can be
numerically reconstructed at any distance z ∈ R+ from the hologram plane. The numerical
reconstruction of a hologram is a process that enables the rendering of the hologram in
order to view the scene content. The first rigorous formula for numerical reconstruction
is known as the Rayleigh-Sommerfeld diffraction [41] formula, which is given as:
O(x, y) = PzRS {H}(x, y) =
Z Z
H(ξ, η)g(x − ξ, y − η, z)dξdη,
(1.10)
where PzRS is the Rayleigh-Sommerfeld propagation of the hologram at distance z from
the hologram plane, and g is the convolution kernel, defined as
z ejkr

The formula in Eq. 1.10 can be simplified using the Fourier convolution theorem as follows:
O(x, y) = PzASM {H}(x, y) = F −1 {F{H(ξ, η)}F{g(ξ, η)}}.
(1.13)
where F and F −1 is the Fourier and Inverse Fourier transform respectively. This formulation is commonly known as the ASM which gives exact reconstruction results for both
near and far fields without any approximation.
1.3.2
Focus Curve
A numerical reconstruction can be computed at any distance z from the hologram
plane. However, only the distance close to the ground truth scene position gives relevant
information. In fact, only the point of the reconstruction plane (x, y, z) that intersects
the scene will be sharp (or in focus), other points will be out of focus.
To illustrate this concept, a straightforward scene composed of two planar objects
located at different depths was recorded and four numerical reconstructions depicted in
Figure 1.4 were computed. The reader can observe from the figure, that when the optimal
49
Partie , Chapter 1 – Background on Computer-generated Hologram
focus distance is used, i.e. the distance where the object is truly located, the resulting
numerical reconstruction is sharp. On the contrary, when the used reconstruction distance
is far from the correct object distance, the obtained numerical reconstruction is out of
focus.
In practical scenarios, The level of focus is not visually assessed but is evaluated using
a mathematically defined operator. Then, the focus curve, i.e., the curve that provides
the change in focus level with respect to the used reconstruction distance, is analyzed.
The obtained focus curve usually deviates from a Dirac function which is characterized
by a singular peak precisely at the ground truth object position. Rather, the evolution of
focus for an object is gradual, following a bell-shaped curve. The mean of this curve aligns
with the optimal focus plane, while the standard deviation is modulated by the speed of
the focus change. For a given scene, a hologram recorded with larger pixel pitches (∆1 , ∆2
in Eq.1.4 and Eq.1.5 ) exhibits a slower focus transition compared to a hologram captured
with a smaller pixel pitch as illustrated in Figure 1.5.
1.4
Numerical Reconstruction Volume
A single numerical reconstruction can be used to visualize the scene content at a given
axial distance and is sufficient to analyze a planar object. However, when the analyzed
scene has a complex geometry that is defined at different axial distances, then multiple
numerical reconstructions have to be performed and analyzed sequentially. A volume
that contains a series of consecutive numerical reconstructions that are stacked together
is commonly referred to as a numerical reconstruction volume. The readers can interpret
the volume as a 3D volumetric rendering of the recorded scene with a level of detail that
is directly influenced by the density of numerical reconstructions.
This section discusses how the energy of a single and multiple object spreads inside
the reconstruction volume. This notion is relatively important to design an efficient scene
geometry retrieval algorithm. Here, to have a better visualization of the energy-spreading
phenomena we will use a particle, i.e. a 3D point, to represent a given object. This
simplification is allowed if the surface of the object is continuous.

1.4.2
Energy Accumulation Point
For a single particle, the energy is always maximum around the ground truth particle
position. However, as the number of particles increases as illustrated in Figure 1.9, the
energy produced by the different particles will sum up, which results in False Positive (FP)
energy points with similar or higher energy than the ground truth particle positions
illustrated in Figure 1.9(a). Therefore, a more elaborate technique than the maximum
intensity level has to be designed to accurately find the positions of the particles.
For most of the work discussed in the next section, the number of particles is usually
controlled to ensure that the energy spread from the different particles does not have a
large intersection area. The energy spreading of the particle can be assessed directly from
the hologram, by ensuring that the circular pattern of each particle PSF is separated from
the others as illustrated in Figure 1.8.

(a).
1.5
Phase Space Representation
The Phase Space Representation(PSR) of a hologram [42] is a local frequency spectrum
representation that enables the semantic interpretation of the hologram contents. The
hologram PSR can be computed using diffrent methods including the Wigner-Ville [43]
(WV) distribution, Short-term Fourier transform (STFT) [43], and S-method [44]. In the
present manuscript, unless specified, the hologram PSR will always be computed using
a windowed STFT using Hann windows of size M × M with a redundancy factor of M2 .
54

1.6
Evaluation Metrics
The methods proposed in the thesis will produce scene geometry in an RGB-D format,
which has to be carefully evaluated to select the best-performing method. Two different
approaches can be used to evaluate the quality of retrieved scene geometry from a hologram H using a proposed algorithm A. The first straightforward approach is to measure
55
Partie , Chapter 1 – Background on Computer-generated Hologram
the MAE between predicted dˆ and ground truth d depth values as follows:
M AE =
N
1 X
di − dˆi .
N i=1
(1.17)
where N is the number of scene points. This metric is sensitive to the relative change
in the prediction quality and is easily interpretable.
The second approach is to assess the quality of the predicted RGB image either visually
or using metrics such as the Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR) or Mean Structural
Similarity Index (MSSIM). The RGB image is computed from the predicted depth map
by assigning to each pixel the intensity of the numerical reconstruction computed at the
estimated depth. The quality of the retrieved geometry can be visually assessed from the
obtained image, where the larger the estimation error, the higher the speckle noise [45]
density. However, its quantitative evaluation using traditional metrics such as the PSNR
and MSSIM is not adequate due to the low sensitivity of those metrics to small changes
in the quality of the predicted depth map, as illustrated in Figure 1.11.
In the remaining of the manuscript, the MAE between the ground truth and the
predicted depth map will be used to rank the different proposed methods and the predicted
RGB image will only be used for visual assessment.
1.7
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have discussed how a hologram can be recorded optically and
numerically. Then, we discussed how it can be reconstructed at a given distance z from
the hologram plane and the relationship between the obtained focus level and the used
reconstruction distance. The computation of hologram PSR and its interpretation was
also discussed. Finally, we have discussed the evaluation metrics that will be used to
evaluate the quality of the retrieved geometry. The important part to remind is that the
level of focus on each numerical reconstruction has a direct link with the targeted scene
geometry. The closer the reconstruction distance to the scene, the better the level of
focus. This relationship will be used extensively in the next chapters for the design of
efficient scene geometry retrieval algorithms.

Chapter 2
R ELATED WORKS
The following chapter will discuss the related works proposed in the recent State of
the Art (SOTA) for 3D scene geometry estimation from optically acquired and computergenerated holograms. Figure 2.1 provides an overview of the decomposition of the discussed SOTA methods, which are mainly decomposed into two subgroups. The first group
discussed in Section 2.1 contains the methods for single or multiple particle position detection. The second group discussed in 2.2 contains the methods designed for textured
objects.

Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
2.1
Particle Positions Detection
A particle is a 3D moving point that emits a spherical wave toward the hologram during its motion. In current literature, the term Particle tracking is commonly employed
to encompass methods involving the detection and tracking of moving particles. These
algorithms are applied to a sequence of holograms recorded at different timestamps during
particles motion. Here, as our main objective is scene geometry retrieval, we only discuss the particle detection algorithms. Interested readers can refer to [46] for a detailed
discussion of the tracking algorithms.
The particle position detection is close subject to scene position detection, not only
in terms of the involved methodology but also in terms of the used neural network architectures design.
The current SOTA methods can be classified into two sub-groups based on the particle
density. The methods in the first sub-group are tailored for low particle density, where
there is sufficient space between particles to distinguish their respective PSF circular
patterns in the hologram plane. On the other hand, the methods in the second group
are designed for high particle density, where each particle PSF exhibits large overlapped
areas with other particles PSF.
2.1.1
Low Particle Density
The low particle density case is relatively simple to handle, as each particle PSF can
be segmented and then processed independently without affecting other particles. The
particle segmentation is not mandatory but is recommended if there is some overlap
between different particles PSF.
Figure 2.2 summarizes the three different approaches that can be used to solve lowdensity particle position detection. The first and the second types of approaches take as
input the hologram numerical reconstruction which has been computed as described in
Section 1.4, while the third type of approaches directly processes the input hologram for
particle position detection. The discussed methods produce a list of estimated particles
with their respective spatial and axial positions, as well as their sizes.
Filtration Methods
The first branch comprises all the methods that are based on the filtration of the input
hologram reconstruction volume. Here, as we are working with low-density particles, we
60
2.1. Particle Positions Detection

Figure 2.2 – Schematic diagram for the low particle density methods.
can assume that the probability of having False Positive (FP) energy points inside the
reconstruction volume is relatively low and that the high energy points are distributed
around the ground truth particle position. Therefore, to extract the particle positions, the
obtained reconstruction volume can be simply filtered [47, 48] by employing a manually
defined threshold value to isolate high-energy points. Then, these selected points can be
grouped, and their respective centroids can serve as estimations for the particle positions.
Another interesting approach to filter the high energy points is called deconvolution [49, 50], which re-centers the energy spread by each particle around it, rather than
keeping the energy spread in the double cone shape(see section 1.4.1). The deconvolution
methods require a single reference particle reconstruction volume that acts as a reference
pattern. Then, the de-convolution operation which can designed be using a 3D Fourier
Transform (FT) is performed between the reference reconstruction and the input hologram reconstruction volume, and the centers of masses of the remaining points are used
as an estimate for each particle position.
The precision of these algorithms is directly influenced by the selected reconstruction
interval and the number of sampled axial distances.
Segmentation and Classification
The second branch comprises the methods that first segment individual particle PSF
and then classify the segmented PSF to predict the particle attributes. To extract those
61
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
(a) Hologram
(b)
Reconstruction
Volume
(c) EFI
(d) Thresholded EFI
Figure 2.3 – Illustration of RoI extraction from an EFI extracted by summing all the
numerical reconstructions computed from the hologram in (a). Then by thresholding all
the pixels that have an intensity greater than 2.
PSF, a set of RoI [51] has to be defined to delimit the spatial boundary of each particle
PSF in the hologram plane. This segmentation step is usually performed using an EFI
that is generated by summing up all the numerical reconstructions computed at different
axial distances [52, 53]. Then, the obtained image is thresholded to obtain points of
interest, which are grouped into RoIs by evaluating their local connectivity. The center of
mass of each set of connected pixels is used as the center of a circular RoI that represents
a candidate particle as illustrated in Figure 2.3.
The readers can view a single segmented particle PSF as the particle footprint on the
hologram plane. Therefore detecting the particle position is similar to finding a candidate
point which results in a similar PSF or footprint at the hologram plane. If we have access
to a dictionary of the candidates, we can use a correlation operator[54] to find which
candidate in the dictionary approximates the most the segmented particle PSF. If no
dictionary is provided, a classification algorithm has to be used to predict the optimal
particle attributes that when used produce the same PSF as the segmented one.
The classification algorithm can be implemented either using an iterative optimization
or using a pre-training CNN model. For example, Soulez et al. [55, 56] retrieve the particle
position using an iterative optimization problem. At each iteration of the algorithm, a
single particle PSF produced at the hologram plane is segmented. Then, the particle
attributes (size, spatial, and axial position) are optimized to minimize the difference
between the produced PSF and the segmented PSF. Finally, the computed particle is
stored and its corresponding PSF is subtracted from the input hologram, which is then
used as input for the next iteration. The optimization process stops when the next most
likely particle found by the algorithm has aberrant parameters.
62
2.1. Particle Positions Detection
Yevick et al. [57] used a bank of PSF profiles recorded using different sizes, refractive
index, and axial positions. Then, learn how to infer those characteristics from unseen PSF
profiles, by training an Support Vector Machine (SVM) model. Although the proposed
method gives a significant gain in computational time compared to traditional methods,
its main drawback relies on the use of discretized target values to train the SVM. To
alleviate this drawback, [58] proposes to use an Multilayer Perceptron (MLP) network
that takes the PSF profiles holograms and regresses to targeted particle characteristics.
Recently, Lee et al. [59] proposed a three-step methodology for deep particle tracking
in Digital Holographic Microscopy (DHM). First, the particle holograms are transformed
into binary images using a pre-trained SegNet [60] model. Second, the PSF produced
by each particle at the hologram plane is extracted using a circular Hough transform.
Finally, individual hologram areas are extracted using the detected spherical pattern, and
a CNN model is used to predict the particle axial position.
More elaborate approaches try to unify the segmentation and the detection into a single
stage. For example, Shimobaba et al. [61] proposed to use of U-Net architecture [62]
to predict the size, axial, and lateral position of each particle from a given hologram.
The experimental results showed that the network can accurately predict the particle
attributes, however, as the particle number increases the network performance decreases
significantly. Zhang et al. [63] proposed a one-stage network (OSNet) that detects and
classifies the particle PSF in a single pass. The network is based on a similar architecture
to the YOLO [64] architecture for natural image object detection. The network takes as
input the particle hologram and extracts local feature descriptors using a CNN network.
Then, the obtained feature map is used to regress the particle position in the hologram
plane and their axial position.
Phase Space Methods
The methods of the third branch are similar to the second, where the main change
relies on the fact that the particle PSF segmentation is done inside the hologram Phase
Space Representation (PSR). In the literature, different methods have been proposed that
are conceptually the same but using different hologram PSR computation formulas.
For example, Onural et al. [65–68] proposed the Wigner transform and demonstrated
that each particle results in a cross pattern in the hologram PSR. The slope of each
impulse line that composes the cross gives the depth information of the particle, and
the intersection points along the spatial axis give the spatial location of the particle.
63
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
Subsequently, Coetmellec et al. [69] showed that a rotated version of this cross can be
obtained using a 1D Fractional Fourier Transform (FRFT). Then the same authors [70]
extended their approach to a 2D FRFT with two fractional orders to retrieve the in-focus
particles intensity map [71].
Zhang et al. [72] proposed employing Gabor phase space analysis and demonstrated
that each particle leads to a cross appearing in the corresponding hologram PSR. The
author used the angle formed by the two lines of the cross, called the Gabor angle in the
paper, to accurately estimate the particle position.
More recently, Oh et al. [73] proposed to compute the energy flow between the spectrums of Windowed Fourier Transform (WFT) at different spatial positions for fast focus
estimation. The proposed method can be interpreted in a purely geometrical way. The
reader can view each WFT (fx , fy ) at a specific spatial position (x, y), as the perspective
projection of the scene on a tiny image often called lenslet. Therefore computing the
energy flow between multiple spatial positions is similar to computing the optical flow
between the lenslet images. The obtained optical flow is then used to parameterize 3D
rays, which are intersected to create a 3D density map. Finally, the object axial position
is computed as a weighted average between the estimated axial distance and the weights
provided by the computed 3D density map.
2.1.2
High Particle Density
So far we have discussed the low particle density hologram, where sufficient space is
present between the particles to ensure that their respective energy does not overlap and
thus preventing FP from appearing in the computed reconstruction volume. However,
as the number of particles increases, the energy produced by the different particles will
sum up, which results in FP energy points with similar or higher energy than the energy
observed at the ground truth particle positions. This will thus create uncertainty during
particle position estimation.
To properly handle high particle density, the modern approaches first compute a numerical reconstruction volume from the input hologram. Then a learned segmentation
model is used to segment the in-focus particles on each computed numerical reconstruction [74, 75]. The learned model can be guided by additional information such as the
input hologram, or a minimum intensity (or phase) map computed by assigning for each
pixel of the reconstruction plane the obtained minimum intensity (or maximum phase)
reached by the pixel across the numerical reconstruction.
64
2.2. Automatic Shape Focus Plane Estimation
More recently, Chen et al. [76, 77] proposed to simultaneously learn numerical propagation and particle detection in a single network called MB-HoloNet. The proposed
network takes as input a particle hologram, performs 3D hologram reconstruction, and
outputs the 3D particle volume. The proposed method showed superior performance
compared to the previously cited method. However, it suffers a large GPU consumption,
which increases with the hologram size and the number of reconstructions.
2.2
Automatic Shape Focus Plane Estimation
Now that we have discussed how we can recover single and multiple particle positions,
we will discuss how we can retrieve complex scene geometry. Most of the discussed
methods come from the DHM literature [11, 78] and are designed for monitoring pure
phase specimens, i.e., biological cells that are transparent with optical properties slightly
differing from their surroundings in terms of absorbance and color.
These methods do not directly estimate a dense depth map that describes the scene
geometry, but extract it from the phase(or argument) of the numerical reconstruction at
the optimal focus plane, i.e., the reconstruction distance at which the amplitude (or the
phase) of the computed numerical reconstruction is the sharpest.
This two-step methodology is called Quantitative phase imaging (QPI) and is based
on the phase delay that is introduced as light traverses the biological sample during the
hologram acquisition. This phase delay is distributed according to the intracellular refractive index and can be directly visualized for the visualization of the three-dimensional cell
structure. However, we can extract other relevant information from the obtained phase ϕ,
such as the cellular thickness (h) and intracellular refractive index nc , using a decoupling
approach [15].
There exist other methods for pure amplitude objects that directly estimate a dense
depth map by dividing the spatial domain into independent areas with a size that depends
on the desired depth map resolution. Then, they find the optimal focus plane for each of
these areas.
The common aspect of these two types of approaches is the estimation of the "optimal
focus plane", i.e., the distance of the sharpest numerical reconstruction from a set of
candidate distances. This estimation is performed by evaluating the level of focus of each
numerical reconstruction using a FM that can be defined mathematically or using a deep
neural architecture.
65
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
Figure 2.4 gives a schematic diagram of the discussed methods with their respective
inputs and outputs. The first and the second types of approaches take as input the
hologram numerical reconstruction which has been computed as described in Section 1.4.
In contrast, the third type of approach directly estimates using a CNN network the infocus phase (or amplitude) of the numerical reconstruction at the optimal focus plane.
