Here we will use the term scene optimal focus plane distance, rather than the scene
position which means that the processed scene is a planar object located at a certain
distance which is not the case for the discussed methods.
2.2.1
Depth From Focus
The DFF is a relatively simple methodology to retrieve the scene optimal focus plane
distance. As its name suggests, the method relates the level of focus with the used
reconstruction distance and tries to find the axial distance where the amplitude (or phase)
of the computed numerical reconstruction is the sharpest.
The main component of the DFF methods is the used FM to quantify the level of focus.
The different types of FM that have been proposed in the literature can be grouped
into three groups: the sparsity-based measures, the gradient-based measures, and the
transform-based measures.
66
2.2. Automatic Shape Focus Plane Estimation
Sparsity Based Measures
In signal processing, a signal is said to be sparse if its decomposition within a predetermined basis, results in a minimal set of non-null coefficients. In simple terms, the
signal energy is concentrated within a few non-null coefficients. This notion of sparsity
can be used to quantify the in and out-of-focus numerical reconstruction using Sparsity
Measures.
The intuition behind using sparsity measures as a focus measure is relatively simple: As
we move away from the optimal focus plane, the energy of the object will be distributed
across a larger area which is proportional to the distance to the optimal focus plane.
Therefore, the computed level of sparsity will be low at the out-of-focus plans, and high
at the optimal focus plane.
A pioneer work on the subject is Gillespie et al. [79] who proposed the self Entropy
(ENTR) computed from the histogram of the magnitude of the numerical reconstructions.
The entropy curve obtained along the optical axis does not show an absolute maximum
at the focus distance but is symmetrical. Therefore, to find the correct position of the
focus plane, a symmetry testing algorithm is used to evaluate the point of symmetry.
Subsequently, Ren et al. [80] extended the use of ENTR for multi-object depth detection
by optimizing the ENTR using a centered patch around each pixel of the spatial domain.
Ma et al. [81] used the Gray Level Variance (GLV) to estimate a dense depth map
of a doll’s head using 6 × 6 no-overlapping patches spatial decomposition. The author
motivates the use of the operator by the fact that for sufficiently contrasted and textured
objects, the value of the GLV operator progressively increases as we get closer to the
optimal focus due to the higher texture variation at the edges and corners of an infocus image compared to an out-of-focus (or blurred) one. McElhinney et al. [82] extend
the method by adding a background/foreground segmentation step, using a manually
defined threshold of the maximum obtained variance level. The same methodology is
subsequently used by the same authors in [83] for surface anomaly detection using either
overlapping (81 × 81) patches centered around each pixel for high-precision depth maps
or no-overlapping patches for low-quality depth maps.
Dubois et al. [84] proposed the Integral of the Amplitude Modulus (IAM) of the
numerical reconstruction. The authors show that this measure is minimized for objects of
pure amplitude while it is maximized for pure phase objects. Antkowiak et al. [85] extend
the method for a more localized version, where the same metric is used but is normalized
using a local intensity value. Li et al. [86] proposed to use the measure in [84] on the
67
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
Fourier Transform (FT) of the numerical reconstruction, which is called the spectral ℓ1
norm. Trujillo et al. [87] propose the Modified Enclosed Energy (MEE) method, which
first manually defines a minimal bounding box around the non-focused object of interest
at an initial reconstruction distance. Then, on each numerical reconstruction, the authors
redefines the minimal bounding box containing the object energy. Finally, the distance
at which the bounding box has a minimal size is selected as the optimal focus plane.
Yang et al. [88] propose the Correlation Coefficient (CC) based on the intuition that
the different numerical reconstructions computed around the optimal focus plane will
exhibit similar texture. Therefore, the authors propose fixing a certain interval, called
the correlation interval, around each numerical reconstruction. Then, they evaluate the
level of correlation with the other numerical reconstructions inside the chosen interval.
Finally, the particle position is selected where the maximum correlation is reached.
Memmolo et al. [89] proposed the Contrast of Texture (CT) measure which is the
square root of the standard deviation of the image gray level by its mean. The method
is evaluated on a large number of holograms created using a stretching [90] method.
Subsequently, the same authors [91] proposed the use of the Gini index (GI) measure
initially proposed for natural images.
The previously cited methods require mono-chromatic holograms as input, there are
also interesting methods for polychromatic holograms [71, 92–96].
Gradient Based Measures
Rather than finding the axial distance by detecting where the energy is concentrated
in a small area, one can notice that an in-focus object should contain more contour
information than an out-of-focus one. Therefore, one can quantify the level of focus by
detecting how much contour or edge is present in the numerical reconstruction. Different
types of edge or contour detection methods have been proposed in the literature, either
based on the first or second derivative of the numerical reconstruction [97].
Tachiki al. [18] proposed the Modified Sum Laplacian (LoG) operator for multi-object
depth estimation. The authors applied LoG operators to a no-overlapping patch spatial
decomposition, where each patch is referred to as a cell and is considered an independent
object. The author categorized each cell into three categories: either a textured area,
where a maximum of the focus curve is expected; or a uniformly textured area, where
a minimum of the focus curve is expected; or a background area where the median of
the focus curve falls below a predefined threshold. To select the optimal focus plane, the
68
2.2. Automatic Shape Focus Plane Estimation
authors proposed using the local peak height, where instead of finding the absolute peak
of the focus curve, the authors selected all the peaks in the focus curve and subtracted
from them the value of the closest valley. Then, the peak with the largest height difference
(or local peak height) is selected and is kept as an estimate of the optimal focus plane
only if its widths pass a predefined threshold. The remaining are considered FP peaks.
The presence of a global minimum rather than a global maximum in the focus curve is
known as a polarity change and appears when the processed image is uniformly textured.
This phenomenon is common for most of the FM in the literature and has to be correctly
handled, especially when using non-overlapping spatial domain decomposition.
Langehanenberg et al. [98] proposed the High Frequency Sum (SPEC), which is the
sum of the high frequencies obtained after high-pass filtering of the FT of the numerical
reconstruction. The proposed operator is tested on pure amplitude and pure phase objects
for living cells, and particle tracking.
Ren et al. [99] propose using the lp-norm of the 2D structure tensor eigenvalues computed around each pixel (x, y) of the reconstruction plane. The tensor matrix is derived
from the gradient of the image and summarizes the predominant directions of the gradient
in a specified neighborhood of a point. Tamamitsu et al. [100] proposed the Sparsity of
the Gradient (SoG) to quantify the level of edge sparsity by evaluating a sparsity metric,
namely the GI and Tamura coefficient (TC) computed on the spatial gradient modulus
of the image, rather than directly on the image itself. The authors evaluate the proposed
methodology using RoI of different sizes centered on the investigated sample using both
the reconstruction amplitude and retrieved phase. The experimental results show, first
that for sparse samples the GI is less robust for large RoI sizes and is subject to polarity
change. Second, when the sparsity metrics are evaluated on the magnitude of the retrieved
phase the obtained in-focus curves are smoother and sharper. The authors evaluate the
proposed methods on the different types of samples, to demonstrate that the proposed
method can generalize to different types of specimens without being subject to polarity
change when the RoI size is well chosen.
There are also interesting methods for polychromatic holograms [101].
Transform Based Measures
Calculating the gradient directly on the amplitudes of numerical reconstructions may
not be the optimal approach, as they can be susceptible to corruption with speckle noise.
An alternative is to compute the numerical reconstruction using a more robust approach,
69
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
such as the wavelet transform.
For example, Onural et al. [102] reinterprets Fresnel propagation through a wavelet
decomposition, using a basis defined by the Fresnel propagation kernel. The used wavelets
are parametrized with a scaling parameter that defines the reconstruction distance and
two spatial translation parameters. Subsequently, Buraga-Lefebvre et al. [103] re-uses
the same formulation and constructs a numerical reconstruction volume by varying the
wavelet scale factors. Then the optimal reconstruction distance is selected where the
modulus of the reconstruction is maximum.
Lebrun et al. [104] also use wavelet transform with varying scale factors for numerical
reconstruction, and apply it to recover an EFI of off-axis objects positioned on an inclined
plane at an angle θ relative to the hologram plane.
Liebling et al. [19] proposed the Fresnelet-based autofocus algorithm (WAVFRES)
that uses a novel decomposition basis constructed with separable products of cubic Battle–Lemarié scaling [105] and wavelet functions. This basis referred to as FRESNELETS,
is then propagated using the Fresnel function to a distance d from the hologram plane. The
transformed basis is subsequently employed to decompose the numerical reconstruction
obtained at the same distance. The authors demonstrated that the coefficients resulting
from the hologram decomposition in the initial basis can be used to reconstruct it at
any distance using the projected basis, and vice versa. To evaluate the level of focus
in the numerical reconstruction, it is decomposed with the associated basis at the same
distance. Then, the obtained coefficients are sorted from largest to smallest, and the sum
of a portion of the largest ones is calculated. Finally, the optimal focus plane is found at
the distance where the energy is maximized using a limited number of reconstructions.
Benchmarks
In the aforementioned referenced studies, the proposed methods are frequently introduced without comparison to state-of-the-art techniques. This omission may be attributed
to the absence of open-access datasets and benchmarks available for consolidating the
proposed methods. However, few works [17, 106–110] tried to evaluate the highly cited
methods. The evaluation is often done using two or three hologram generated optically.
Recently, Mohammed et al. [108] evaluated 13 focusing operators, including most
of those mentioned in the preceding sections. The focusing operators are evaluated on
simulated and optically acquired single-particle holograms. The authors considered both
the case of pure amplitude and pure phase particles. The obtained experimental results
70
2.2. Automatic Shape Focus Plane Estimation
show that most of these operators exhibit similar behavior, some of them tend to have
polarity change or noisier focus curve with multiple extremum.
In the current literature, the GLVN is most often preferred to other FM for its simplicity, speed, and accuracy.

2.2.2
Deep Learning Methods
In recent years, the intersection of advanced neural network architectures and sophisticated optimization techniques has paved the way for unprecedented breakthroughs in
image analysis, pattern recognition, and visual perception. Holographic optimal focus
plane detection has also greatly benefited from this new trend, empowering researchers
to consistently pioneer innovative approaches that extend the frontiers of what has been
accomplished so far.
In this section, we discuss some of the proposed learning-based approaches for hologram auto-focusing. Interested readers can refer to [20] for an extended review.
The discussed method can be categorized into two groups. The first group contains
the earlier deep learning-based approaches that simply replace traditional FM by learned
neural architecture. These methods have not drastically changed the paradigm of optimal
focus plane detection but have changed how the focus level is described by the network.
Instead of supervising the network to distinguish the in-focus numerical reconstruction
71
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
from a set of candidate ones, either by predicting a binary label (in/out of focus) or
continuous focus value, these methods supervised the network to predict its offset (or its
distance) from the optimal focus plane. In this way, the network not only learns how
to describe the focus level but also to distinguish which numerical reconstructions is the
closest one to the optimal focus plane. In addition, this new problem formulation helps to
partially alleviate the lack of large training dataset data, as each numerical reconstruction
can be considered as a training sample.
The second group contains the deep learning methods that directly predict the phase
(or amplitude) of the numerical reconstruction at the optimal focus plane, either given
as input an out-of-focus numerical reconstruction or directly from the input hologram for
the most recent approaches.
Regression and Classification Methods
A Pioneer work for deep hologram optimal focus detection was proposed by Pitkaaho
et al. [112, 113], how trained an AlexNet architecture [114] to predict, given a numerical
reconstruction, the propagation distance (or offset) that should be added to the used
reconstruction distance in order to reach the optimal focus plane. The authors used
a dataset of 494 holograms of semitransparent Madine Darby Canine Kidney (MDCK)
multicellular samples. Each hologram is recorded at the optimal reconstruction distance,
and at 20 other reconstruction distances located at ±100mm from the in-focus plane
using an offset step of 10mm. In total, for each hologram, 21 numerical reconstructions
are recorded with an offset equal to i ∗ 10mm from the optimal reconstruction plane.
Then, the network is supervised to predict the correct offset label i used to record the
hologram. The author highlighted that the trained network generalizes well for different
types of biological cells. However, it fails when the recording setup is different from the
training one.
Ren et al. [115] removed the need for numerical reconstructions, and directly used
the hologram as input for the network. The authors created a dataset by capturing the
local area of the USAF 1951 test pattern at different axial and spatial positions using
linear motion controllers. The axial positions are discretized to five discrete values, and
the utilized reconstruction distance is considered as a class label to train the network.
The auto-focusing problem was re-formulated as a classification task, where the network
was trained to forecast the label corresponding to the used reconstruction distance. The
visualization of the T-SNE [116] projection derived from the network final hidden layer
72
2.2. Automatic Shape Focus Plane Estimation
features reveals that the trained model effectively assimilated pertinent features. The
extraction of these relevant features enables accurate determination of the correct focus
distance. Notably, it exhibits superior performance compared to the SVM and K Nearest
Neighbors (KNN) methodologies.
The same authors extended their methodology in [117] to handle continuous values for
the reconstruction distance. The authors replaced the classification head with a regression
one and supervised the network using mean square error between the ground truth reconstruction distance and the predicted distance. Subsequently, Montoya et al. [118] used
a similar regression learning-based autofocusing strategy and proposed FocusNET. The
proposed network is a sample CNN architecture that given as input a hologram applies
a resizing layer, and a rotation layer, and then extracts the amplitude of the FT. The
obtained amplitude is concatenated with the rotated and resized hologram to regress the
optimal reconstruction. The use of the FT has been demonstrated in [119] to be more
effective for training the network and increasing its generalization ability.
More recently, Cuenat et al. [120] introduced a novel approach employing tiny versions
of three cutting-edge network architectures: VGG16 [121], Vision Transformer (ViT)B16 [122], and Swin Transformer (SwinT) [123]. These reduced networks underwent
training and assessment using data recorded optically using a pseudo-periodic pattern.
In addition, the authors simulated data utilizing the USAF 1951 resolution chart. For
each dataset, two cases were simulated—one treating the object as a pure amplitude
and the other as a pure phase object. To enhance model performance, the authors advocated supervising the models using the log(cosh) loss function [124], originally employed in Variational Auto Encoders (VAE). The experimental results highlighted that
the proposed tiny architectures closely approximate the performance of the original architectures while having a lower inference speed. The authors highlighted the superiority
of transformer-based architectures over convolution-based ones, ultimately selecting the
tiny ViT architecture for its robustness and adeptness in handling occlusion.
Autofocus and Unwrapped Phase Extraction
Rather than predicting discrete or continuous depth estimates, researchers have studied if the network was able to predict an in-focus numerical reconstruction from the input
hologram. This assumption is quite reasonable, as the pre-trainned networks for the regression and classification tasks have demonstrated that the learned feature maps are
relevant for predicting the correct in-focus distance. Therefore the same features should
73
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
contain the necessary information to reconstruct the in-focus numerical reconstruction.
For example, Wu et al. [125, 126] proposed the HIDEF network based on a U-NET
architecture to simultaneously perform autofocusing and phase recovery. To train the
network, the authors created a dataset by capturing aerosol particles using an on-chip
holographic microscope. Then, each hologram is numerically propagated without prior
removal of twin images at various reconstruction distances, and non-overlapping patches
are extracted at each reconstruction distance. Finally, the network is supervised to predict
the in-focus un-wrapped phase and amplitude information given the cropped patches
computed at different reconstruction distances. The ground truth optimal focus plane
is computed using the TC measure, and then the unwrapped phase is computed using
the Multi Height Phase Recovery (MH-PR) [127]. The trained network has demonstrated
superior performance than the MH-PR method. However, the obtained performance drops
progressively as the offset between the reconstruction distance of the input numerical
reconstruction and the optimal focus plane increases. This can be explained by the
fact that for higher offsets, the obtained numerical reconstruction becomes more blurred,
making it harder for the network to approximate what the in-focus version of the image
should look like. This is similar to what can occur in image denoising (or deblurring),
where the higher the degradation level, the lower the obtained performance, as the network
cannot approximate the appropriate denoised (or deblurred) image.
Huang et al. [128] extended the previous methodology by adding two contributions.
First, as the generator produces synthetic data, an additional discriminator network receives both real and synthetic data and learns to differentiate between them. Through
this process, the generator improves its ability to create more convincing data. Second,
the authors use M different numerical reconstructions around a mean reconstruction distance, rather than a single one. Then, the generator network which is a Recurrent U-Net
network [129], takes all the computed numerical reconstructions and fuses them into a
single one using a Convolutional Recurrent Block [130].
Zhang et al. [131] proposed the use of a U-net network to recover the in-focus unwrapped phase directly from the input hologram. For this purpose, the authors generated
a large-scale synthetic dataset of pure-phase objects holograms. The dataset was created
using 10000 couples of generated images (phase and intensity). This dataset encompassed
two distinct recording intervals: a close-range and a far-range interval. Additionally, the
authors generated a supplementary dataset by randomly assigning phase information to
the background. Due to the discontinuity that can appear in the ground truth phase
74
2.3. Current Limitations
map, the authors proposed to supervise the neural network, by decomposing the predicted phase into cosin and sin images. Then compare them to the ground truth values.
The experimental results show that the network gives a reasonable performance for closerange recording distance. However, the performance tends to decrease as the recording
distance is far away.
In a similar work, Ren et al [132, 133] proposed an end-to-end learning architecture
called HRNet to directly predict different reconstruction modalities, including in-focus
amplitude reconstruction, and quantitative phase imaging.
The previously cited methods can be used only to reconstruct either intensity or phase.
To alleviate this issue, Wang et al. [134] proposed a one-to-two U-Net architecture called
(Y-Net) to realize simultaneous reconstruction of both intensity and phase from a single
hologram. The architecture is based on the original U-Net architecture, with an additional
expansion path. The authors train the proposed network, on a hologram generated from
mouse Phagocytes, and compares its performance to a single U-Net for intensity and phase
prediction in the same expansion path. The experimental results show the U-Net network
is not capable of learning the reconstruction of intensity and phase at the same time.
Later on, the same authors extended their methodology and proposed Y4-Net [135] to
handle dual-wavelength hologram reconstruction.
2.3
Current Limitations
In this chapter, we have discussed how we can recover the focus plane from the recorded
hologram. In summary, the discussed method tries to find the axial distance where the
computed numerical reconstruction contains the most texture information inside a minimal bounding box. This process can be performed by computing the level of focus at
each sampled axial distance using a set of FM.
In the early literature, the used FM were mostly designed using both linear and nonlinear combinations of image derivatives. Such approaches result under optimal conditions
in a bell-shaped focus curve with a single extremum at the optimal distance. However,
in practice, the obtained focus curve is often noisy and contains multiple extrema. In
the recent literature, the FM are defined by CNN architecture and are trained on a large
hologram dataset to output a perfect bell-shaped curve with a maximum at the ground
truth focal distance value using well-designed loss functions. The learned CNN models
have demonstrated their ability to extract relevant features from the input hologram and
75
Partie , Chapter 2 – Related Works
to use those features to predict the correct optimal focus plane distance. More recent
studies have further demonstrated that the obtained features can be used to directly
predict the in-focus phase map.
The success of CNN in predicting the optimal focus plane is due to a well-posed
problem. In fact, the model is simply supervised to map the input hologram to a single
depth information, which can be done reasonably well using modern CNN architectures.
The problem arises when the network is required to predict for each point of the scene
its exact axial and spatial position. This poses a challenge for the model due to a dual
dilemma. On one side, during hologram recording, every scene point influences all pixels
in the hologram, leading to the loss of spatial localization of the scene object within
the hologram plane. Conversely, the absence of spatial localization poses a challenge
for conventional CNN architectures, which rely on the convolution operation to extract
features locally around each pixel. In other terms, there is no pixel-to-pixel mapping
between the input hologram and the output depth map which can be found for example
in image segmentation.
To overcome these challenges, the discussed methods do not directly estimate the scene
geometry from the input hologram but utilize the phase (or argument) of the numerical
reconstruction at the estimated optimal focus distance of the scene. The obtained phase
contains the phase delay that has been introduced during the hologram acquisition and
that provides relevant information about the structure of the investigated scene.
In the present manuscript, the phase information is randomly sampled for each scene
point during the hologram recording and thus does not relate to any depth information.
Therefore, we cannot use a single optimal focus distance to recover the surface profile
using the phase of the numerical reconstruction at the estimated distance. In order to
predict a dense scene depth map, a direct mapping has to be found between each scene
point and the input hologram for scene geometry estimation.
Through this manuscript we will discuss how we can effectively establish a pixel-topixel mapping to design efficient learning-based methods for extracting the scene geometry
from holographic data with high precision. Different types of approaches will be considered
and then evaluated on their speed and accuracy. Finally, we will evaluate how the scene
geometry estimation error affects motion estimation in holographic video compression.
76
Part I

Numerical Reconstruction

In this chapter, we will introduce a non-learning-based method that will be used as
a baseline for evaluating subsequent methods. The discussed methodology is based on
the DFF methodology which is a well-documented approach in image processing and is
extensively used in Digital Holography (DH) and DHM for autofocusing as presented in
Section 2.2.1.

3.1
Proposed Methodology
In this section, we will discuss the main components of the proposed DM-DFF method.
78
3.1. Proposed Methodology
3.1.1
Problem Formulation
Given a hologram H ∈ CL×L , our goal is to retrieve an estimate of the used scene
geometry in the form of a depth map denoted D̂ of size L × L, that associate for each
point (x, y) of the spatial domain it axial position (or depth) D(x, y) w.r.t the hologram
plane and which is the closest possible to the ground truth depth map D. To do so we will
rely on the works discussed in section 2.2.1, which in essence try to quantify the level of
focus on each computed numerical reconstruction using a Focus Measures (FM) function.
Then, they select the axial distance of the numerical reconstruction with the highest focus
level to estimate the scene axial distance. The main difference here is that the focus will
be maximized locally rather than globally to retrieve the scene geometry from the computed numerical reconstructions accurately. Figure 3.1 gives an overview of the proposed
methodology which compromises three steps. First, a numerical reconstruction volume is
constructed by computing a series of planar numerical reconstructions, at various sampled
axial distances. Second, the spatial domain is decomposed into a set of patches, and the
focus is optimized independently for each patch. Finally, the initial estimate is enhanced
using a local consensus and an Extended Focus Image (EFI) is computed by assigning
to each point (x, y) the intensity of the numerical reconstruction at the estimated depth.
The estimated depth D̂ and EFI map can be seen as the RGB-D format that has been
used to record the hologram as described in Section 1.2. Here, we will suppose that the
surface described by the depth is continuous for each object and will vary according to
the geometry of the investigated objects.
In this context, we assume that the surface represented by the extracted depth map is
continuous and exhibits variations, i.e. local surface curvature, which fluctuates according
to the geometry of the objects under investigation. For instance, the resulting surface
may display null variation in areas corresponding to planar portions of an object, while
showcasing pronounced variations in regions with high curvature.

Given a minimum zmin and maximum zmax values that bound the axial distance of the
recorded scene, a set of N candidates axial distances are uniformly sampled as follows:

3.1. Proposed Methodology
In the context of overlapping patch decomposition, the estimated axial distance is
only attributed to the central pixel of the patch, whereas in the non-overlapping patch
decomposition, the estimated distance is allocated to every pixel within the patch.
The decision between these two decompositions is influenced by both computational
efficiency and precision. In the overlapping decomposition, the focus is estimated locally
around each pixel, enhancing the ability to capture rapid variations in surface geometry.
However, this performance gain comes at the expense of high computation costs. On the
contrary, non-overlapping decomposition reduces computational complexity by processing
each pixel only once, but it may struggle to capture rapid surface variations if the patch
size is not appropriately adjusted.

In addition to multiple local extrema, the obtained focus curve can also be subject to
polarity change which can be observed in two cases. First, if the investigated object is a
pure phase object [84]. Second, if the investigated object has a uniform texture with no
contour information. In both cases, the amplitude of the numerical reconstruction at the
optimal focus distance will lack contour information and thus a global minimum of the
focus curve is reached.

of the object surface and the used patch size.
To better describe this phenomenon, we introduce two metrics that are used for visual
diagnosis of the obtained results. The first metric is the SVI, which measures the number
of unique axial distances of the object surface inside the extracted patch. The second
metric is the MIA which measures the intersection area between the planar patch and
the object surface at the optimal focus plane. The two metrics can be mathematically
formulated as:

In the following example, the smaller patch completely intersects the object surface
which is defined at a single axial distance, thus the MIA and the SVI are equal to 1. For
the larger patch size, the surface of the intersection between the object and the patch at
the optimal focus plane is relatively small with an MIA of 0.21 and an SVI of 3.
Those metrics are reflected on the obtained focus curve, where we can observe that
the larger the SVI the noisier the focus curve, with several peaks or valleys at the axial
distance where the object surface is defined. The height of those peaks or valleys will be
correlated with the obtained MIA, the higher it is, the greater the computed focus level.
However, the value of the MIA alone can not ensure a higher peak, as the surface texture
also plays an important role in the measured value.
In summary, a good patch size should have a low SVI and a high MIA, while containing
a sufficient amount of texture to correctly asses the focus level. In practice, the patch size
is predefined based on the conducted experimental results, but it can also be manually
changed by the user so that the EFI associated with the predicted depth map contains
the least amount of speckle possible.
3.1.6
Depth refinement by voting agreement
In the case of overlapping spatial decomposition, the focus distance is estimated locally
around each pixel to capture rapid surface variation. However, the obtained surface is not
always smooth and can contain discontinuities due to the lack of robustness of the FM.
To reduce the discontinuities present in the initial depth map estimate, we propose a local
consensus method based on a voting agreement. The procedure is relatively simple, given
a centered patch around each pixel from the initial depth map, a histogram of the patch
is computed and the axial distance with the highest count is selected. More formally,

Extended Focus Image
An EFI is created by assigning for each pixel of the spatial domain the intensity of

The refined depth map and the EFI represent the final RGB-D scene geometry estimation

3.2. Background segmentation
3.2
Background segmentation
So far, we have estimated the object surface on the entire spatial domain, however
in practice the object is only located in a part of the spatial domain. Therefore, a segmentation step is required to filter the foreground points from the background ones. This
segmentation step can be performed in different manners. Here the minimal spreading
energy support approach [87] is chosen because it does not require any hyperparameter
tunning or model pre-training. This approach illustrated in Figure 3.7, is relatively simple. For each computed numerical reconstruction Izi a minimal spreading energy support
Ωzi is computed using the following formula:
P
Ωzi = arg min P
Ω
(x,y)∈Ω Izi (x, y)
(x,y)∈X ×Y Izi (x, y)
= T.
(3.20)
where T is the threshold hyperparameter which can be set to 1.
As discussed in Section 1.4, the energy of a given object is spread inside a double conic
shape, where the base of the cone with minimal energy support is located at the optimal
focus plane. Then, as we move away either to the left or right from this optimal focus
plane, the object energy support progressively increases and reaches its maximum at the
hologram plane.
For a scene composed of multiple objects, with each object spreading its energy inside
a double cone shape, the minimal energy support of the scene is given by the union of
the minimal energy support of each object at the optimal focus plane. Unfortunately,
the optimal focus plane of the object is not accessible. However, we can approximate the
scene minimal energy support by intersecting all the computed numerical reconstruction
supports, as follows:
Ω=

Ωzi .

i=0
An example is given in Figure 3.7, where the scene is composed of four objects: three
dice and one checkerboard. The reader can observe that the three dice are completely
included inside the spatial domain of the checkerboard. Therefore, we can expect that
their respective double cones will be included inside the checkerboard double cone, and
finding the minimal energy support of the scene is equivalent to finding the minimal energy
support of the checkerboard. This assumption is confirmed by the computed numerical
reconstructions and their respective supports. The reader can observe that the size of the

obtained support progressively diminishes as we move toward the checkerboard optimal
focus plane located at z220 . Then, when we intersect all the obtained supports, we observe
that the resulting support perfectly matches the spatial domain of the checkerboard.
3.3
Experiments
In this section, the proposed DM-DFF method is evaluated on computer-generated
holograms using different types of focus measures.
3.3.1
Experimental Setup
Datasets: The proposed methodology is evaluated using the IRT b-com holographic
videos open-access dataset, specifically designed for codec analysis and machine learning
applications. This dataset comprises six distinct scenes (Piano, Table, Woods, Dices,
Cars, Animals), each consisting of 300 holographic frames recorded along a predefined
path. The recorded holograms have a size 1024 × 1024 and a pixel pitch of 6µm.
In the following experiments and the subsequent ones, the evaluation metrics will be
90

computed based on the frames indexed by i ∗ 10, starting from the first frame. For each
scene, the selected 30 frames for the evaluation cover all possible scene orientations, and
thus are sufficient for correctly assessing the performance of the proposed methods. The
remaining frames will serve as a training set for subsequent learning-based methods.
Axial Domain Sampling : In the dataset all the scenes are recorded using a Z axis
discretized into 255 uniformly sampled values in the interval [1/zmax , zmax ]. The sampled
axial distances are mathematically given as:

were sampled between the rear and the front of the test scene. In contrast, in the present
work, reconstruction distances are sampled between minimal and maximal axial distances
common to all test scenes. This difference in sampling can lead to a larger error magnitude in the present work, as the sampled axial distance can be far away from the scene
boundaries.
Numerical Volume Reconstruction: A numerical reconstruction is computed at
each sampled axial using the ASM method. Each numerical reconstruction took 1.31
second using the native 2021 Matlab FT implementation on 11th Gen Intel Core i911900F.
Focus Measures: In the original contribution [136], we evaluated 21 FMs divided
into five categories: Gradient-based operators, Laplacian-based operators, DCT-based
operators, Wavelet-based operators, and Miscellaneous operators. However, to facilitate a
more comprehensive analysis, we will only present the first three best-performing metrics.
Interested reader can refer to the original contribution for a broader benchmark.
The selected FMs compromises one sparsity metric which is the GLVN and two
gradient-based metrics which are the Image contrast (CONT) and the Thresholded absolute gradient (GRAT). The FMs are mathematically defined as follows:

First, independently of the used FM and the patch size, the automatic switch gives
a significant performance gain in comparison to a direct selection of the arg max value.

error.
Second, the obtained performance tends to increase with the used patch size until it
reaches a breakpoint for a too-large patch size. This phenomenon can be analyzed for a
given scene using the SVI and MIA metrics as illustrated in Figure 3.8. The reader can
observe from the figure that the SVI metric is low for the flat areas that face directly
toward the hologram plane such as the middle part of the piano and is large for highly
curved areas or flat areas that are slanted w.r.t to the hologram plane. On the other
hand, the MIA is relatively high at a low patch size and decreases with the increase in
the patch size such as the top part of the piano.

As stated earlier an increase in the SVI causes a noisier focus curve, and therefore
uncertainty in the choice of the optimal focus curve if the associated MIA is not high
enough to ensure the highest peaks or values at the optimal focus plane. For example,
the reader can observe from the figure that the top part of the Piano is approximately
perpendicular to the hologram plane and thus results in high SVI and low MIA which
combined lead to a large absolute estimation error. In this case, using a smaller patch size
can increase the MIA while decreasing the SVI, thus reducing the estimation uncertainty.
However, the extracted patch will have a lower level of texture that can be used to estimate
the focus level correctly. As the patch size increases, more texture can be used to estimate
the focus level, but a noisier focus curve is expected. So there is a trade-off between the
patch size and the obtained performance.
Another case that can cause a significant drop in performance is when the investigated
scene contains areas with a high-depth gradient. For example, areas where there is a

3.3.5
Foreground/Background Segmentation
For each test hologram, a foreground support is estimated as described in Section 3.2,
and the result is compared to the ground truth support using the Precision, Recall, and
IoU metrics. The foreground support estimation and evaluation were repeated multiple
times by varying the value of T in Eq. 3.20, and the obtained metrics are reported in
Table 3.5. The computation of the foreground support takes 15.15 seconds for a reconstruction volume with 256 numerical reconstructions, and it is independent of the content
of the processed scene.
The reader can observe from the table, that an increase of T causes a decrease in the
precision and an increase in the recall. This behavior indicates that more scene points

the threshold where there is a good tradeoff between the size of the obtained support and
the number of retrieved foreground points.
Figure 3.12 gives an example of the obtained mask using different values of T . The
reader can observe that a high value of T is required to retrieve the low-intensity scene
points. However, as the value of T increases, more background points are labeled as
foreground, which results in a coarser mask.
3.4
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have introduced a baseline method for retrieving the scene geometry from the hologram numerical reconstruction. The proposed method is based on the
principle of DFF, and it extends it for extracting a per-pixel depth map, instead of a
unique depth value for the entire scene. For each pixel, it assigns the axial distance where
the local focus around the pixel is optimal. The method gives reasonable performance on
planar surfaces. However, the use of nonoriented planar patches limits the performance
of the method on highly curved areas.
The combination of no-overlapping patch decomposition and the refinement method
helps to capture slow varying surface variation, but its steel lacks accuracy for higher
precision application.

Chapter 4
PATCH N ET: A CNN M ODEL FOR
P IXEL - WISE F OCUS E STIMATION
In the previous chapter, we have introduced a simple baseline method, which in essence
tries to quantify the local focus level change using a mathematically defined FM operator. Then, it assigns the distance where the focus is locally optimal as an estimated scene
position. Although the method gives reasonable results, its performance is limited by its
inability to precisely predict which part of the image contributes to the computed focus
level. This limitation considerably affects the correct approximation of the local object
surface curvature, especially in areas with a high surface variation. To address this limitation, we propose in the present chapter the development of a neural network-based FM
operator that can not only evaluate the focus level but also precisely predict where in the
image are the in-focus pixels as illustrated in Figure 4.1. This problem reformulation can
significantly increase the quality of the retrieved geometry while decreasing the inference
time.
Related Paper
— Nabil Madali, Antonin Gilles, Patrick Gioia, and Luce Morin, "Automatic depth
map retrieval from digital holograms using a deep learning approach," Opt. Express
31, 4199-4215 (2023)
4.1
Proposed Methodology
In this section, we will discuss the main components of the proposed method. First,
the proposed method problem formulation is presented in Section 4.1.1. Then, the used
neural network architecture is described in Section 4.1.2. Sections 4.1.3 and 4.1.4 describe
the loss function and the training strategy used to train the neural network, respectively.
Finally, the geometric interpretation of the proposed method is discussed in Section 4.1.5.
101
Partie I, Chapter 4 – PatchNet: A CNN Model for Pixel-wise Focus Estimation

Figure 4.1 – The figure illustrates the process of focus-level map extraction using the pretrained the learned FM operator called Uθ . The operator takes a sequence of computed
numerical reconstructions as input. Then it predicts a focus level map for each one that
indicates the local level of focus for each pixel in the spatial domain. For a given pixel (x,
y), the predicted values across the input numerical reconstructions sum to 1.
4.1.1
Problem Formulation
Our goal is to learn a neural network denoted U that predicts the local focus level
around each pixel (m, n) of the image, given as input an amplitude image Iz computed
at a distance z. This can be mathematically formulated as:
FˆLz (m, n) = Uθ {Iz } (m, n),
(4.1)
where θ are the network parameters, and FˆLz is the predicted focus level map for the
amplitude image Iz .
Although the problem formulation is conceptually similar to the one used for the DMDFF method, the two methods have some major differences. To better discuss these
differences, we have illustrated in Figure 4.2 the extraction of the local focus map of the
numerical reconstruction I122 from the example illustrated in Figure 4.1. The reader can
observe that to extract the focus level (i, j) (the white dot in the figure) inside the spatial
domain Ω of the cropped numerical reconstruction I122 , we are required first to extract
over
a central patch Pi,j
{I122 } (the white rectangle in the figure) around it that describes
102
4.1. Proposed Methodology
its local neighborhood. Then, we apply the FM to estimate its focus level. The same
operation has to be repeated for each pixel (i, j) of the spatial domain Ω to extract the
local focus maps, which is time-consuming. On the other hand, the proposed approach
does not require extracting overlapping patches around the pixel; instead, it directly uses
a convolution layer to describe the local neighborhoods around each pixel and uses the
extracted information to predict its focus level. In a single network inference, the whole
focus level map can be predicted.

In addition to a faster inference time, the network supervision during the training stage
ensures that appropriate convolution filters are learned to precisely predict the pixel focus
level with a focus curve that is not subject to polarity change and always gives the optimal
focus curve at the global maximum of the curve.

4.1.2
Network Architecture
To effectively estimate the local focus level of each pixel in the input image, we employ
a U-shaped neural network architecture designed to maintain the output size identical to
the input size. The network architecture is illustrated in Figure 4.4 and comprises a
contracting path followed by an expanding path.
104
4.1. Proposed Methodology
3
16 16
16+16 16
1
32+32 32

64+64 64

128+128

Conv 3 × 3, 𝑅𝑒𝐿𝑢
Copy and crop
Conv 1 × 1
Max Pooling 2 × 2
Upsampling 2 × 2
Figure 4.4 – Illustration of the used U-shaped neural network architecture.
The Contracting Path
The contracting path consists of a sequence of convolution blocks(or stages), where
each block contains two convolution layers and ends with a max-pooling operation. The
convolution layers are used to extract local image characteristics, including contours,
edges, and texture information. Then the max-pooling operation condenses the extracted
characteristics, retaining only the most pertinent information while reducing spatial dimensions by a factor of 2.
As we delve deeper into the contracting path, the spatial dimensions progressively
diminish while richer features are extracted. Typically, the network lower layers capture
fundamental features like edges and textures, whereas the deeper layers reveal increasingly
abstract and high-level representations.
The Expansive Path
The extracted local feature vectors serve as the foundation for the expanding path,
which incrementally extracts task-specific features while simultaneously upsampling the
resulting volume to the original input size. The expanding path incorporates skip-link
connections, facilitating the flow of information between the contracting and expanding
105
Partie I, Chapter 4 – PatchNet: A CNN Model for Pixel-wise Focus Estimation
paths. This integration enables the network to predict finer features without suffering
from upsampling artifacts.
4.1.3
Loss Function
To train the network Uθ in Eq. 4.1, we are required to have labeled data (or ground
truth data) that tell us what the expected focus level that the network should predict
when having as input one amplitude image. Then, the network parameters are optimized
to minimize the Euclidean distance between the ground truth data and the predicted one,
as follows:
θ∗ = arg min |F L(Iz ) − Uθ {Iz }|2
(4.4)
θ
where θ∗ is the optimal network parameters that minimize the loss function. Unfortunately, the ground truth for the focus level is unavailable to us. However, we do possess
ground truth scene geometry, specifying the optimal focus distance for each pixel within
the spatial domain. Consequently, we must reformulate the loss function to guide the
model only using the provided scene geometry. To achieve this, we employ the crossentropy loss, which is usually used in multi-label classification tasks for supervising the
network toward correct image labeling. In our scenario, we adopt a similar problem formulation, where the network receives as input one in-focus image and multiple out-of-focus
ones and is trained to discern the in-focus image from the out-of-focus ones.
Here, we consider that for a given pixel (x, y) of the spatial domain X × Y, the correct
in-focus image is located at D(x, y), where D is the ground truth scene depth map. The
remaining numerical reconstructions computed at sampled axial distances z ̸= D(x, y)
are considered as out-of-focus images. The binary vector {1D(x,y)=z : z ∈ Z} indicating
the presence or absence of the in-focus image is called the true distribution in multi-label
classification.
To supervise the network to correctly approximate this distribution, we first apply
the softmax function to the output of the network Uθ {.} to convert for a given pixel
(x, y) the raw focus curve {Uθ {Iz }(x, y) : z ∈ Z} (also known as logits) into a probability
distribution {ŷz (x, y) : z ∈ Z}, as follows:
eUθ {Iz }(x,y)
.
Uθ {Ik }(x,y)
k∈Z e
ŷz (x, y) = P
(4.5)
Each element in the resulting vector represents the probability ŷz (x, y) that the pixel
106
4.1. Proposed Methodology
is in-focus class at the distance z. Then the cross-entropy loss is used to quantify the
difference between this predicted distribution and the true distribution,

eθ (x, y, z) = −1D(x,y)=z · log(ŷz (x, y)) + (1 − 1D(x,y)=z ) · log(1 − ŷz (x, y)).
(4.6)
(4.7)
where 1D(x,y)=z indicates if the optimal focus distance of the (x, y) is located at a
distance z from the hologram plane. When 1D(x,y)=z = 1, the loss term log(ŷz (x, y))
penalizes the model for predicting low probabilities for in-focus class. Similarly, when
1D(x,y)=z = 0, the loss term log(1 − ŷz (x, y)) penalizes the model for predicting high
probabilities for out-of-focus class.
4.1.4
Patch-based Learning
In Eq. 4.6, the loss function is minimized over the entire spatial domain X × Y. This
can be problematic because the larger the size of the spatial domain and the number of
sampled axial distances, the higher the GPU consumption during the network parameters
optimization. To solve this problem we can train the model on a smaller spatial domain,
for example using rectangular patches of size s × s denoted Ω that are randomly sampled
on the surface of the object on each training iteration. The new loss function can be
written as follows:



 1
X X
eθ (x, y, z) .
(4.8)
θ∗ = arg min

 |Ω|
θ
(x,y)∈Ω z∈Z
In addition to reducing the GPU consumption, randomly cropping patches on the surface
of the object during the training step will help to process a larger variety of texture types
thus increasing the network generalization ability. The trained model Uθ is referred to as
the "PatchNet" model for the use of patches during the network training and evaluation.
4.1.5
Geometrical Interpretation
The geometrical interpretation of the proposed methodology is similar to the DMDFF approach, where the used rectangular patch Ω in Eq. 4.8 defines a planar surface
that intersects the object surface as it moved along the axial axis. Each time there is an
107
Partie I, Chapter 4 – PatchNet: A CNN Model for Pixel-wise Focus Estimation
Amplitude Images
interaction an in-focus area appears inside the planar patch with a shape that depends on
the local surface curvature as illustrated in Figure 4.5. The number of times the planar
patch intersects the object surface is given by the SVI metric and the size of the in-focus
area by the MIA metric.
Masks
Focus Level Maps
𝒰𝜃
Figure 4.5 – The figure illustrates the predicted focus value of the model for the central
pixel of the amplitude images (highlighted in white in the figure). The resulting curve is
called the focus curve and is expected to have a global maximum at the optimal focus
plane of the pixel.
Therefore, retrieving the object surface is similar to finding the in-focus pixels on each
computed amplitude image. In the DM-DFF approach, this is done by extracting a patch
around each pixel in the spatial domain and then using a FM operator to evaluate its local
focus level. The extracted patch can be seen as a local descriptor of the pixel neighborhood
and will contain both out-of-focus and in-focus pixels. As the FM operator extracts a
108
4.2. Experimentation
global focus value of the whole patch, it will produce a high value when there are more
in-focus pixels inside the patch than the out-of-focus one and a low value otherwise. As
the FM function does not contain a weighting mechanism that assigns more importance
to the in-focus pixels close to the central pixel than to other in-focus pixels located farther
away, it will have poor sensitivity to relatively small changes in depth variation. In the
present approach, the network convolution filters can extract local feature descriptors
around each pixel with an adequate weighting of the in and out-of-focus pixels inside the
pixel neighborhood. These extracted features are then used to precisely predict the pixel
focus value.
The learned network is faster than a traditional FM operator as it does not require the
extraction of a central patch around each pixel of the amplitude image and can directly
predict a focus level map in a single network inference pass. In addition, the network is
more precise, as it learns the appropriate convolution filters during the training stage to
precisely estimate the focus level.
As the network is supervised to predict a single optimal focus plane per pixel, the
resulting focus curve per pixel will approach a Dirac function with a single maximum
at the optimal focus plane and zero otherwise. The term ’segmentation’ can be used to
describe the behavior of the network that tries to segment the in-focus pixel from each
amplitude image by predicting a binary value of 1 for the in-focus pixel and zero otherwise.
The in-focus pixel binary masks can be extracted from the predicted focus level maps by
filtering the predicted in-focus values along the axial dimension, assigning a value of 1 at
the location of the global maximum of the focus curve and zero otherwise. The reader
can observe that the extracted binary mask perfectly matches the in-focus pixels from
each amplitude image.
In summary, we can conclude that the learned network is similar to a segmentation
network that segments the in-focus pixels from each amplitude image. This is in contrast
to a traditional FM operator, which evaluates the global focus level without providing the
precise location of the in-focus pixels.
4.2
Experimentation
The following section describes the experimentation that has been conducted to validate the proposed approach.
109
Partie I, Chapter 4 – PatchNet: A CNN Model for Pixel-wise Focus Estimation
4.2.1
Experimental Setup
Dataset: The proposed method has been trained and evaluated on the same dataset
as the DM-DFF method. To accurately compare the obtained performance, we have used
the same test set as for the DM-DFF method, which consists of frames from Piano, Table,
Woods, Cars, Dices, and Animals scenes that are indexed by i ∗ 10 (For each scene, we
have a test frame every ten frames). The remaining frames are used for network training.
Here, we have deliberately removed the Dices and Animals scenes from the training
set, using them only for the test stage to evaluate the network performance on unseen
scenes.
For each of the training scenes (Piano, Table, Woods, and Cars), we have 270 training
frames and 30 validation frames. Additionally, we have 30 test frames for each of the test
scenes (Dices and Animals). In total, 1080 frames (270 ∗ 4) are used for the training set,
120 frames (30∗4) for the validation set, and 60 frames (30∗2) for the test set. Numerical
Reconstruction Volume: In a similar manner to the DM-DFF method, a numerical
reconstruction volume is constructed for each frame of the dataset by performing a series
of Nz numerical reconstructions at sampled axial distances. Here, we used the same
candidate axial distance as for the DM-DFF method for two reasons. First, it will provide
a consistent performance analysis across the evaluated methods. Second, the used axial
distance is drawn from the ground truth depth maps and thus can be used to label any
given point in the computed numerical reconstruction as in-focus or out-of-focus.
Network Parameter Optimization: During the training process, every training
frame is selected 10 times (or epochs). When selected, 1000 randomly cropped reconstruction volumes of size Nz × 32 × 32 are sampled on the object surface to optimize the
network. To sample these 3D volumes, a set of pixels (xi , yi ) are randomly sampled on
the object surface. Then centered 2D patches Pxover
around them are defined and used
i ,yi
to crop the scene reconstruction volume. This produces for each sampled pixel (xi , yi )
a cropped reconstruction volume {Pxover
{Izi } : zi : 1, ..., Nz }, which is fed as a batch to
i ,yi
the network. In total, 10 × 1080 × 1000 iterations have been performed, where 10 is the
number of epochs, 1080 is the number of training samples, and 1000 is the number of
sampled volumes per sample. To optimize the network parameters the Adam optimizer
from the PyTorch library is used. The network tends to converge quite easily, and using
more iterations will not necessarily improve the obtained performance.
Evaluation Metric: As for the DM-DFF method, the proposed approach is evaluated
on the quality of the retrieved depth map using MAE metric, and the EFIs are only used
110
4.2. Experimentation
for visual assessment.

Table 4.1 – The obtained MAE metric with and without the refinement using the voting
agreement method denoted as "VA" followed by the used patch size. The first, second,
and third methods are colored green, blue, and red, respectively.
The table reveals that the initial predicted depth maps from the PatchNet model
surpass those predicted by the GLVN model with a notable decrease in MAE error, ranging
from two to three times lower depending on the scene under consideration. Subsequent
refinement processes contribute to enhancing the initial predictions, yielding a mean MAE
of approximately 2 across all the analyzed scenes.
The performance gain from the refinement process is closely related to the used patch
size. The reader can notice that as the patch size increases the performance reaches a
peak and then progressively diminishes. This trend can be attributed to larger patch sizes
tending to flatten initially curved surfaces, consequently amplifying the MAE between the
refined and ground truth surfaces.
In addition, we can notice from the table that there are consistent performance discrepancies across scenes, with notably lower performance for the Piano and Table scenes.
These disparities can be attributed to challenging scene orientation that causes numerous areas with high-depth variation and small size in-focus areas that could be used to
correctly asses the focus score. Indeed, we can assume that when the used planar patch
intersects the scene surface in relatively small areas with a size that is below the size of
111
Partie I, Chapter 4 – PatchNet: A CNN Model for Pixel-wise Focus Estimation
the used convolution filters, then the network fails to correctly estimate the focus level
which results in a higher MAE error.
To delve deeper into this observation, we plot the MAE error against the MIA metric
in Figure 4.6, and we color-coded it using the SVI metric. We recall that the MIA metric
quantifies the size of the in-focus area by computing the ratio of the in-focus pixel to the
total number of pixels inside a given amplitude image. This metric is correlated with the
SVI metric that quantifies the local object surface variation.
The reader can observe from the figure, that independently of the processed scene,
there exists a negative exponential relationship between the MIA metric and the obtained
MAE error. The larger the MIA metric, the easier the focus evaluation and the lower the
MAE error. On the contrary, the smaller the MIA metric, the harder for the model to
evaluate the level of focus, and the greater the obtained the MAE error.
Moreover, we can observe that the in-focus area size is correlated with local surface
variation, where higher SVI values correspond to a smaller MIA metric and lower SVI
values correspond to a larger MIA metric. The SVI metric distribution varies across
scenes, with scenes like Table exhibiting significantly higher areas of greater SVI, thereby
contributing to larger MAE values as shown in Table 4.1.
From the figure, we can notice that over an MIA of 0.3 or an in-focus area over
0.3 × 32 × 32 ≈ 9.6 pixels, the model can correctly estimate the local focus level. Below
this value, the size of the in-focus area is too small for the used convolution filters to
correctly evaluate the focus level. Here it is important to note that the same problem will
occur when increasing the spatial domain of the patch beyond 32 × 32 pixels. As small
MIA can be caused by tiny surface parts, increasing the spatial dimension of the patch
will not necessarily increase its size.
To better illustrate this, we present in Figure 4.7 two contrasting examples: the first
displaying an area characterized by a low SVI and high MIA, and the second exhibiting
the opposite traits. The readers can observe from the figure that for the first case, which
is simpler, the network did correctly retrieve the local object surface, whereas in the
second case, the network completely failed to retrieve the local object surface and only
approximated a mean surface height. This visual analysis comes in complement with the
plot of Figure 4.6 that indicates that as the depth variation is high the size of in-focus
areas decreases and thus the network fails to correctly evaluate the focus level which
results in a bad approximation of the object surface.
The obtained error may also originate from the model inability to accurately gauge the

pixels are kept. This could reduce the error but will significantly increase the computation

4.2.5
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have introduced a learning-based model, which in essence replaces
the classical FM operator to not only predict the focus level but also which pixels are
116
4.2. Experimentation
Figure 4.10 – Illustration of the focus curve produced by the Patch-Net and the GLVN.
The reader can observe that the PatchNet model produces a Dirac function with a global
maximum at the optimal focus plane, contrary to the GLVN operator, which produces
a noisy bell-shaped curve with a standard deviation approximately equal to the depth
extent of the local object surface.
in focus in the image. The conducted experiments have demonstrated that the learned
network can predict the local pixel focus level with high precision and can retrieve finer
scene geometry. In addition to its accuracy, the provided model is faster than a traditional
FM operator as it requires a smaller number of inferences to predict a dense scene depth
map. However, the network may fail to retrieve tiny surface parts with areas that are
below the network receptive field size. The network also suffers from a linear increasing
GPU consumption with an increase in the spatial and axial domain sizes.
An increase in the obtained performance, may not necessarily come with a larger
network architecture and may require a refinement stage that could be guided with the
EFI that provided the overall structure of the scene.

Chapter 5
H- SEG : A H ORIZONTAL
R ECONSTRUCTION VOLUME
S EGMENTATION
In the preceding chapter, we introduced a novel approach to pixel-wise focus estimation, offering an alternative to the conventional FM operator. This innovative method
not only predicts the focus level but also delineates the in-focus pixels within the input
image, mitigating the primary limitations of traditional FM operators. Our experiments
have convincingly showcased the efficacy of this method in accurately retrieving scene
geometry while significantly reducing inference time compared to the DFF method. Nevertheless, a notable drawback lies in its direct correlation between GPU consumption
and the spatial and axial domain discretization sizes. Furthermore, the model tends to
struggle to capture finer details of the scene that fall below the network’s receptive field
size. To address these challenges, this chapter proposes a reformulation of the focus level
estimation problem aimed at enhancing performance while concurrently reducing GPU
requirements.
Related Paper
— Nabil Madali, Antonin Gilles, Patrick Gioia, and Luce Morin, "H-Seg: a horizontal
reconstruction volume segmentation method for accurate depth estimation in a
computer-generated hologram," Opt. Lett. 48, 3195-3198 (2023).
5.1
Proposed Methodology

Section 5.1.1 and its geometric interpretation in Section 5.1.2. Then, the used neural network architecture is described in Section 5.1.3. Finally, the loss function and the training

5.1.1
Problem Formulation
Given a series of numerical reconstructions {Izi } computed at different axial distances
{zi }, a 3D reconstruction volume V is constructed by stacking those reconstructions along
the axial dimension, as follows:
V (x, y, z) = Iz (x, y).
(5.1)
Then, the numerical reconstruction volume is sliced horizontally along the Y axis, and a
neural network denoted Tθ is used to predict an in-focus map for each extracted horizontal
slice. This can be mathematically formulated as follows:
F Ly (x, z) = Tθ {Sy }(x, z),
(5.2)
Sy (x, z) = V (x, y, z),
(5.3)
where Sy is a horizontal slice extracted from the reconstruction volume by fixing the
heights of the points at y, and F Ly is the predicted in-focus level from the neural network
Tθ .
After the training process, the learned network can be used to predict a scene depth
map and an associated EFI, by assigning for each point (x, y) the distance and the intensity
value where the focus level is optimal. More formally:
d(x, y) = arg max{F Ly (x, y)},
(5.4)
EF I(x, y) = Id(x,y) (x, y).
(5.5)
z
120
5.1. Proposed Methodology
where d and EF I are the predicted depth and RGB map and represent the final output
of our proposed methodology.
Before delving into the network architecture and defining the loss function for training,
we will briefly discuss the geometric interpretation of the problem formulation and its
primary advantages over the PatchNet model. This discussion will provide a foundational
understanding of the approach and highlight its improvements over existing methods.
5.1.2
Geometric Interpretation
In both the PatchNet and the DFF method, we employ a planar vertical patch of size
s × s that, as it traversed along the axial axis, intersected the scene at one or multiple
axial distances. Each time the planar patch intersected the scene, the areas of intersection
would appear in focus within the patch. Consequently, retrieving the scene geometry was
similar to identifying when and where there are in-focus pixels inside the planar patch.
In this H-Seg proposed method, the planar vertical patch is substituted with a 2D
horizontal plane that spans along the XZ plane. As this horizontal plane moves along
the Y axis, it will intersect the scene points that will appear as sharp in-focus lines
on the extracted horizontal slice. Therefore, retrieving the scene geometry is similar to
identifying the in-focus lines on each horizontal slice.
Figure 5.2 illustrates an example of an extracted horizontal slice in (d) from the Cars
scene, positioned at the origin of the Y-axis (see Section 1.2), i.e the horizontal slice
defined by the 2D plane (x, 0, z). As depicted in the figure, this extracted horizontal
slice exhibits distinct sharp in-focus lines located at the ground truth intersection points
highlighted in white in (d). Consequently, if the trained network Tθ can accurately extract
these in-focus lines from the horizontal slice, we can retrieve the scene points situated at
the origin of the Y-axis, i.e the 3D points (x, y, z) that have a height equal to y = 0. This
process can be repeated for other horizontal slices to generate a dense scene depth map.
This new formulation offers several advantages. First, despite significantly increasing
both the spatial and axial dimensions, the resulting size of the horizontal slice does not lead
to a substantial escalation in the GPU consumption, as observed in the PatchNet model.
Second, the width of the in-focus lines remains constant across the different horizontal
slices and is only correlated with the speed of the focus change. So we can more easily
retrieve areas with high depth variation compared to using vertical planar patches. Third,
horizontal reconstruction volume scanning decreases the number of inference operations
, where H and W are the height and width of the hologram respectively,
from Hs × Ws to H

5.1.3
Network Architecture
The utilized U-shaped neural network architecture is depicted in Figure 5.3, comprising
a contracting path and an expansive path. The expansive path closely resembles the
one employed in the PatchNet model, consisting of a series of Upsampling convolution
blocks. Each block serves to extract task-specific features by progressively upsampling
information from preceding blocks while incorporating information from the contracting
path to mitigate upsampling artifacts.
The primary modification occurs within the contracting path, where the original backbone network is substituted with the ResNet-50 model, which is known for its efficacy
across various computer vision tasks such as image classification, object detection, and
122
5.1. Proposed Methodology
image segmentation.
The employed ResNet-50 architecture comprises a standard convolutional layer followed by four residual layers. Each residual layer consists of several residual blocks,
each comprising three convolutional operations: the first convolution is used to reduce
the input channel number. Then, the second convolution to extract new spatial features
while maintaining the channel count. Finally, the third convolution is used to increase
the channel number without altering the spatial size. A key aspect of ResNet-50 is its
incorporation of residual connections, which are shortcut connections that add the input
tensor to the output of the residual layer. This mechanism aids in mitigating the vanishing
gradient problem and enables the construction of deeper networks capable of capturing
complex patterns effectively.

5.1.4
The Loss Function
The training loss plays a crucial role in network convergence, especially given the
unbalanced nature of the problem being addressed. Indeed, on each horizontal slice, outof-focus points are considerably more prevalent than in-focus ones. Consequently, during
model training, the model may prioritize predicting out-of-focus labels over the correct
ones, as the error difference between predicting or not predicting the correct in-focus
points tends to be relatively low. For instance, if a given horizontal slice contains only 5%
in-focus points, and the model predicts 100% out-of-focus, it would achieve 95% accuracy
without any parameter optimization.
To address this problem, we can incorporate class weighting into the used loss function.
This will ensure that equal importance is assigned to all classes and will prevent the
dominance of majority classes. Here, we will employ a simple cross-entropy loss that will
result in the following optimization problem:




X
X
1
Φθ (x, y, z) + Ψθ (x, y, z) ,
θ∗ = arg min

 |X × Z|
θ
(x,z)∈X ×Z y∈Y
(5.6)
Φθ (x, y, z) = −1D(x,y)=z · log(Tθ {Sy } (x, z)),
(5.7)
Ψθ (x, y, z) = (1 − 1D(x,y)=z ) · log(1 − Tθ {Sy } (x, z)).
(5.8)
Then, we add the weighting parameter α (set to 0.9 in the experiments) to balance between
the in-focus and out-focus points. More formally :




X
X
1
θ∗ = arg min
αΦθ (x, y, z) + (1 − α)Ψθ (x, y, z) .

 |X × Z|
θ
(x,z)∈X ×Z y∈Y
(5.9)
This final loss will ensure higher penalties when the model fails to predict the correct
in-focus points than out-of-focus ones.
5.2
Experiments
In this section, we will evaluate the proposed method and compare its performance
to that of vertical focus estimation approaches, considering not only precision but also
inference speed and GPU consumption.
124
5.3. Experimental Setup
5.3
Experimental Setup
Dataset: The proposed method has been trained and evaluated on the same train
and test split dataset as for the PatchNet model.
Horizontal Slice Sampling: For each training frame, a scene numerical reconstruction volume of size H × W × Nz is created, where W and H equal to 1024 in the
experiment are the width and height of the hologram respectively, and Nz is the number
of numerical reconstructions. Then, the 3D reconstruction volume is reorganized along
the Y-axis into a set of H horizontal slices of size W × Nz . For each horizontal slice, we
either augment it using a vertical or horizontal flip with a probability of 0.33 each, or we
keep it as it is with a probability of 0.33. The augmented slices are fed to the network in
randomized order with a batch size of 16 elements per iteration. In total, the model was
trained for 1080×1024×10 iterations, where 1080 is the number of training samples, 1024
is the number of horizontal slices per training sample, and 10 is the number of epochs.
For each iteration, the Adam optimizer from the Pytorch library is used to optimize the
network parameters θ according to the loss function in Eq.5.9.
Evaluation Metrics: The MAE metric between the predicted and the ground truth
depth maps is used for model evaluation.
