The performance gap between the two models can be explained by an easier problem
formulation for the H-seg method that does not suffer from varying sizes of the in-focus
area caused by local surface variation. To better illustrate this notion, we have extracted
in Figure 5.4 an example of a vertical planar patch used in the PatchNet method, along
with a horizontal slice located in the middle of the planar patch. The boundaries of the
extracted patch along the x-axis are highlighted in green in the horizontal slice. From the
figure, readers can observe that as the vertical planar patch moves along the axial axis, it
intersects the scene multiple times, and with each intersection, an in-focus area appears
inside the patch.
126
5.3. Experimental Setup
Here, as the used patch size is fixed, we can arguably assume that as the number
of intersections increases, the area of the intersection will decrease, making it harder for
the network to correctly estimate the focus change. On the other hand, the horizontal
slices do not suffer from this problem, as the width of the in-focus lines remains fixed and
independent of the scene geometry.
(a) GT
(b) PatchNet
(c) H-seg
(d) GT
(e) PatchNet
(f) H-seg
Figure 5.5 – Illustration of the retrieved scene geometry using the PatchNet method in
(b) and (e), and the H-seg method in (c) and (f), compared to the ground truth geometry
in (a) and (d).
In this particular example, if we want to retrieve the scene geometry at the middle
line of the planar patch depicted in Figure 5.4 as a dotted line, then, the PatchNet has
to correctly extract the different parts of the scene that are in focus at different distances
which can be quite difficult. On the other hand, the H-seg model has simply to identify
the in-focus lines enclosed on the green line in the horizontal slice which is an easier task.
Figure 5.5 illustrates two examples with different levels of surface variation and the
retrieved surface for both the PatchNet and H-seg. In the first example, we can observe
127
Partie I, Chapter 5 – H-seg : A Horizontal Reconstruction Volume Segmentation
from the figure that when the local surface variation is high, the PatchNet model fails to
correctly retrieve the scene geometry as there is a tiny in-focus area that the model can
use to estimate the focus level. In the second example, we can observe that the PatchNet
model correctly retrieved the large scene areas and failed to retrieve tiny parts located
at the top of the image. In contrast, H-seg consistently retrieves the scene geometry in
both instances. However, the results tend to be relatively coarse, lacking sharp depth
transitions.
The rate at which focus changes between images in the reconstruction volume slows
down with a larger pixel pitch, and results in thinner in-focus lines on the extracted
horizontal slices as illustrated in Figure 5.6. Consequently, when a network is trained
using large pixel pitches, it learns to detect these focus lines based on local sharpness
features. However, when applied to holograms with smaller pixel pitches, the learned
filters may not be effective at this resolution since the in-focus lines become thinner. On
the other hand, when a network is trained using a small pixel pitch, the in-focus lines are
very thin, leading the network to focus more on the overall geometric structure of the line
and less on local features such as line sharpness. As a result, when applied to large pixel
pitches with different geometric formations of in-focus lines, the H-seg network performs
poorly due to its reliance on global features rather than local ones.
(a)
(b)
Figure 5.6 – Two horizontal slices extracted at different acquisition pixel pitches. In (a),
the pixel pitch is 6µm, and the in-focus lines are sharp and thick enough to be easily
identified by the neural network. In (b), the pixel pitch is 2µm, and the in-focus lines are
finer and more difficult to identify. However, their geometric formation, represented in
red as a conic, and can still be learned.
5.3.2
Loss Function and Hard Cases
Although the learned network yields reasonable results, it still has some limitations
that have to be addressed to significantly enhance model performance. The first limitation arises from the imbalanced nature of the problem formulation, leading the network to
128
5.3. Experimental Setup
consistently predict out-of-focus levels unless an appropriate loss function is used to correctly penalize the model during the training process. To illustrate this point, Figure 5.7
demonstrates the reconstructed geometry of the same scene with slight adjustments to
the α parameter in the used cross-entropy loss function, from 0.9 to 0.7. The readers can
observe from the figure that a lower value of α induces greater sparsity in the predicted
depth map than a higher value. This is due to inadequate penalization weights during the
model training which caused the model to predict more out-of-focus labels than it should
have. The alpha parameter can be manually defined by computing the mean percentage
of in-focus points per horizontal slice on the training set.
(a) α = 0.9
(b) α = 0.8
Figure 5.7 – Illustration of the impact of the loss function on the sparsity level. In (a),
the depth map is predicted from a test sample of the Piano scene when the network is
trained using an α parameter set to 0.8 in the loss function. In (b), the depth map is
shown when the α parameter is set to 0.9 in the loss function.
The second limitation of the method is the presence of difficult patterns that can either
cause holes in the predicted map or bad scene curvature estimation. To better illustrate
this second point, we illustrated some horizontal slice patterns that mostly appear in the
dataset and their predicted in-focus points using the H-seg method in Figure 5.8.
The first pattern illustrated in Figure 5.8(a) is relatively simple and appears when the
horizontal plane cuts the top or the bottom of the object in one or few points. In this
case, we view a double conic shape centered at the location of the points with a sharp in129

that the network gives reasonable results for the first pattern in 5.8(c) and the second

in 5.8(l). For the harder patterns, the network tends to overestimate the curvature of the

lines. In addition to missing scene points(highlighted by the red circle in the figure), this
result will appear as black holes in the predicted depth map. Here, we can assume that
the higher the number of intersection points, i.e. the in-focus points, on each horizontal
slice, the larger the estimation error.

given slice of size W × Nz , there are a total of W possible in-focus points per slice.
The readers can observe from the figure, that there is a relationship between these
two factors. The larger the number of in-focus points the greater the risk of misclassified
objects. This can be explained by the fact that as the number of in-focus points increases,
there will be a higher energy summation that can cause blurry in-focus lines that are
harder to retrieve by the model.

Partie I, Chapter 5 – H-seg : A Horizontal Reconstruction Volume Segmentation
5.3.3
Occlusion Areas
Another point that can affect the network performance is the presence of occult areas,
i.e. regions where two objects are near each other. In this case, the in-focus lines present
in the occult areas will be blurred and can not be retrieved correctly by the model.
To illustrate this phenomenon, we present an example in Figure 5.10, showcasing the
retrieved scene geometry at two different timestamps. In the first timestamp, the scene
objects are adequately spaced, resulting in a well-estimated scene geometry. However,
in the second timestamp, the hand of one object intersects with another, creating occult
areas. These areas exhibit a high level of sparsity in the predicted depth map, indicating
the network failure to accurately retrieve the scene geometry.
We can assume that when there is a sharp surface transition between two objects, the
network will struggle to retrieve the scene geometry, resulting in sparse areas. To confirm
this intuition we have reported in Figure 5.11 the level of sparsity and the mean SVI for
each scene of the datasets.
The readers can observe from the figure that the larger the SVI, the greater the level of
sparsity. This correlation is logical, as a larger SVI indicates significant surface variation
that commonly arises when there is proximity between objects. These areas lead to blurry
in-focus lines that the network fails to capture and thus result in higher sparsity levels.

The readers can observe from the table that the network GPU consumption is significantly lower than the one reported in Table 4.2 for the PatchNet method. This reduced
132
5.4. Performance on External Datasets
GPU demand can be attributed to the constraints imposed by the loss computation in
PatchNet, necessitating knowledge of all predicted focus values along the axial axis for a
given pixel to be computed. Consequently, the PatchNet method must process all images
computed along the axial axis, resulting in a 3D volume whose size increases with the
patch size and the axial axis sampling rate employed. In contrast, the H-seg model can
fulfill this constraint by processing a single 2D horizontal slice that spans the entire XZ
plane. For a neural network, the GPU requirement for processing a 2D image is lower
and increases relatively slowly with the image size in comparison to a 3D volume.
The provided table also demonstrates the network capability to handle large hologram
dimensions with a high axial axis sampling rate without imposing substantial demands
on GPU resources. For example, an 8K hologram with 1024 numerical reconstructions,
only requires 5GB of VRAM which is accessible for most modern GPU models.
The total computational time required to process a hologram of size H × W with an
axial axis discretized into Nz segments can be determined using the second table. This
involves multiplying the processing time for a single horizontal slice of size W × Nz by the
number of slices, which is equal to H. For instance, consider a hologram of dimensions
1024×1024 with 256 sampled axial distances. The computation time needed for processing
is approximately 1024 × 0.014 seconds, totaling roughly 14.336 seconds. This is merely
half the computation time required for the PatchNet model.
5.4
Performance on External Datasets
Beyond assessing the proposed method with the test set holograms, we extended our
evaluation to include external holographic samples. Our analysis encompasses a total
of nine holograms, comprising six CGH from the Interfere-I [138] and Interfere-III [139]
datasets, alongside three optically acquired holograms from the EmergIMG [140] dataset.
Table 5.3 contains metadata related to the recording of holograms, as provided by the
datasets.
The evaluated holograms are recorded from planar scenes and thus require a single
optimal focus distance per hologram. To adapt the proposed approaches from local to
global focus distance prediction, we first created a 3D reconstruction from each hologram,
considering that the maximum reconstruction zmax is equal to two times the scene ground
truth reconstruction. Then we uniformly sampled Nz (fixed to 256 in the experiments)
reconstruction distances using the following formula,

2
2
The obtained reconstruction volume is processed as follows for each method:
— For the GVLN method, the focus is evaluated globally on each numerical reconstruction of the reconstruction volume, resulting in an Nz focus curve.
— For the PatchNet model, the spatial resolution of the reconstruction volume is reduced from Nz × H × W × 3 to Nz × 256 × 256 × 3 by resizing each numerical
reconstruction. Then the downsampled reconstruction volume is fed to the network, and the predicted focus scores of size Nz × 256 × 256 are summed along the
spatial axis to obtain the final Nz focus curve.
— For the H-seg method, a single central horizontal slice of size Nz × W is extracted
at the middle of the reconstruction volume (with y equal to H/2) and cropped to
a fixed size of 1024 × 256 × 3. This is then fed to the network, and the predicted
focus level map of size 1024 × 256 is summed along the X-axis to obtain an Nz
focus curve.
For all methods, the maximum of the focus curve is selected to estimate ground reconstruction distance, and the L1 norm between the predicted value and the ground value is
chosen as an evaluation metric.
The obtained estimation errors are reported in Table 5.4, and some of the focus curves
with the EFI at the predicted focus distance are illustrated in Figures 5.12 and 5.13.
From the table, we can observe that the PatchNet method is the most precise followed
by the GVLN method. The H-seg method performs well on the colored Interfere-III

holograms but struggles to correctly estimate the optimal focus distance for the remaining
grayscale holograms, especially for the optically acquired ones.
This performance difference can be directly assessed from the obtained focus curves,
where a noisier focus curve without a clear peak often results in a poorly estimated optimal
focus distance that produces a blurred EFI. Among the evaluated methods, the PatchNet
method produces the most precise focus curve with a clear peak at the optimal focus plane
and a low area under the curve, indicating high model confidence.
The poor performance of the H-seg method can be explained by the produced diffraction pattern on the extracted horizontal slice illustrated in Figure 5.14. The reader can
observe that the holograms from the Interfere I and EmergIMG datasets do not exhibit
the usual diffraction patterns shaped in double conic that the model is trained to detect.
Only the colored CGHs from the Interfere III exhibit those patterns, which are less pronounced for the Shere1A hologram. Here, we can observe an expectation for the 2D Multi
that exhibited a double conic shape that the model correctly detected, thus predicting
the correct optimal focus distance.
The performance of the PatchNet and the GLVN methods is not affected by the hologram acquisition approach, as the features of an out-of-focus numerical reconstruction
are independent of whether the hologram is acquired optically or computer-generated. In
simple terms, an out-of-focus numerical reconstruction is blurry, with energy uniformly
spread across the spatial domain, contrary to an in-focus image with sharp and concentrated energy.
135
Partie I, Chapter 5 – H-seg : A Horizontal Reconstruction Volume Segmentation
5.5
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have proposed to estimate focus level horizontally rather than
vertically as is the case in the current methods and we introduce the H-seg model.
The conducted experiments have demonstrated that the proposed model has better
performance than the previously proposed methods, with lower GPU consumption and
faster inference time. However, this performance gain comes with some drawbacks that
can be summarized in two points. First, as the number of scene points per horizontal slice
increases, the target in-focus lines become blurry due to the summation of the energy
spread by each point. The blurriness of the in-focus line causes a drop in the network
performance, which can not be compensated with learned geometrical information about
the line formation due to the high point density. Second, the network produces poor
results at the occluded areas that usually appear when two objects are close or intersect
each other.
The network tends to have a higher performance when the horizontal plane intersects
the scene at a few points. In this case, the model can rely not only on the sharpness of the
in-focus lines but also on the geometrical information of the line formation provided by
the double conic shape. As the number of points increases, less discriminate geometrical
information could be extracted and the network could only rely on the sharpness of the
in-focus lines. These sharpness measures can be insufficient in the occult areas or at a
larger point density.
136
5.5. Conclusion

Chapter 6
S ELF S UPERVISED D EPTH E STIMATION
In the preceding chapters, we introduced two distinct learning-based methods for focus
estimation, both utilizing similar network architectures but differing in how the focus level
is estimated. The first method estimates focus in vertical planes, while the second one
does it in horizontal planes. These methods have demonstrated remarkable accuracy in
retrieving scene geometry, boasting mean estimation errors three to four times better than
traditional FM operators.
However, this performance enhancement is only possible by the open access to labeled
datasets for model training. In scenarios where such datasets are unavailable, one may
question if there exists an unsupervised method that could have similar performance as
the learning-based methods without requiring labeled data.
To address this concern, this chapter introduces a self-supervised methodology that
consolidates weak or imperfect estimates produced by various FM operators into a unified
estimate closer to the ground truth value.
Related Paper
— Madali, Nabil, Antonin Gilles, Patrick Gioia, and Luce Morin. 2023. "SelfSupervised Focus Measure Fusing for Depth Estimation from Computer-Generated
Holograms", 2023 IEEE International Conference on Image Processing (ICIP).
DFF Method

Partie I, Chapter 6 – Self Supervised Depth Estimation
6.1
Proposed Methodology
In this section, we will discuss the main components of the proposed method illustrated
in Figure 6.1.
6.1.1
Problem Formulation
The initial step of the proposed methodology involves obtaining weak estimates of the
scene geometry. These estimates can be obtained by applying the DM-DFF method using
different FM operators and patch sizes. Each of these extracted estimates can be viewed
as a corrupted version of the ground truth scene geometry, resulting from an unknown
degradation process.
Then, with this collection of corrupted or incomplete estimates {ŷi } of the ground
truth scene geometry y, the objective is to synthesize them into a single refined estimate
ŷ that closely approximates the ground truth value. To formulate this mathematically,
we will assume that the corrupted estimates are uniformly distributed within a cluster
around the ground truth value. Therefore, finding the ground truth value is analogous to
identifying the cluster value z that minimizes the average deviation from all the estimates
according to some loss function L. More formally,
arg min
z
1 X
L (ŷi , z) ,
N i
(6.1)
where N is the number of corrupted estimates. To solve this problem, a methodology
similar to the one proposed in [141] will be used. The proposed methodology illustrated
in Figure 6.2, simply considers that if a deep generator network fθ is used to approximate
the solution z in Eq. 6.1 given a single corrupted estimate (x∗ in the figure) as a target,
then during the network training path (illustrated in the figure by the arrow that connects
the point t0 and x∗ ), it will necessarily pass through a solution (t3 in the figure) that is
close to the ground truth value (xgt in the figure). This training path is due to the fact
that the network needs significantly more training time to capture high-frequency details
compared to low-frequency details. Therefore, if we stop the network training sufficiently
early, i.e. optimal stopping point, we can retrieve a smooth noise-free estimate that
correctly approximates the ground truth value.
In our case, we have N corrupted estimates that we can use to train the neural network
fθ . Each of these corrupted estimates ŷi will have a different degree and distribution of

Figure 6.2 – In (a), the theoretical explanation of the relationship between the neural
network fθ training path and the ground truth noise-free image xgt is provided. During
training, the network is supervised to approximate a noisy version of the ground truth

ground truth image are located in the subspace highlighted in gray in the figure. In (b), a

the degradation or noise level, which we can formalize as follows:
ŷi (x, y) = Filow (x, y) + Fihigh (x, y)
(6.2)
where Filow (x, y) and Fihigh (x, y) are the low and high frequency image components.
We can use this disparity in the distribution of the low and high-frequency components
to help with network training. To do that we will randomly sample a single corrupted
estimate from all the possible estimates at each training iteration, as suggested in the
paper, and use it as the network target. The network optimization can be formulated as
follows:
etθ = |fθ − x∗ |2 ,
(6.3)
∂etθ
,
(6.4)
∂θ
where θ are the network parameters, etθ is the estimation error between the network
prediction and the randomly sampled corrupted estimate x∗ from the set {ŷi } at t-ith
θt+1 = θt −
145
Partie I, Chapter 6 – Self Supervised Depth Estimation
iteration. After a certain number of iterations, the value fθ is expected to approximate
the ground truth value. In the paper [141], the authors have empirically defined the
number of iterations that should be used to efficiently denoise an image.
The main idea behind this training strategy is that if we fix a small area Ω in the
image spatial domain X × Y, we can argue that at least one of the corrupted images will
have this portion of the image noise-free, i.e., the high-frequency components are null
for all the pixels (x, y) in Ω. So if we use this image as the target for the network, it
will converge more rapidly to the optimal solution. Unfortunately, we do not have prior
knowledge about the level of degradation of each image, and we can only expect that
for any region Ω in the spatial domain, there is at least one image that has a greater
distribution of low-frequency components than high-frequency ones and can therefore be
denoised more easily by the network.
6.2
Experiments
To evaluate the proposed method we have used the same test set as for the previous
methods. For each test frame, the DM-DFF method is applied to extract scene depth map
estimates using the three previously selected FM operators (GLVN, CONT,GRAT) and
variable patch sizes in the set {5, 7, 9, 13, 17, 21}. To introduce more diverse estimates,
we include estimates obtained using the maximum and minimum as optimal focus plane
selection criteria, without employing the auto-switch method.
Then, the assembled encoder-decoder proposed in [141] for the inpainting task was
initialized randomly and trained for 20000 iterations. At each training iteration, the
network is supervised to minimize the ℓ2 distance between the produced estimate and a
randomly sampled corrupted estimate from the set that contains all the corrupt scene
geometry estimates. Each element of the set has a probability equal to N1 to be sampled,
where N is the cardinality of the set. The learning rate was initially set to 0.1, and an
exponential learning rate decay was employed to reduce it progressively by a factor of 0.8
every 100 iterations.
Finally, the output from the last iteration was compared to the ground truth depth
map using the MAE metric to evaluate the proposed method.
146

Table 6.1 – The obtained MAE between the ground truth and the predicted values using
different methods. The first, and second methods are colored green, and red, respectively.
The readers can observe from the table that combining weak estimates of the same
scene can produce significantly better results than when it is used separately. The obtained
performance gain is not constant and tends to vary according to the level of degradation
on the initial estimates of a particular scene. This can be explained by the fact, that when
the scene geometry is badly estimated, there will be large holes and missing surface areas
that can hardly be recovered during the training path. On the contrary, when the scene
geometry is initially well estimated, the network just has to fill holes or refine misclassified
areas using information across different corrupted estimates.
For a geometrical interpretation, we can view the initial estimates as random variables
that are distributed around the ground truth value inside a subspace χ. The size of this
subspace will vary according to the performance of the used FM operators. The lower
it is, the larger the subspace size. In sample words, the lower the mean MAE, the more
condensed the initial estimates around the ground truth value, and therefore the higher
the chance to pass close to it before converging to a corrupted estimate. On the other hand
the larger the MAE metric, the more scattered the initial estimates around the ground
truth value and the lower the chance to pass close to it during the training process.
Figure 6.3 provides an example of the intermediate output during the network training
process. Readers can observe that, during the training process, the network progressively
recovers scene details while simultaneously filling the small holes present in the initial
estimates. With increasing iterations, more scene details are recovered until reaching a
certain threshold where the produced result starts to diverge from the ground truth value,
leading to further degradation in the resulting depth map.
147
Partie I, Chapter 6 – Self Supervised Depth Estimation
The network capability to fill small holes can be attributed to the up-sampling layers in
the network architecture, which essentially interpolate values between input and output
tensors. Thus, even if a pixel lacks a depth value in the initial estimate, the network
assigns a mean local surface height based on surrounding pixel values. However, while the
network can remove small holes with only access to a single corrupted image, it struggles
to adequately fill larger areas without sufficient corrupted images approximating those
regions.
To illustrate this, we initially extracted the scene geometry of a poorly oriented Piano
scene using the GLVN operator with a patch size of 17×17. Subsequently, we evaluated the
proposed method solely using this corrupted image, and the obtained results are illustrated
in Figure 6.4. The readers can observe that the initial predicted depth contains large holes
located in the top part of the Piano, which is nearly perpendicular to the hologram plane.
This misalignment of the scene causes small in-focus areas that the operator could use to
accurately estimate the focus level and thus a large estimation error. The same hole is
also observed in the refined estimate, which indicates that the network is unable to fill the
large hole without access to other corrupted images providing partial or complete values
for the hole points.
Although the proposed method significantly improves the results of the DFF method,
the obtained results remain below the PatchNet learning-based approach. This is quite
logical as those methods are trained to produce the expected results and thus have a lower
MAE metric.
6.3
Conclusion
In the present chapter, we present a simple self-supervised method for combining weak
depth estimates of the same scene into a refined estimate that is closer to the ground truth
value.
The proposed method significantly improves the obtained results from the DFF method.
However, its high computation cost (approximately 20 minutes per frame) prevents it
from real-time use. In addition, the experimental results have demonstrated that if a
given scene part is not correctly retrieved in at least one of the training images, then the
network fails to retrieve it.
148
6.3. Conclusion

Part II
Phase Space Based Methods
151
Chapter 7
PSDFH: A P HASE -S PACE -BASED
D EPTH FROM H OLOGRAM E XTRACTION
M ETHOD
In the previous chapters, we explored various learning-based approaches for retrieving hologram scene geometry based on local focus change optimization. The proposed
methods have shown their ability to retrieve precise scene geometries with high accuracy
and lower inference time than non-learning-based methods. Nonetheless, the extensive reliance on numerical reconstruction renders these methods computationally intensive, not
only during the computation of the numerical reconstruction volume but also in terms of
storage requirements. To alleviate this drawback, we will discuss in the present chapter
the hologram Phase Space Representation (PSR), and how it can facilitate the design
of methods that match or exceed the performance of previously discussed approaches
while minimizing the computational overhead. The chapter only discusses 1D holograms
to facilitate the visualization of the results and the interpretability of the method. The
extension of the method to 2D holograms will be discussed in the following chapter.
Related Paper
— Madali, Nabil, Antonin Gilles, Patrick Gioia, and Luce Morin. 2023. "PSDFH:
A Phase-Space-Based Depth from Hologram Extraction Method" Applied Sciences
13, no. 4: 2463.
7.1
Phase Space Framework
Before delving into how we can use the hologram PSR for scene geometry retrieval,
we will discuss the working framework illustrated in Figure 7.1 that will be used in the
chapter.

7.1.2
Hologram PSR Binarization
The computed holographic PSR representation cannot be directly employed for scene
geometry retrieval, due to the lack of a direct correlation between scene points and the
PSR values. However, there exists an analytical formula, to be discussed later, that
establishes a connection between a candidate 2D point and the support of the obtained
hologram PSR. Hence, a binarization operation is applied to extract the support of the

computed PSR matrix. This operation involves identifying the minimal set of points
whose summation yields the same energy value as the original matrix. Formally: :

The figure 7.2 provides an overview of the different steps involved in point cloud PSR

is to extract an initial set that relates each sampled spatial location x to a frequency value
fxi (x). Then, values highlighted in green that are higher than the maximum diffraction
angle are eliminated. Finally, the remaining values are discretized by linearly interpolating
them to the sampled frequency values to obtain a 2D image representation of the mapping.
7.1.4
An Illustrative Example
To illustrate the working framework, we will consider a simple 2D scene presented in
Figure 7.3 that is composed of a single planer object.
The reader can observe from the figure that the resulting hologram PSR exhibits a
prominent non-zero band, which lacks uniform coloration despite the scene object having a distinct color. Following the binarization process, we can observe that the band
points are progressively recovered as the threshold value increases. However, with excessively high thresholds, false positive points situated at the image boundary within
high-frequency regions are also retrieved. The estimated support closely aligns with the
analytically computed support. However, there are some observed defects (highlighted by
the red circles in the figure) in the estimated support that could be rectified through the
application of morphological operators.
The key takeaway is the ability to map scene points into the phase space and obtain
the same PSR support shape as the one estimated from hologram PSR. If we could reverse
this operation, i.e. mapping from the estimated PSR support to the scene points. Then,
it would enable us to directly estimate the scene geometry from the hologram PSR.

Scene Geometry Retrieval

The proposed methodology is illustrated in Figure 7.4 and will be explained in detail in
the next sections.

binarization of the hologram PSR, as discussed previously. Following these steps, we
obtain an estimate of the holographic PSR support Ω∗ , which can be seen as the scene

7.2.3
Object PSR Segmentation
RoI Clusters
The processed scene may consist of multiple objects, and directly processing them
using the estimated scene PSR support may not be optimal due to the limited resolution
of the PSR and the overlap of the individual object support. Hence, a segmentation step
is necessary to isolate individual object PSR support from the initial estimated scene
support. In the present work, the segmentation operation will not be performed inside
the PSR, but will rather be done on the associated RoI in the 2D space.
To illustrate what motivated this choice, we illustrated in Figure 7.7 the scene PSR
support and the extracted RoI for a basic scene containing four planar objects. The
readers can observe that performing the segmentation operation inside the scene PSR
support could be challenging due to the presence of an intersection area where two scene
objects support intersect and thus produce an uncertainty regarding the exact shape of
each object support.
On the other hand, we can observe that well-spaced clusters shaped as diamonds
appear around each scene object on the extracted scene RoI. In the figure, we have
illustrated the obtained PSR support for one of the clusters, which results in one of the
bands in the intersection area in the scene PSR representation. The reader can observe
that when we remove the obtained PSR support from the scene PSR and we extract the
associated RoI, then, the cluster associated with the second band in the intersection area
disappears, as the highest support intersect score of cluster points is below one.
In the provided example, segmenting individual object PSR support is comparable
to extracting the PSR support of each extracted cluster in the 2D space. However,
in practical scenarios where there is significant overlap in scene object PSR support,
the resulting RoI tends to be noisier and may include multiple false positive clusters as
159

illustrated in Figure 7.8. Therefore, to execute the segmentation operation accurately,
it is essential to isolate only the true positive clusters before computing their respective
PSR support.
True Positive Clusters Selection
Finding the minimal true positive clusters that result in PSR support that is similar to
the scene PSR support can be formulated as the following iterative algorithm: Initially,
an empty placeholder support is initialized to zero. Subsequently, in each iteration, a
candidate cluster PSR support is temporarily incorporated into the placeholder cluster.
If the resulting support yields a greater intersection area with the scene PSR support
compared to other clusters, the candidate cluster is retained in the placeholder and suppressed otherwise. With the progression of iterations, the placeholder cluster converges
towards the scene PSR support. The algorithm stops when their intersection areas exceed
0.95% of the scene PSR support.

7.3
Object Position Estimation
Minimal Representation Lines
After decomposing the initial scene PSR support into individual object support, we
are still required to estimate the exact position of the scene points inside each retained
true positive cluster.
In the original contribution, we have proposed to reduce each cluster using a smaller
set of geometric primitives such as the lines as described in the Algorithm 1. This choice
was motivated by the fact that we can find inside each cluster a line that we have called
"the minimal representation line" that results in the same PSR support as the cluster.
The extracted set of minimal representation lines could be used as a coarse estimate of the
scene geometry. The used algorithm is similar to the one used for the true position clusters
selection with only replacing candidate clusters with candidate lines that we extracted
inside each selected cluster.

Minimal Representation Points
Another interesting geometrical primitives that could be used to reduce the cluster
are the points. To illustrate how we can perform this operation, we have illustrated in
Figure 7.9 the PSR support and the extract RoI for a simple scene composed of a single
planar object.
The reader can observe from the figure, that scene PSR support contains a single
band with a certain width and slope that gives information about the object size and
axial position respectively. The associated RoI has a diamond shape and contains the
scene objects in its centers. The shape of the extracted RoI provided us with information

Figure 7.9 – In the top right of the figure, we can observe the obtained RoI for a scene
composed of single planar objects slightly inclined with respect to the hologram with
hologram PSR support, illustrated in the top left of the figure. The PSR supports of two

on where the scene object is expected to be located. From this information, we can extract
spatial and axial search ranges that bound the location of the scene points along X and
Y axes. The reader can observe that as we get closer to the boundary points the axial
search range progressively diminishes until it reduces to a single point. To explain this
phenomenon we have drawn the PSR support of the boundary points on the original scene
support. The reader can observe that those points are represented by a support formed
by a single line that falls within the boundaries of the scene support. The slope of those
lines is controlled by the axial distance of the boundary points. So, a slight increase in the
axial distance will result in a line that partially intersects the scene support and therefore
intersection support below one.
This interesting aspect of the RoI can be used to iteratively retrieve the exact scene
point position. Initially, we can select one of the boundary points and we can remove
its contribution from the scene PSR support. The reader can see that the RoI shape
163
Partie II, Chapter 7 – PSDFH: A Phase-Space-Based Depth from Hologram Extraction Method
(a) Circle
(b) Inclined Line
(c) Triangle
(d) Three Planar Objects
Figure 7.10 – Illustration of the scene geometries used for the evaluation.
from the resulting PSR support will still be shaped as diamonds delimited but the point
that comes just after the one we have selected. The same operation could be performed
iteratively until all the points in the spatial search interval are proceeded.
In challenging scenarios where the scene object exhibits high curvature, simply removing the contribution of boundary points may result in no candidate points achieving
a score of one within the extracted RoI. To address this issue, we refrain from filtering
the computed scores to one. Instead, at each iteration for a given boundary point, we
select the axial distance where the computed intersection score is maximal. This approach
ensures that all points within the spatial search interval are processed.
7.4
Experiments
7.5
Experimental Setup
The proposed method is evaluated on six different scenes that we have illustrated in
Figure 7.10. The selected scenes are respectively composed of a circle, an inclined line, a
triangle, and three planar objects. The scene holograms are recorded using ASM method
with a size of 1024 × 1 and a pixel pitch of 1µm

7.6
Experimental Results
The computed holograms PSR and their respective supports are depicted in Figure 7.10. The reader can observe from the figure that each scene yields a distinct shape
(or footprint) of the PSR support. Among these shapes, the planar object produces simple
bands with widths and slopes determined by the objects size and axial distances, making
them relatively easy to identify. The triangle and the circle although they have different
curvatures, generate a double conic shape with a finer central region in the case of the
triangle. The inclined line results in a distinguishable triangular shape, contrasting with
the band produced by a horizontal line.
In addition to the PSR support, we can observe that the hologram PSR exhibits a
non-uniform intensity distribution, with certain areas displaying higher intensity values
than others. Manually interpreting this additional information could be tedious due to the
absence of an analytic relationship between scene point color and the obtained intensity
values.
The extracted RoI from each PSR representation, the computed minimal representation line, and points are illustrated in Figure 7.12. Here, as we have well-separated scene
165
Partie II, Chapter 7 – PSDFH: A Phase-Space-Based Depth from Hologram Extraction Method
objects, we will have a single cluster per scene object that we have illustrated in the figure.
The reader can observe from the figure that each object PSR support results in a
unique RoI shape (or footprint) in the 2D space. Among these shapes, we can observe
that the planar objects result in diamond shapes centered on the objects themselves. For
the curved object, we can observe an RoI shape that aligns with the object curvature and
tends to be finer in the upper part of the object that faces toward the hologram plane.
For example, we can observe that the upper parts of RoIs produced by the inclined line
and the circle are finer and closer to the object than the one produced by the triangle.
The obtained RoI can be directly used to delimit the spatial and the axial position of
the scene objects. They can also be further refined into a set of minimal representation
lines or points as illustrated in the second and third columns of the figure. The reader
can observe that for planar objects, the minimal representation line directly gives the
correct position of the scene objects. This is relatively logical as the refinement algorithm
searches inside the cluster for the line that best approximates the obtained object PSR
support. The minimal representation line tends to be less accurate for curved objects and
lines with a certain slope. For the curved object with a "C" form, we can observe that the
resulting minimal representation lines are the closers of the curve "C|" that connect the
two extremities of the curved object. For the inclined line "/", we can observe that the
selected lines are the piecewise horizontal projection of the line ”/” . In all the cases the
minimal representation lines give a correct approximation of the object position and can
be used for subsequent application.
By changing the used primitive from lines to points we can observe a better retrieval
of the objects curvature. However, the obtained results are noisier and lack precision. In
addition, the computation time passes from 12 seconds for refining a single RoI into lines
to over 10 minutes when refining using a set of points.
7.7
Conclusion
In the present chapter, we first introduced some basic notions about the hologram
PSR and the relationship that exists between its support and the 2D space. Then, we
used this relationship to design a simple algorithm for scene geometry retrieval.
The main idea of the proposed algorithm is that each scene object will result in a
distinct PSR support which itself results in a distinct RoI shape that delimits the spatial
and axial position of the scene object inside the 2D plane. Therefore, retrieving the
166
7.8. Phase Space As a Perspective Projection
scene geometry is similar to finding a minimal set of 2D points that results in the same
PSR support as the scene object. To solve this problem, we extracted simple geometrical
primitives from the obtained RoI and we can iteratively select the minimal set of those
primitives that results in a similar PSR support as a targeted one.
Our experimental findings indicate a successful approximation of relatively planar
scene objects. However, as the complexity of object geometry increases, our ability to
accurately approximate them diminishes.
In the next chapter, we will introduce a more elaborate method that instead of refining
the RoI tries to directly identify and classify the produced RoI shape to approximate the
scene geometry correctly.
Appendix
In this section, we will introduce additional notions about the hologram PSR that we
did not include in the main chapter.

The Mapping function is Eq. 7.8 can be reformulated as perspective projection in the

h

For a given scene, if we fix the camera position at (0, ξ) and we project all the scene
points into it. Then, it will theoretically result in a perspective projection of the scene
inside the image plane. To better illustrate this notion, we have drawn in Figure 7.13 the
obtained perspective projection on three camera centers for a simple scene composed of a
single planar object. The reader can observe that the obtained projection corresponds to
the row of the hologram PSR support. The locations of these rows are not random and
correspond to the location of the camera center. If we analyze the PSR support row-wise,
we can observe the motion of the scene across different camera centers that are placed
167
Partie II, Chapter 7 – PSDFH: A Phase-Space-Based Depth from Hologram Extraction Method
along the same axis. This notion is interesting because if we find matching points across
the different cameras we can perform a raytracing operation to retrieve the scene points.
Here, as the investigated scene contains a planar object, we can extract matching points
between two cameras simply by connecting the null index of consecutive rows in ascending
order. For example, in the figure we have illustrated in blue the resulting matching points
for the rightmost point of the scene. Given the matching points {x̂i } between the cameras
{ξi }, we can draw a set of lines that connect each scene point and its estimated projection
in the image as follows:
x = tan(asin(x̂i λ)).z + ξi
(7.14)
where λ is the hologram wavelength. Then we can intersect those lines to estimate the
point position. Finding matching points could be a tedious task when the investigated
object is highly curved and does not have a sufficient amount of texture variation to
distinguish its different parts.
In the literature, the image of PSR support is called the "epipolar image" and the blue
line is called an epipolar line.

at the extremity of the obtained and weak in the inner object part.

Chapter 8
PS-NET : E ND - TO -E ND P HASE S PACE
D EPTH E STIMATION
In the previous chapter, we discussed the hologram PSR and how we can use its support for scene geometry retrieval using a proposed iterative method. The main intuition
of the method was that any given scene object would have a specific PSR support shape,
which we have tried to reverse iteratively to retrieve the object geometry. Our experiments demonstrated the efficacy of this approach in accurately retrieving relatively planar
objects. However, it exhibited limitations in accurately estimating highly curved objects
and incurred significant computational costs due to its iterative nature.
To address these shortcomings, the current chapter introduces an end-to-end methodology that replaces the iterative algorithms of the prior method with neural networks.
This transformation notably enhances accuracy while reducing inference time, offering a
more efficient solution.
Related Paper
— Nabil Madali, Antonin Gilles, Patrick Gioia, and Luce Morin, "PS-NET: an endto-end phase space depth estimation approach for computer-generated holograms,"
Opt. Express 32, 2473-2489 (2024).
8.1
Proposed Methodology
Figure 8.1 provides an overview of the proposed method that compromises four steps.
In the first step, the hologram PSR is computed and binarized using a segmentation
neural network. In the second step, the obtained support is utilized to compute a 3D
occupancy volume that associates for each candidate point a PSR support intersection
score and additional color information derived from the hologram PSR. In the third step,
the obtained volume is sliced horizontally and a neural network is used to identify and
reverse the RoIs shapes present in each slice to estimate the scene geometry. Lastly, in the
173

Hologram PSR Computation and Binarisation
Given an input hologram denoted by H ∈ CL×L , the first step of our proposed methodology is to compute the 4D hologram PSR denoted C using a windowed STFT (see Section 1.5) as follows:
C[n1 , n2 , m1 , m2 ] =

174
8.2. Hologram PSR Computation and Binarisation
Adopting a neural network model over the previously used minimal energy support
method is driven by two key factors. First, the pre-trained network provides highly
accurate support with relatively few defects that have to be post-processed. Second, the
network ability to handle batches of images in a single inference pass drastically reduces
the computation time.
Figure 8.2 illustrates some selected lenslet images extracted from the Animals scene
along with their corresponding support. The reader can observe that these images closely
resemble those generated through a perspective projection of the scene onto virtual cameras positioned at (ξ1 , ξ2 ). However, they lack fine object details due to the limited
resolution of the PSR resulting from the sampling constraints [143] during the STFT
computation. In the figure, we have intentionally set the spatial index n2 to 32, while incrementally adjusting the value of n1 to portray the scene lateral motion from left to right
across various cameras. Similarly, by maintaining the spatial index n2 and incrementing
the values of n1 , one can observe the scene vertical motion from top to bottom. These
motion attributes of the lenslets image closely resemble what can be found in a light field
matrix.
The obtained binary support M̂ is re-arranged into a set, defined as follows
Ω̂(P) = {(x1 [n1 ], x2 [n2 ], ξ1 [m1 ], ξ2 [m2 ]) | 1M̂ [n1,n2,m1,m2]=1 }.

Network Architecture and Loss Function
The network architecture Gθ is the same as the original U-net architecture introduced
in [62] and is supervised to minimize the cross entropy loss between the predicted and the
ground truth PSR support as follows:
θ1∗ = min
θ1
XX
BCE(M̂ [n1 , n2 ], M [n1 , n2 ])
(8.6)
n1 n2
where BCE is the binary cross-entropy loss, θ1∗ are the optimal network weights, M̂ and M
are the predicted and ground truth 4D PSR support. Here, as the segmentation problem
is well-balanced, their no need for additional class weights in the loss function.
Alternative state-of-the-art neural architectures could also be used, but many of them
tend to be over-parametrized relative to the requirements of the task at hand and the resolution of the input images. The chosen network contains a sufficient number of parameters
175
Partie II, Chapter 8 – PS-NET : End-to-End Phase Space Depth Estimation
to ensure a high segmentation accuracy without falling into overfitting concerns.

8.3. 3D Occupancy Volume Extraction
8.3
3D Occupancy Volume Extraction
In the second step of the proposed method, a 3D occupancy volume is computed by
assigning for each candidate points p ∈ R3 a PSR support intersection score and additional
color information.
PSR Support Intersection Score
Similarly to the previous method, the PSR support intersection score is computed by
intersecting a candidate point PSR support with the scene PSR support. This can be
mathematically formulated as:
score(p) =
Ω(p)
T
Ω̂(P )
|Ω(p)|
(8.14)
where Ω(p) is the analytically computed support of the point p, Ω̂(P ) is the estimated
scene support in Eq. 8.5, and score(p) is the score that the point p belong to the scene.
Here, we chose not to threshold the obtained score to a single value to avoid potential degradation resulting from inadequate support segmentation. Instead, the network
receives the calculated score and conducts a filtration process based on the local neighborhood of each point.
Color Information
To aid the subsequent network in identifying and simplifying the RoIs, we consider
not only the non-nullity but also the value of the phase space coefficients affected by
each candidate 3D point. However, directly utilizing all those coefficients associated as
network input would lead to a significant GPU load due to the large number of coefficients.
Therefore, we retrain their mean values, which we refer to as the ’point color information’.
This information can be mathematically by:
color(p) =
X
1
c,
| Φ(p) | c∈Φ(p)
(8.15)
where Φ(p) are phase space coefficients associated with the point p that are given as:
Φ(p) = {C[n1 , n2 , m1 , m2 ] | (x1 [n1 ], x2 [n2 ], ξ1 [m1 ], ξ2 [m2 ]) ∈ Ω(p)}.
177
(8.16)
Partie II, Chapter 8 – PS-NET : End-to-End Phase Space Depth Estimation
The calculated color information acts as a complement to the support intersection score,
aiding in the discrimination between background and foreground points. For instance,
when considering a candidate point that truly belongs to the scene, its coefficients should
exhibit close similarity and are clustered around a mean value. Conversely, background
points may feature coefficients predominantly set to zero, or if non-zero, they tend to be
randomly dispersed, resulting in a more erratic color profile.
Figure 8.3 illustrates the mapping of two candidate points inside a set of extracted
lenslet images and their respective support. The reader can observe that when the candidate point truly belongs to the scene (highlighted in red) it will be mapped to a precise
location on the object surface across the images. On the other hand, when the point does
not belong to the scene, it will be randomly mapped along the object surface.
The randomness in the distribution of coefficients becomes especially noticeable within
a local area surrounding the background point, where even a slight change in the point
position results in a shift in color value, leading to a more noisy local color distribution.
Therefore, the network could use the local consistency of color information as a criterion
for scene object detection.
8.4
Shape-to-Shape Network
The direct processing of the 3D occupancy volume is impractical due to constraints
on GPU resources. Therefore, we have simplified the problem by decomposing the initial
volume into 2D horizontal slices extracted along the Y axis. Subsequently, a neural
network is employed to identify the RoIs shapes within each slice and reverse them to
retrieve the scene geometry. This process can be formalized mathematically as follows:
Sy = Tθ2 {Iy },
(8.17)
where Tθ2 is the used neural network,Sy is the score map that gives for each point (x, z)
the probability that it belong to the scene, and Iy is the extracted horizontal slice at a
height y given as:
Iy (x, z) = [score(p); color(p)] p = (x, y, z) ∈ R3 .
(8.18)
The computed scores maps are then used to extract a dense scene depth map, by
assigning for each point the axial distance where the highest score is obtained:
178
8.5. Refinement Network
d(x, y) = arg max {Sy (x, z)} ,
(8.19)
z
where d(x, y) is the scene depth map. In addition to the depth map, an EFI image can
also be computed by assigning the color information at the estimated axial distance as
follows:
EF I(x, y) = color(p),
p = (x, y, d(x, y)),
(8.20)
Figure 8.4 illustrates an example of a horizontal slice inputted into the network. The
reader can observe that the computed PSR support scores are notably high near the
object scene location, and gradually decrease as we move away from them. The obtained
scores can be thresholded to extract the RoIs that bound the object location and that
have shapes that encapsulated object curvature. Additionally, color information serves as
complementary data, aiding the network in discerning internal object parts, particularly
when dealing with oversized RoIs.
The primary function of network T is threefold: first, it filters the intersection score
and color information to extract a set of RoIs from each horizontal slice. This filtration
process employs convolution layers, leveraging not only pixel-level data but also contextual
information from the surroundings. Furthermore, the convolution layers aid in addressing
small gaps resulting from imperfect PSR support segmentation.
Secondly, the network is tasked with segmenting individual RoI shapes and learning
the intricate relationship between these shapes and the scene geometry. Lastly, the learned
relationship is used to estimate the scene geometry which is the closest possible to the
ground truth.
The used network architecture and the training procedure are the same as for the
H-seg method and are described in Section 5.1.1.
8.5
Refinement Network
Precisely retrieving the scene geometry from the RoIs shapes could be challenging,
especially for highly curved objects. Therefore, an additional refinement step is required
to refine the initial predicted coarse depth using local neighborhood information. The
refinement step is performed using a third neural network that takes as input the coarse
depth map and the associated EFI and produces a refined depth map. This can be
179
Partie II, Chapter 8 – PS-NET : End-to-End Phase Space Depth Estimation
mathematically formulated as:
dref = Rθ3 {[d, EF I]}
(8.21)
where Rθ3 is the used neural network, and [., .] is the concatenation operator. The learned
network is tasked with achieving two main objectives. First, it must address small gaps
that may arise in the coarse predicted depth map. Second, the network should improve
the initially predicted object curvature either using the color gradient of the EFI image
or by learning the object shape beforehand.
8.5.1
Network Architecture and Loss Function
The network architecture is a standard U-net architecture that takes as input the
coarse depth map and the EFI and produces a refined depth map. Recent network architectures that involve earlier or post features fusing [144] could potentially be utilized.
However, their effectiveness heavily relies on a substantial amount of data, which unfortunately is not available to us. The network Tθ3 is supervised to minimize the Mean
Squared Error (MSE) between the refined depth map and the ground truth depth map
as follows:

where θ3∗ are the optimal network parameters.
8.6
Experiments
The following section discusses the training and evaluation of the proposed method.
8.6.1
Experimental Setup
Dataset: The model was trained and evaluated using the same train and test split
as the previous method. The only modification made to the dataset was adjusting the
recording pixel pitch from 6µm to 1µm.
Hologram PSR Computation: For each frame of the dataset, we have computed
the hologram PSR using the dgt2 function from the LTFAT [145] library with a spatial
and frequential discretization set to 64. This will yield a 4D array of size 64 × 64 × 64 × 64
180
8.6. Experiments
for each color change, equating to a total of 4096 2D lenslet images that can be used
within the proposed approach.
Networks Training Procedure: The three neural networks are trained individually
and sequentially: first, the segmentation network; followed by the shape-to-shape network;
and finally, the refinement network.
For the segmentation network, 2D lenslet images are extracted from each training
frame and progressively fed into the network in a randomized order, employing a batch
size of 1024 and augmentation via horizontal and vertical flips. A total of 100 × 4 × 1080
iterations are used to optimize the network parameters.
The trained network is utilized to extract the hologram PSR supports that are used in
the computation of the 3D occupancy volume. Then, the computed volume is employed to
train the shape-to-shape network, using the same methodology as for the H-seg method.
After completing the network training, the network is deployed to predict a coarse
depth map and its associated EFI for each frame in the dataset. These initial predictions
are utilized to train the refinement network over 200 epochs using Stochastic Gradient
Descent (SGD) with cyclic learning rate decay ranging from 0.1 to 0.01. Additionally,
early stopping is implemented if the validation loss fails to decrease for 10 consecutive
epochs.
Evaluation Procedure: The shape-to-shape and the refinement networks are trained
a first time using ground truth hologram PSR support and the obtained results are evaluated using the MAE metric. Then a second time using the predicted hologram PSR
supports. This approach allows us to assess the impact of hologram PSR binarisation on
the final results.
Comparison Methods: The PatchNet and H-seg methods were retrained and evaluated using the dataset recorded at 1µm. Furthermore, we assessed the performance of
the GLVN operator using a patch size of 17 × 17.
8.6.2
Experimental Results

for the hologram binarization step. The first rows of the table present a comparison of

T for the minimal energy support method.

The reader can observe that as the threshold value increases from T=0.8 to T=1, it
leads to a decrease in the computed precision and an increase in the recall. This tradeoff is explained by a weaker criterion for positive instance selection at a high threshold
value, which results in more points labeled as foreground where only a portion of them are
true positive pixels. For example, when T = 1 we can observe that all the true positive
instances are correctly labeled. However, they only represent 37% of the labeled positive
instances.
The U-Net models do not require a predefined threshold value and can use the learned
convolution filters to find if a pixel should be labeled foreground or background. On
average the network captures 94% of all true positive instances, with 12% of false positive
predictions among all the predicted true positive instances.
Comparing the IoU metric between the two segmentation models offers deeper insights
into their respective performances in delineating segmented object boundaries. The first
model achieves an IoU score of 0.37, indicating a relatively low overlap between its predicted segmentation and the ground truth. This implies that while the model successfully
identifies some target objects, it struggles to accurately delineate their boundaries, resulting in a notable portion of misclassified pixels. In contrast, the second model demonstrates a substantially higher IoU score, reaching 0.83, indicating significantly improved
alignment between its predicted segmentation and the ground truth. This suggests that
the second model not only effectively identifies the target objects but also delineates their
boundaries with greater precision, resulting in a more substantial overlap with the ground
truth segmentation.
In addition to its accuracy, the U-Net has a ten times lower inference time than the
first method. This faster inference time can be explained by the GPU parallelization of
182
8.6. Experiments
the network convolution operations, and the ability to infer the results of multiple images
in a single network forward pass.
Figure 8.5 illustrate examples of hologram PSR supports obtained using both methods
at distinct spatial locations. The reader can observe from the figure that employing
a low threshold value yields sparse supports with few scene pixels that are accurately
labeled. As the threshold value increases, more points are identified as positive instances,
encompassing both true scene elements and false positive pixels. While a higher threshold
may result in only a few insignificant false positives in some instances, it can lead to a
significant number of false positives in others, severely degrading the mask quality. For
instance, a threshold of T = 1 suffices for the second spatial location but proves inadequate
for the first.
The degree of degradation can fluctuate across spatial locations, with more challenging scenarios often occurring at lateral positions where scenes typically reside in highfrequency regions.
Unlike the first method, the U-Net does not necessitate a predefined threshold; instead,
it is trained to predict accurate hologram PSR supports. The obtained PSR supports are
superior to those provided by the minimal energy support method, but they lack finer
details due to the lower input resolution.
Scene Retrieval Performance
Table 8.1a presents the MAE metric obtained for depth map prediction on the test set.
The readers can observe from the table that the numerical reconstruction-based methods
exhibit inferior performance compared to the proposed phase space method independently
of how the hologram PSR supports are produced. Among the evaluated techniques,
learning-based approaches (such as PatchNet and H-seg) consistently outperform nonlearning-based methods (like GLVN).
This performance disparity between the two categories of methods can be attributed
to two key factors. First, the rate of focus change is significantly faster at 1µm than at
6µm. Second, the presence of speckle noise in the computed numerical reconstructions.
To illustrate how these factors impact focus level estimation, Figure 8.6 illustrates
three consecutive numerical reconstructions obtained at 6µm and 1µm. The reader can
observe from the figure that when the pixel pitch is set to 6µm, the resulting numerical
reconstruction at the optimal focus plane exhibits sharp detail and is devoid of speckle
noise. Then, as we deviate from the optimal focus plane, speckle noise progressively

contaminates the image, causing a gradual loss of detail until the image becomes out of
focus.
Conversely, when the pixel pitch is reduced to 1µm, the numerical reconstruction at
the optimal focus plane is noisier with weaker details compared to its 6µm counterpart.
Subsequently, as we move away from the optimal focus plane, the level of speckle contamination intensifies causing a rapid degradation of image quality.
Training a focus estimation operator on a large pixel pitch is relatively straightforward, as the network simply needs to discern which numerical reconstruction has the
least speckle noise among the input images. In such cases, the network architecture does
not necessarily require additional temporal connections between numerical reconstructions to extract more nuanced information that could aid in handling challenging scenarios. However, as the pixel pitch increases, relying solely on the level of speckle-noise
becomes inadequate since even an in-focus image contains inherent noise. Consequently,
the network must extract additional insights into how noise levels vary across numerical
reconstructions, a task that necessitates temporal information.
These considerations also heavily influence the horizontal focus estimation utilized in
the H-seg model. As mentioned earlier in the manuscript, a smaller pixel pitch yields finer
in-focus lines that the H-seg model must accurately capture. However, solely relying on
local information from convolution layers may not suffice to extract these lines, requiring
the model to discern more global geometric features regarding line formation. Extracting
such features becomes particularly challenging in occluded areas or when continuous infocus lines span the entire X-axis.
The proposed approach offers two advantages for horizontal in-focus line detection
and extraction that are depicted in Figure 8.7. First, the network uses specified RoIs for
guidance rather than searching the entire horizontal slice. Second, the geometric features
184
8.6. Experiments
of the line formation are directly recorded inside the shape of the RoIs and can be directly
used for retrieving the in-focus lines.
Nevertheless, despite its superior problem formulation for low-pixel pitch scenarios,
the proposed method still faces limiting factors that have to be addressed. First, the
performance of the network is intricately linked to the quality of the hologram PSR
support binarization. The reader can observe from the provided table that utilizing the
ground truth supports significantly reduces the MAE by two to three times compared to
using predicted supports. This performance loss can only be attributed to the degradation
of the support intersection scores, which are the only component of the input that is
influenced by the quality of the predicted support.
To better illustrate the impact of support degradation on the obtained support intersection score, we provided in Figure 8.7 the obtained scores after randomly degrading the
ground truth support by removing α positive instances and introducing β false positive
instances. As the α value increases, the distinct boundary delineating the RoIs gradually diminishes. In addition, we can observe that the obtained scores are progressively
smoothed, and we hardly distinguish specific shapes that could be used to retrieve the
scene geometry. On the other hand, increasing the β value results in the emergence of
false positive points with scores similar to or higher than the scene points. Here, we
have intentionally fixed the α and β values across supports within the same scene, but
in practical scenarios, these values may vary across the supports and scenes. This can be
challenging because the network needs to learn to recognize the RoI despite changes in
its appearance caused by the random degradation.
Second, the network often struggles to accurately estimate highly curved objects within
large RoI, performing better when dealing with smaller RoI exhibiting reasonable object
curvature.
Figure 8.9 illustrates various RoI shapes encountered in the dataset, along with the
retrieved geometry obtained using the shape-to-shape network. The simplest shapes for
processing include triangles, diamonds, and hexagons, typically associated with objects
having low curvature or relatively planar surfaces. In these shapes, the network frequently
fails to capture the curvature inherent and often predicts a straight line connecting their
outermost points.
The most challenging shapes arise from objects with pronounced curves, spanning
significant portions of the X-axis. In such cases, the extracted shape lacks a definitive
form and tends to vary based on the curvature of the encapsulated object. This presents
185
Partie II, Chapter 8 – PS-NET : End-to-End Phase Space Depth Estimation
a significant challenge, as the network cannot learn a single output for each shape and
must instead approximate the object curvature for each input RoI shape. It usually fails
to do it and tends to provide a sparse approximation of their curvature.
The observed sparsity in the initial depth map estimates poses no significant challenge
and can be effectively managed by leveraging the progressive nature of RoI shapes along
the Y axis. In fact, when visually inspecting the extracted horizontal slices generated
during Y-axis traversal (from top-to-bottom or left-to-right scanning), we can observe
that the RoI dimensions gradually expand as illustrated in Figure 8.10. Consequently,
the network is anticipated to exhibit superior performance on horizontal slices extracted
at scene boundaries with small RoI shapes, while displaying reduced effectiveness on slices
extracted from the interior regions of the scene with large RoI shapes.
If we process the horizontal slices of a continuous shape sequentially and assume a certain degree of similarity between the depth estimates for two consecutive horizontal slices,
we can iteratively refine the coarse estimate obtained. This constraint could be incorporated either into the network architecture directly or through an additional refinement
process.
In the current study, the refinement network takes as input the initial predicted coarse
depth map alongside its associated EFI. This enables the network to incorporate three
key attributes: First, it can utilize the relationship among the estimated values across
horizontal slices. Second, it can exploit the color gradient of EFI to enhance local depth
consistency. Third, the network can learn a shape prior, enabling it to accurately refine
various object parts.
From the table, we can observe that the performance improvements following the
refinement process are notable either when predicting the depth maps using the ground
truth or the predicted support inputs. However, a performance gap persists between these
two cases. This discrepancy might be attributed to the presence of significant holes that
the network struggles to recover, or to the challenge of refining input depth maps that
contain high levels of noise.
Figure 8.11 illustrates the results achieved following the refinement process. The reader
can observe from the figure, that the learned network utilizes both local and global information to address gaps and smooth out areas with depth inconsistencies. This refinement
mechanism resembles information propagation, wherein the network transfers depth values from well-estimated regions to poorly estimated ones. However, the effectiveness of
this process remains constrained by the initial prediction and its associated EFI.
186
8.7. Conclusion
For instance, in the Woods scene, certain regions initially poorly estimated exhibit a
blurred appearance in the EFI. This blurring will provide inappropriate color information,
which fails to effectively guide depth value propagation by the network. This limitation
becomes particularly apparent when dealing with occluded objects positioned adjacent to
one another. In such scenarios, the computed ac! (ac!) lacks sharp separation boundaries,
therefore making it difficult to properly separate the two objects in the resulting depth
map.
8.6.3
Computation time
The support binarisation network takes 5.3 seconds to infer all the support from a given
hologram and requires 159MB of VRAM. The computation of phase space intersection
scores and color requires about 2.6 minutes.
The shape-to-shape network has the same computation time and GPU consumption
as the H-seg method given in Table 5.2. The refinement network takes 0.8 to complete
and requires 771MB of VRAM.
8.7
Conclusion
In this chapter, we introduced a novel end-to-end approach for retrieving scene geometry from the hologram phase space representation. The main intuition of the proposed
method is that each scene object will have a distinct shape in the hologram PSR support,
which can be mapped back to the 3D space and reversed using the shape-to-shape network
to retrieve the object geometry.
The conducted experiments have demonstrated that the obtained shapes could be
reversed with high accuracy for relatively planar or low-curvature objects. However,
challenges arise with highly curved objects, necessitating an additional refinement step to
enhance initial geometry predictions.
Compared to previous numerical reconstruction-based methods, our approach yields
superior results with shorter inference times. This performance disparity stems from the
pre-localization of scene objects provided by the RoI and the uniqueness of the obtained
shape that could be learned and used for geometry retrieval.
A key limitation lies in the dependency on the hologram PSR support binarization,
which significantly impacts the obtained results. Inconsistent binarization performance
187
Partie II, Chapter 8 – PS-NET : End-to-End Phase Space Depth Estimation
during inference, compared to training, may cause the shape-to-shape network to inaccurately estimate scene geometry due to sample variations between the training and test
set.
Optimal binarization is typically achieved on lenslet images from central views and
progressively degrades as we approach the later views due to the higher energy disparity
inside the lenslet images. Thus, restricting PSR support intersection score computation
to central views may enhance accuracy.
Another limitation of the proposed methods is the lack of proper handling of object
occlusion inside the lenslet images. The occlusion can be observable as we move from the
central view to the lateral view, where objects that are far away from the hologram plane
will be occluded by objects that are closer to the hologram plane. This is problematic
because we will have a large number of candidates that will be labeled true positive
candidates because they have a phase space support that perfectly intersects the scene
support but does not perfectly intersect the support of the individual scene objects. In
simple terms, the support intersection score of a point could be higher if we compare it
to the scene support, but will be lower compared to the maximum that we can obtain if
we compare it to the individual scene object support. This limitation could be handled
by segmenting individual object scene support and extracting a set of candidate points
for each object independently.
Future efforts will focus on eliminating the binarization step to streamline hologram
PSR processing. This may entail incorporating additional geometrical and epipolar constraints that establish relationships among the various lenslet images.
188
8.7. Conclusion

Part III
Application of Proposed Methods
For Optical Flow Estimation
197
Chapter 9
A COMPARATIVE REVIEW OF OPTICAL
FLOW ESTIMATION METHODS FOR
COMPUTER - GENERATED HOLOGRAMS
In the previous chapters, we proposed different approaches for scene geometry retrieval
that can extract a 3D RGB-D scene representation from an input hologram. During the
conducted experiments we mainly focused on the produced errors within individual frames
inside an extensive holographic video sequence. However, limited attention has been given
to understanding the propagation of these errors across the holographic frames. This oversight is significant, particularly when using the produced RGB-D video representations
to precisely estimate the 3D scene motion vectors over the video sequence.
In this chapter, we will introduce a two step methodology for 3D optical flow estimation
that is based on the retrieved RGB-D video representations. Then, we will extensively
discussed how the precision of the estimated optical flow vectors is affected by the quality
of the retrieved scene geometry.
9.1
Problem Formulation
Consider two consecutive holograms, denoted as Ht and Ht+1 , captured before and
after a certain scene motion that we aim to retrieve. Solving this task directly with
traditional optical flow algorithms from the input holograms can be challenging due to
two primary reasons. First, the acquired holograms do not result from a straightforward
perspective projection of the scene onto the hologram planes. Consequently, there will be
no clear spatial localization of scene objects inside the hologram planes that would ensure
the brightness constancy across holograms. Second, our objective extends beyond merely
retrieving spatial motion along the X and Y axes. In fact, only motion compensating for
the spatial motion without considering the scene axial motion would lead to a substantial
198
9.1. Problem Formulation
residual error between the predicted and actual holograms at time t + 1.

(𝑚𝑥 , 𝑚𝑦 , 𝑚𝑧 )
RGB
