Figure 9.1 – Illustration of the different steps involved in the proposed method.
These additional axial motion vectors can not directly be computed from the hologram
and require the knowledge of the scene geometry. To efficiently compute the scene 3D
motion vectors, we proposed a two-step methodology illustrated in Figure 9.1.
In the first step, the RGB-D scene representations are extracted from the input holograms at the consecutive timestamp t and t + 1 as follows:
RGBt , DT = Ψ(Ht ),
(9.1)
RGBt+1 , Dt+1 = Ψ(Ht+1 ),
(9.2)
where Ψ is one of the previously proposed methods for scene geometry retrieval. The
obtained RGB and depth map represent a simple perspective projection of the scene,
providing both the intensity and depth information of the projected scene points. In the
second step, the RGB images are utilized to compute the scene spatial motion using a
traditional optical flow algorithm,
mx , my = A (RGBt , RGBt+1 ) ,
(9.3)
where A is the used optical flow algorithm. Then, the obtained motion vectors are employed to motion-compensate the depth information to the timestamp t + 1 and the result
is subtracted from the actual depth map to derive the axial motion vectors,
mz (x, y) = Dt+1 (x, y) − Dt (x + mx , y + my ).
(9.4)
The computed vectors (mx , my , mz ) yield an estimation of the scene 3D optical flow
199
Partie III, Chapter 9 – A comparative review of optical flow estimation methods for
computer-generated holograms
between the timestamps t and t + 1.
9.1.1
An Illustrative Example
Figure 9.2 presents an illustrative example showcasing the application of the proposed
method for motion estimation, using a dice object as an example. Upon visual inspection of the computed holograms, we can observe that the object localization within the
hologram plane is not distinctly clear. The energy emanating from the object is dispersed
across the hologram, lacking concentration in any specific region. Furthermore, it is noticeable that as the object approaches the hologram plane, the area over which its energy
is spread increases proportionally. For example, the energy at time t is more concentrated
than at time t + 1, meaning that the object is further from the hologram plane.
In this particular case, applying a traditional optical flow algorithm directly on the
hologram would be inefficient, given the absence of distinct texture information that could
be matched across the holograms. The extracted RGB images permit the recovery of the
spatial localization of the dice object inside the hologram plane and can be used to identify
its spatial motion from timestamp t to t + 1. The obtained spatial motion vectors could
be used to motion-compensate the depth map to timestamp t + 1, and the results could
be subtracted to extract the axial motion vectors.

9.2
Experiments
This section describes the conducted experiments to evaluate and validate the proposed
approach.
200
9.2. Experiments
9.2.1
Experimental Setup
Dataset: The method is evaluated using the same dataset previously employed to
assess other methods. The only change that has been made is to change the training and
test split to have a continuous sequence of frames that can be used to evaluate the optical
flow algorithms. The training split is composed of the first 200 frames of the Piano, Table,
Woods, and Cars scenes. The remaining 100 frames, in addition to the 300 frames from
the Animals and Dices scenes are used in the test set.
Scene Geometry Retrieval Algorithms: Three different approaches have been
evaluated for scene geometry retrieval. First, the DFF method using a patch size of
17 × 17 and the GLVN operator. Second, the PatchNet method using a patch size of
32 × 32. Third, the H-seg method supervised using the cross-entropy loss with class
weights set to α = 0.9.
Optical Flow Algorithms: Three SOTA learning based optical flow method listed
in Table 9.1 have been evaluated. All the used models have been pre-trainned on the MPI
Sintel Dataset [146], and the used weights have been grabbed from the official author
Github respiratory. In addition to a simple block matching algorithm employing a block
size of 16 × 16 and MAD as the matching criteria.
Abbr.
GMA [147]
GMFlow [148]
FlowFormer [149]
BM
Paper
Learning to Estimate Hidden Motions with Global Motion Aggregation
Unifying Flow, Stereo and Depth Estimation
A Transformer Architecture and Its Masked Cost Volume Autoencoding for Optical Flow
Block-matching algorithm
Table 9.1 – Abbreviations of the optical flow methods used in experiments.
Evaluation Procedure: The evaluation of the proposed approach is structured into
two main components. Initially, we conduct a qualitative and visual analysis of the errors
generated during scene geometry retrieval. The qualitative analysis is performed using
the MAE metric defined as follows:
M AE =
X
1
|D(x, y) − D̂(x, y)|
|X × Y| x,y∈X ×Y
(9.5)
where D and D̂ are the ground truth and the predicted depth maps respectively.
Subsequently, various optical flow methods are employed on different qualities of retrieved geometries to examine their influence on motion flow vector estimation within the
scene.
201
Partie III, Chapter 9 – A comparative review of optical flow estimation methods for
computer-generated holograms
For a given quality, the 3D flow field vectors are evaluated using the Average Endpoint
Error (AEE), which is defined as follows:
AEE(mx , my ) =
q
X
1
(mx (i, j) − m̂x (i, j))2 + (my (i, j) − m̂y (i, j))2 ,
|X × Y| (i,j)∈X ×Y
(9.6)
where mx , my and m̂x , m̂y are the ground truth and the predicted motion vectors along
the X and Y direction respectively.
Here the ground truth motion vectors are predicted using the ground truth RGB-D
format provided in the dataset. The rigid body transform which is associated with each
frame, can not be used to estimate ground truth motion vectors due to the aperture
problem [150].
In addition, to the error of the spatial motion vectors, the axial motion vectors are
also evaluated as follows:
AEE(mz ) =
X
1
|mz (x, y) − m̂z (x, y)|
|X × Y| (x,y)X ×Y
(9.7)
where mz , m̂z are the ground truth and the predicted motion vectors. Here, the evaluation
is performed separately to better understand the error propagation in both the spatial
and the axial domain.
9.2.2
Scene Geometry Retrieval Quality Evaluation
Figures 9.3 and 9.4 provides the obtained MAD metric for each evaluated holographic
video sequence.
From the figure, three key observations emerge. First, regardless of the employed
method or the analyzed scene, the obtained performances are not constant but fluctuate from one frame to the other. Second, the observed fluctuations in the obtained
performances are similar across the evaluated methods. The main difference between
them is that the learning-based method produces a lower estimation error than the nonlearning-based methods. The first and the second observations combined suggest that
while all methods fail with challenging scenarios inherent to certain scene orientations,
the learning-based methods better handle simple cases and those result in lower estimation
error. Third, a notable disparity in performance fluctuation exists between the validation
and test sets. This phenomenon can be caused by similar texture in the validation and
202
9.2. Experiments
the training set that causes an estimation bias in the network prediction. Conversely, the
absence of such similarity in the test set leads to more consistent results across varying
textures, devoid of any bias introduced by prior knowledge.
The PatchNet exhibits superior performance than the H-seg and GLVN methods,
maintaining stable estimation performance across frames. The diminished generalization
capacity of the H-seg method likely results in the change that has been made in the
training and test sets split. Notably, the validation set comprises the last 100 frames of
each holographic video sequence, presenting distinct horizontal patterns not encountered
during training. While employing horizontal and vertical flipping can enhance model
generalization to some extent, it remains insufficient to cover the vast possibilities of the
input space. For instance, flipping a curve horizontally and vertically can only generate
two additional patterns. An additional data augmentation method such as the random
shifts may lead to additional performance gains of the learned model.
To better investigate the relationship between the observed oscillation in the MAD
curve and the scene orientation, we have provided in the second and third columns of the
figure, the computed SVI and MIA metrics using a patch of size 32 × 32.
As stated earlier in the manuscript the SVI metric provides the local structure variation
of the scene geometry. The highest it is the lower the intersection area (which is given
by the MIA) between the object surface and the planar patch used in the GLVN and the
PatchNet methods.
The relationship between these two metrics can be observed from the provided figure,
where the peak(or valley) in the SVI has its counterpart valleys (or peaks) in the MIA.
However, it is important to note that the presented values are the means computed from
the analyzed frames, and thus the observed relationship is not perfectly linear.
The reader can observe the oscillations in the MAD curve are correlated with the
obtained SVI metric, where the highest it is the lower the obtained performance. This is
quite logical as high SVI means a high local structure variation which is hard to retrieve
using the proposed methods. The correlation between the MAD and the SVI is more
visible for the learning-based method and is less pronounced for the non-learning-based
method.
A particular case where this correlation is not visible is for the Piano scene, where two
peaks are observed in the MAD curve but only one of them is correlated with a high SVI.
This behavior could be explained by the shape of the scene which is composed of two parts
that are connected at certain angles and will be oriented differently as the scene moves.
203
Partie III, Chapter 9 – A comparative review of optical flow estimation methods for
computer-generated holograms
To better illustrate this, we have provided in Figure 9.5 the obtained scene geometry at
the two peaks of the MAD curve.
The reader can observe that in the areas where there is a high SVI, we can observe
that the network fails to correctly estimate the scene geometry and produces holes that
are visible as speckle noise in the EFI. This bad estimation performance is due to the low
intersection area between the planar patch and the object surface that the model could
not retrieve.
At the second peak (the second row of the figure), we can observe that the scene is
almost facing the hologram plane which results in low SVI and high MIA metrics. The
only part of the scene that has a high SVI is the top part which has a certain incline from
the hologram plane and is relatively fine compared to the surface of the planar patch. In
this area, we can observe that the network fails to retrieve the correct scene geometry are
produces holes in the depth maps.
As there are a lower number of high SVI areas compared to low SVI ones, their mean
values produce valleys where a peak is expected to explain the second peak in the MAD
curve.
Another point that could explain the observed oscillations in the MAD curve is the
presence of occult areas in certain frames that the network fails to handle. To illustrate
that, we provided in Figure 9.6 the retrieved scene geometries at the peaks of the Table
and the Woods curve. The Woods scene is facing toward the hologram plane. However,
the different parts of the scenes are not completely flat as extend along the axial axis.
For example, the upper part of the palm tree on the right or the middle of the tree on
the left. In those areas, we can observe a high SVI and low MIA which results in bad
estimation error and speckle noise in the EFI. In addition, we can observe from the EFI a
high speckle noise density when two objects overlap at the intersection of the tree objects
on the right and at the intersection of the tiny house on the third tree in the middle. This
indicates that the network is unable to separate the two objects boundaries.
The same analysis can be observed in the Table scene, where there is a high speckle
noise density when two chairs intersect, or when a chair intersects the table. The occult
areas do not occur on all frames and will depend on the acquisition angle and the initial
space between the scene objects.
The previously obtained MAD error curves are computed on each frame separately and
thus do not provide information about the temporal degradation that appears inside the
video sequence, i.e. how a retrieved surface at timestamp t degrades at timestamp t + 1.
204
9.2. Experiments
To better analyze this point, we have reported in Figure 9.7 the local MAD difference
between two consecutive frames using no overlapped spatial patch decomposition of size
32 × 32.
This error shows how the model performance changes between two consecutive frames
and can be formulated as follows:
Et = ϵt − ϵt−1 ,
(9.8)
where ϵt , ϵt−1 are the estimation errors at timestamps t and t − 1 defined for a given
timestamp t as:
ϵt = St − Ŝt ,
(9.9)
where S and Ŝt are the ground truth and the retrieved scene geometry at time t. The
larger the temporal error Et , the lower the model performance consistency between two
consecutive time frames. In simple words, a large temporal error means that the retrieved
geometry at given timestamps will heavily deviate from the one retrieved geometry at
the subsequent timestamps. This will be transcribed in the extracted EFIs as a varying
degree in the speckle-noise contamination, i.e. one EFI will be noisier than the other and
therefore they can be hardly matched together.
The reader can observe from the figure that for the PatchNet model, the obtained
temporal error is concentrated around zero with some peaks that appear at certain locations. This indicates that the model performance does not vary much between two
consecutive frames, expected for some harder cases, where the model fails to have a consistent performance between the two timestamps. On the other hand, the obtained error
for the GLVN model is nosier and oscillates between a lower and higher band, indicating
the randomness in the model performance between two consecutive frames.
To analyze some hard cases that the model may fail on, we have illustrated in Figure 9.8
some extracted surfaces and their associated EFI at selected peaks in the PatchNet curve.
From the obtained figure we can distinguish four distinct cases in which the model may
fail to have consistent performance. In the first case, illustrated in the first and second
rows of the figure, we can observe that the investigated surface contains some black area
that does have not null depth values. These black areas are considered by the model
as background points and their positions are predicted at random with relatively low
confidence. In the second case illustrated in the third row of the figure, we can observe
that the model fails to correctly the scene surface in both timestamps due to the high
205
Partie III, Chapter 9 – A comparative review of optical flow estimation methods for
computer-generated holograms
local surface variation. In the third case illustrated in the fourth row, we can observe
that the model correctly estimates the scene surface at the first timestamp, but fails to
retrieve the surface at the subsequent. The reader can observe that the scene surface has
changed significantly between the two timestamps, and the network fails to capture this
surface transition. In the fourth case illustrated in the last row, the investigated surface
contains occult objects that intersect each other. The reader can observe that for the
two timestamps, the model has only retrieved the surface of the first object and fails to
retrieve the surface of the second object.
In all cases, we can observe that associated surface textures are relatively similar.
However, they contain some mismatched areas that are usually filled with speckle noise.
In practice, the presence of random noise is not problematic for modern optical flow
algorithms that rely on feature vectors extracted from CNN network. The extracted
feature vectors from the two timestamps are expected to be similar when the level of the
temporal error between the two timestamps is low. The extracted vectors may diverge, if
the temporal error is high or when the degradation is spread on the entire spatial domain.
In simple terms, the matching step in the optical flow algorithm may fail to match two
images if the two images do not closely resemble due to the varying level of speckle-noise
degradation caused by bad scene geometry retrieval.
Interestingly the hard cases can be detected using the model prediction confidence
score. In fact, as illustrated in Figure 9.9, the lower the model confidence, the higher
the estimation error. The reader can observe from the figure that the model confidence
score is usually over 0.8. However, there exist some not negligible areas where the model
has a really low estimation confidence which results in high estimation error. Figure 9.10
illustrates some extracted depth maps, amplitude maps, and their associated confidence
scores.
The reader can observe that the obtained depth maps contain some degradation that
can be visually assessed by looking at the speckle noise contamination in the associated
amplitude maps. Filtring the bad estimated areas using visual examination is not possible
in real-time processing and a more elaborate method has to be defined. In this particular
case, we can observe that the model confidence score can directly be used to assess the
quality of the retrieved geometry and can be filtered to extract the bad estimated areas.
Here, we have selected a threshold value set to 0.3 and we have extracted the area that
falls below this value in the mask illustrated in the last columns of the figure. The readers
can observe that the badly estimated areas are correctly labeled and they encompass all
206
9.2. Experiments
the parts of the amplitude image with high speckle noise contamination.
The identification and the filtration of badly estimated areas may be necessary for a
supplementary refinement step that is used to enhance the quality of the initially retrieved
scene geometry.

From the table, we can observe that independently of the investigated scene and the
used optical flow method, the quality of the retrieved scene geometry greatly impacts
the estimated optical flow vectors. The reader can observe that the PatchNet model
consistently produces lower optical flow estimation error with a factor of two to three
than the GLVN model. For a given optical flow model, the tradeoff between the retrieved
scene geometry and the estimated optical flow is particularly visible across the evaluated
scene. For example, we can observe that the committed error is Table scene which contains
many frames with high local structure variation that the optical flow error is higher than
the other scene independently of the used method for scene geometry retrieval. Here, we
can not compare the optical flow methods between them as we do not have access to the
ground truth optical flow vectors. However, we can notice that the learning-based methods
have a similar error magnitude which is lower than the non-learning-based methods. This
207
Partie III, Chapter 9 – A comparative review of optical flow estimation methods for
computer-generated holograms
could be explained by the use of a search interval in the block-matching method which
is larger than the magnitude of the optical flow vectors present in the training set of the
learning-based methods.
To better analyze the relationship between the scene geometry retrieval and the predicted optical flow, we have selected the Flowformer optical flow method and we have
plotted in Figure 9.11 the produced error for the scene geometry retrieval and the optical
flow estimation for the Table scene. The reader can observe that the curve of the optical
flow error closely matches the one of the scene geometry retrieval, where bad retrieved
scene geometry often leads to poor quality of the optical flow vectors.
Although this figure highlights the relationship between the scene geometry retrieval
and the optical flow estimation, it is not a localized metric that describes how local degradation impacts the optical flow estimation around a given point. To better illustrate the
notion of locality, we have plotted the model prediction confidence that gives local information on the quality of the retrieved geometry with respect to the produced optical
flow error. The reader can observe that the higher the model confidence, the lower the
optical flow error. This is relatively intuitive as low model confidence usually means a
badly retrieved geometry, and thus a bad quality in optical flow estimation. The confidence score can be filtered in order to distinguish inside a local area the bad estimated
points which are considered as noise, and the correctly estimated points. Here we have
set a threshold value to 0.3 and we have plotted the density of noisy points inside local
areas of size 32 × 32 with respect to the optical flow error. The reader can observe that
the higher the density of the noisy points, the larger the estimation error. This behavior
can be explained by the degradation of the extracted feature vectors that diverge at the
consecutive timestamps as the noise level increases. Besides the density of the noise, the
noise spreading across the spatial domain is also important. The reader can observe from
the figure that the more the noise is spread in the space, the larger the optical flow error.
This is due to the fact, that a concentred noise will only degrade part of the estimated
optical flow, contrary to a spread noise that will degrade the whole optical flow estimation.
To better illustrate those notions, we have extracted in Figure 9.12 some patches on
consecutive amplitude maps with their associated confidence scores, and the magnitude of
the optical flow error. From the figure, we can observe that lower confidence scores usually
indicated a badly retrieved scene geometry and therefore speckle noise in the extracted
amplitude maps.
208
9.2. Experiments
The reader can observe from that the optical flow error is usually distributed according to the density and the spreading of the speckle noise distribution. The higher the
noise density and the more it is spread, the larger the estimation error. On the contrary,
the lower the noise density and the more it is concentrated, the better the optical flow
estimation. For example, in the first row of the figure, the speckle noise density is concentrated in a single area, and respectively the optical flow error is also concentrated in
this specific area. In the remaining row of the figure, we can observe more spread speckle
noise distribution, which causes a non-uniform distribution of the optical flow error.

Computation Time
Table 9.4 gives the compaction time of the evaluated optical flow method on two
consecutive frames. The reader can observe that the FlowFormer model has the longest
inference time among the deep optical flow methods followed by the BM algorithm. The
processing of a hologram video sequence containing 100 frames takes approximately 3.3
minutes. However, this inference time could be significantly reduced by supplying all
holographic frames of the video in a single batch to the network, provided there is a
sufficient amount of accessible computational resources.

9.3
Conclusion
In the present chapter, we have introduced a two-step methodology for 3D optical
flow estimation from a pair of consecutive holographic frames. The proposed method
first retrieves an RGB-D scene representation from the input holograms. Then, use the
obtained results to compute the scene 3D motion vectors along the three principle axes.
The conducted experiments have demonstrated that there is a clear relationship between the quality of the retrieved scene geometry and the precision of estimated optical
flow vectors. The higher the level of degradation in the retrieved scene geometry, the less
precise the estimated optical flow vectors.
The degradation in the retrieved scene geometry appears as speckle noise in the extracted RGB image with a density and distribution that vary from one frame to the
other. The randomness in the speckle noise distribution between two consecutive frames
can highly disturb the optical flow algorithm, as the same texture could be degraded at
different degrees between the consecutive timeframes.
Although we have highlighted how the level of degradation impacts the precision of
optical flow estimation, we still have to investigate how a certain level of degradation can
affect the motion estimation and the compression performance.

C ONCLUSIONS AND P ERSPECTIVES
9.4
Conclusions
In the first part of this thesis, we have introduced several methodologies for precise
holographic scene geometry retrieval. These methodologies can be categorized into two
distinct groups.
— The numerical reconstruction-based method uses the focus level of a given
numerical at a given axial distance to approximate its offset for the targeted scene
geometry. These methods consist of three key steps. First, the input hologram is
numerically reconstructed at evenly spaced axial distances. Then, the focus level
is determined either through a manually defined mathematical operator or via a
trained neural network. Finally, the scene geometry is extracted by identifying the
optical focus distance using predefined criteria. An additional refinement step of
the initially retrieved geometry can be necessary for high-precision applications
— The phase space methods rely on the shape, or footprint, of the scene PSR to
approximate its geometry. This approach involves three primary steps. First, the
hologram PSR is computed and binarized using either a learning-based or nonlearning-based method. Subsequently, a set of candidates that likely belong to the
scenes are extracted based on geometrical constraints. Finally, the initial set of
extracted candidates is reduced to obtain a coarse estimate of the scene.
Then, in the second part of the thesis, we discussed how the proposed methods can
be employed to design a two-stage approach for determining the 3D scene motion within
holographic video sequences.
Overall, we have observed that the scene geometry can be precisely estimated from
the input hologram. However, the obtained results are not perfect and contain varying
estimation artifacts with types that vary according to the evaluated method. Those
artifacts are usually located in an area where there is a high variation of the targeted
scene geometry and can hardly be corrected without additional shapes prior.
Directly using the estimated scene geometry without undergoing a refinement process,
can significantly degrade the accuracy of the computed motion vectors. Indeed, the
221
quality of the retrieved scene geometry can change from one frame to the other, and this
randomness can disturb the texture matching in the optical flow estimation algorithms.
The conducted experiments have clearly outlined the relationship between the quality of
the retrieved scene and the precision of the motion vectors. Although this relationship
can be assessed, it remains unclear how the level and the spreading of the degradation
affect the motion estimation.
9.5
Limitations and future works
The limitation and evolution perspective will be discussed separately for each group
of methods. Then, a common practical limitation will discussed.
9.5.1
Numerical reconstruction-based method
The numerical reconstruction-based methods are highly precise but their practical
application is limited by three key factors. First, they have a high computational and
storage cost due to their extensive use of numerical reconstruction. Each evaluated hologram has to be reconstructed at different axial distances, and the resulting reconstruction
volume has to be kept in memory and processed by the focus measures. Unfortunately, we
can not process the computed reconstruction individually and sequentially search for the
optimal focus plane, as many of the current SOTA focus measures produce a noisy focus
curve with multiple peaks and valleys. Second, many hyperparameters are involved in
the hologram volumetric rendering, and each of these hyperparameters can affect the final
results. Finally, the learned-based focus operators can have a large GPU consumption
that can prevent their use in embedding devices or high-resolution holograms.
In addition to those practical limitations, we can also notice that the produced methods have an upper performance bound and cannot achieve optimal performance without
further refinement. The conducted experiments reveal that all proposed methods fail to
accurately retrieve local scene geometry when there is a high surface variation.
For a given test scene, regions with high local variation typically constitute a small
fraction of the overall processed areas and are expected to be relatively evenly distributed
across the spatial domain. The resulting degradation in these regions manifests as speckle
noise in the associated EFI, with noise density that is correlated with the magnitude of
estimation errors. Despite significant degradation in EFI, both human observers and
222
trained networks can readily discern scene contents and extract shape priors that can be
used to refine the degraded areas.
This kind of refinement technique is extensively documented in current literature [151,
152] and is often tailored to enhance RGB-D representations extracted from bad acquisition sensors like the Kinect camera. A key distinction lies in the RGB-D data utilized:
while typical RGB-D data may suffer degradation only in the depth map, our current
work deals with degradation in both RGB and depth maps. However, the degradation
in RGB images is speckle noise contamination, which can be effectively removed using
state-of-the-art denoising models.
The correlation between speckle noise contamination and error magnitude, i.e., the offset between predicted and ground depth, presents an opportunity to devise self-supervised
focus measures. The proposed approach could iteratively search a depth map that produces an EFI with minimal speckle noise contamination. While the concept is intuitive, its
practical implementation required the implementation of gradient backpropagation from
the EFI to the estimated depth map, which is not permitted in the actual implementation.
In addition, an appropriate loss that correctly asses the speckle level should be designed.
These limitations and potential improvements are discussed in scenarios where numerical reconstruction is computed using the entire hologram as input. However, it is common
practice to utilize a windowed approach [153] for high-resolution holograms, wherein only
a portion of the hologram is cropped for numerical reconstruction. Varying the localization of this crop enables the acquisition of different scene views, with different behaviors
of focus change compared to using the entire hologram as input. A comprehensive study
is needed to explore how the proposed methods can be effectively adapted to windowed
numerical reconstruction scenarios.
9.5.2
Phase space-based method
The precision of the phase space method is closely tied to the density and shapes of
scene objects. Indeed, current methods heavily rely on extracting and refining candidate
points through a proposed inclusion score. However, the relevance of this inclusion score
will depend on the density of the background points across the lenslet images of the
hologram PSR.
For example, for a given scene that spans the entire spatial domain: each lenslet lens
image will only contain foreground points, and thus will result in an inclusion score of one
independently of the location of the candidate 3D point. Conversely, a scene with a smaller
223
spatial extent will result in a higher number of backgrounds than foreground points in
the lenslet image and thus produce a better filtration and delineation of individual scene
objects using the inclusion score.
Moreover, the conducted experiments have shown that highly curved objects are usually poorly retrieved in the first estimation phase and require further refinement to recover
their exact geometry.
In scenarios with low object density, improving the precision and efficiency of the
proposed methods can be achieved by simplifying the computation of inclusion scores to
a selected subset of points, determined by predefined criteria. Then a sparse-to-dense
methodology could be adopted to estimate dense scene geometry depth map.
For example, a candidate strategy would be to compute the point inclusion score
only on object boundary points, which tend to be more accurately estimated. Then, a
neural network can be used to predict the inner object points depth using the sparse
estimated points and a learned shape prior from the extracted object boundary. The
sparse point densification has gained increasing attention in contemporary computer vision
literature [154, 155], with diverse neural network architectures proposed for this purpose.
For scenarios with higher object density, it is essential to introduce geometrical connections across the different hologram PSR lenslet images. This relationship can be used
to ray trace matching points across the lenslet image to retrieve scene geometry. This
type of approach is often used in the light field literature, where the matching points are
identified through the extraction of an epipolar line that is used to regress the object
depth. A key distinction between traditional light field matrices and holographic lenslet
matrices lies in the size of the extracted lenslet images, which are too small to be used on
modern CNN architecture.
To address these constraints, the matching point can be extracted by using the scene
motion across various lenslet images that can be computed using an optical flow algorithm.
Then used to extract matching points on consecutive lenslet images.
Introducing a connection between the lenslet images also aids in reducing the number
of processed images. For instance, one can opt to utilize only the central lenslet images
corresponding to the central view, while omitting the remaining images. This selective
approach helps mitigate errors that may arise when a large set of images is used to
constrain depth estimation. By focusing on central lenslet images, which often provide
crucial information for depth estimation, computational resources can be utilized more
efficiently, leading to improved accuracy in the final results.
224
9.5.3
Scene motion estimation
The accuracy of scene motion estimation within holographic video sequences is intricately tied to the fidelity of the retrieved scene geometries. As the quality diminishes, the
estimated motion vectors become noisier data and consequently lead to poorly generated
motion-compensated frames.
One might question whether the motion vectors should be enhanced directly or if
the refinement procedure should prioritize improving the quality of the retrieved scene
geometries that will subsequently improve the precision of the motion vectors.
Both of these approaches could be considered and their success will depend on the
density and the spreading of the degradation across the spatial domain, as well as the the
adopted refinement procedure.
A local refinement strategy can be employed when the degradation has low density
and is uniformly dispersed throughout the spatial domain. In such scenarios, any cropped
patch of the spatial domain will contain a relatively small number of corrupt pixels that
can be easily refined from the uncorrupt pixels. Conversely, a global refinement approach
is preferable when the degradation level is high and is randomly distributed across the
spatial domain. In this case, leveraging a global shape prior can aid in effectively refining
the corrupted pixels. The main role of the refinement is to ensure prediction consistency
along the holographic video sequence that will help the texture-matching step in the
optical flow algorithms.
Another promising approach for addressing scene motion estimation could be designed
by considering the different scene perspective projections on lenslet images of the hologram PSR. This direct approach could prevent the error propagation between the scene
geometry retrieval and motion estimation. In addition, can significantly reduce the computation time and allow for real-time processing.
9.5.4
Large dataset availability
A common limitation of the proposed methods is the availability of large-scale openaccess datasets with varying textures and shapes that could enhance the model generalization ability. This limitation arises from three main factors. First, while numerous 3D
synthetic CAD datasets and diverse 2D rendering libraries exist, there is a lack of Python
library capable of directly computing holograms from those data. Second, storing a largescale holographic dataset demands a high storage requirement that can not be affordable
225
at a reasonable cost at the current time. Third, there is a lack of data augmentation
operations that can generate variation in the 3D scene directly from the hologram. The
current motion compensation technique does not provide a perfect motion-compensated
hologram and usually alters the hologram.

Titre : Estimation de la géométrie et du mouvement de la scène par apprentissage profond
pour la compression de vidéos holographiques.
Mot clés : Imagerie 3D, Holographie, Estimation de profondeur, Flux optique.
Résumé :
L’estimation du mouvement
dans les vidéos holographiques nécessitent
d’extraire et d’analyser les variations de la
géométrie 3D à partir du signal holographique.
Malheureusement, récupérer la scène à partir d’un seul hologramme numérique est un
problème inverse mal posé pour lequel aucune solution exacte n’existe.
En effet,
l’onde lumineuse diffusée par chaque point
de la scène contribue à chaque pixel lors de
l’enregistrement de l’hologramme. Par conséquent, le signal holographique brouille les
informations de la scène 3D, qui ne peuvent
pas être récupérées directement. En particulier, un léger changement dans la scène
se traduit par des motifs holographiques très
différents, faisant de l’estimation de mouvement un sujet de recherche non trivial.
Pour résoudre cette problématique, nous
allons étudier plusieurs approches basées
sur les outils d’analyse espace-fréquence et
d’apprentissage profond pour récupérer la
scène à partir des données holographiques.
Ensuite, les mouvements des objets de la
scène seront estimés à partir de ces données
extraites. Des expérimentations approfondies
seront menées pour évaluer d’une part les
performances des méthodes proposées pour
le recouvrement de la géométrie de la scène
sous diverses conditions, et d’autre part les effets des dégradations commises sur la qualité
des vecteurs de mouvement estimés.
Title: Accurate scene geometry retrieval and motion estimation using deep learning techniques for holographic video compression.
Keywords: 3D Imaging, Holography, Depth Estimation, Optical Flow.
Abstract: Motion estimation in holographic
videos requires extracting and analyzing variations in 3D geometry from the holographic
signal. Unfortunately, recovering the scene
from a single digital hologram is an ill-posed
inverse problem for which no exact solution
exists. Indeed, the light wave diffused by
each point in the scene contributes to every pixel during hologram recording. Consequently, the holographic signal scrambles the
information of the 3D scene, which cannot
be directly recovered. In particular, a slight
change in the scene results in very different
holographic patterns, making motion estima-
tion a non-trivial research topic. To address
this challenge, we will investigate several approaches based on space-frequency analysis
tools and deep learning to recover the scene
from holographic data. Then, object movements in the scene will be estimated based on
these recovered data. Comprehensive experiments will be conducted to evaluate, on one
hand, the performance of the proposed methods for scene geometry retrieval under various
conditions, and on the other hand, the effects
of the degradations incurred on the quality of
the estimated motion vectors.
